[
36
, Theorem 2.3.]
or
[
53
, Theorem III.1]
). The proof is similar to the one given by Karpilovsky in the untwisted case. For completeness sake and convenience of the reader we include a proof.

Theorem 5.6
.

Let
G
be a finite group,
R
the ring of integers in a number field
F
and
[\beta]\in H^{2}(G,R^{*})
. If
x=\sum_{g\in G}a_{g}u_{g}
is a torsion unit in
R^{\beta}[G]
such that
a_{1}\neq 0
, then
x\in{\mathcal{U}}(R)
.

Proof.

The proof is along the same lines as
[
36
, Theorem 2.3.]
. Let
u=\sum_{g\in G}r_{g}u_{g}
be a torsion unit in
R^{\beta}[G]
, say of order
m
. Assume
r_{1}\neq 0
.
Let
n=|G|
and consider the left regular representation
\rho:F^{\beta}[G]\rightarrow M_{n}(F)
. Then
\rho(u)
is of finite order
m
(note that
\rho
is injective) and thus it is diagonalisable over the algebraic closure of
F
in
{\mathbb{C}}
(recall that
F
is a number field).
Its eigenvalues
\zeta_{1},\cdots,\zeta_{n}
are roots of unity, each of order a divisor of
m
. We get that
\zeta_{1}+\cdots+\zeta_{n}=\sum_{g\in G}r_{g}\mbox{tr}(\rho(u_{g}))=nr_{1}
. Thus
|nr_{1}|=|\zeta_{1}+\cdots+\zeta_{n}|\leq n
. Moreover,
|nr_{1}|=n
if and only if
\zeta_{1}=\zeta_{2}=\ldots=\zeta_{n}
.
If this holds then the diagonalisation of
\rho(u)
is a multiple of the identity matrix, hence
\rho(u)
is a central, i.e.
\rho(u)\in F
.
Because
\rho
is injective this yields that
u\in F\cap R^{\beta}[G]=R
, as desired.

So, it remains to show that
|nr_{1}|=n
. Assume, the contrary, i.e. suppose
|nr_{1}|<n
. Let
\epsilon
be a primitive
m
-th root of unity. Note that
r_{1}\in{\mathbb{Q}}(\epsilon)\subseteq{\mathbb{C}}
. Since also
r_{1}\in R
and thus
r_{1}
is an algebraic integer, it follows that
r_{1}
is in the ring of integers of
{\mathbb{Q}}(\epsilon)
.

We claim that, for every
\sigma\in\mbox{Gal}({\mathbb{Q}}(\epsilon)/{\mathbb{Q}})
, we have
|\sigma(nr_{1})|<n
.
Indeed, note that complex conjugation and elements of
\mbox{Gal}({\mathbb{Q}}(\epsilon)/{\mathbb{Q}})
commute and thus if

n=|\sigma(nr_{1})|=\sigma(nr_{1})\overline{\sigma(nr_{1})}=\sigma(nr_{1})\sigma(\overline{nr_{1}})

then also
n=\sigma^{-1}(\sigma(nr_{1})\sigma(\overline{nr_{1}}))=|nr_{1}|<1
. This proves the claim.

Next, the assumption says that
|r_{1}|<1
and from the claim we get that
|\sigma(r_{1})|<1
for all

\sigma\in\mbox{Gal}({\mathbb{Q}}(\epsilon)/{\mathbb{Q}})
.
It follows that the norm
0\neq|N_{\mbox{Gal}({\mathbb{Q}}((\epsilon)/{\mathbb{Q}})}(r_{1})|=|\prod_{\sigma\in\mbox{Gal}({\mathbb{Q}}(\epsilon)/{\mathbb{Q}})}\sigma(r_{1})|=\prod_{\sigma\in\mbox{Gal}({\mathbb{Q}}(\epsilon)/{\mathbb{Q}})}|\sigma(r_{1})|<1
. However, this yields a contradiction as
N_{\mbox{Gal}({\mathbb{Q}}(\epsilon)/{\mathbb{Q}})}(r_{1})
is an algebraic integer (see for example Proposition 4.1.8 in
[
26
]
) and thus belongs to
{\mathbb{Z}}
; so it can not be strictly between
0
and
1
.
∎

It would be interesting to have a proof of
Theorem

5.6
which makes a reduction to the well-known Berman-Higman theorem for group rings. So, this is for example an instance where understanding
4.3
would help.
We now proceed to the proof of the main theorem

Proof of
Theorem

5.5
.

The first part follows from a classical trick in untwisted group rings.
Namely, first note that
\{\chi(a)^{-1}a\mid a\in N\}
is indeed contained in
\ker(\widetilde{\Psi}_{\chi,\beta})
. Conversely, take a torsion unit
u\in\ker(\widetilde{\Psi}_{\chi,\beta})
. Because of
Proposition

3.3
,
\ker(\widetilde{\Psi}_{\chi,\beta})=\mathcal{U}(1+R^{\beta}[\Gamma]I_{\chi})
and thus
u=1+\sum_{g\in G}y_{g}u_{\mu(g)}
, with
y_{g}\in I_{\chi}=\operatorname{span}_{R}\{u_{a}-\chi(a)u_{1}\mid a\in N\}.
In particular, if there would be no
1\neq a\in N
so that
u_{a}
is in
\operatorname{supp}(u)
,
then the coefficient of
u_{1}
in the expression of
u\in R^{\beta}[\Gamma]
is equal to
1
and hence, by
Theorem

5.6
,
u\in{\mathcal{U}}(R)
. As
u\in\ker(\widetilde{\Psi}_{\chi,\beta})
this implies
u=1
.

Now assume there exists a non-trivial
a\in N\cap\operatorname{supp}(u)
and consider the element
z:=u.u_{a^{-1}}\chi(a)
. Note that, due to the centrality of
N
, the element
z
again is a torsion unit in
\ker(\widetilde{\Psi}_{\chi,\beta})
. However now
1\in\operatorname{supp}(z)
and therefore, by applying
Theorem

5.6
,
z=1
. In other words,
u=\chi(a)^{\textemdash 1}a
as needed.

For the second part, we will
assume
that we are not in the trivial case that
{\mathcal{U}}(R^{\beta}[\Gamma])
is finite.
Then, by
Theorem

3.2
, we see that
\ker(\widetilde{\Psi}_{\chi})
is finite if and only if
{\mathcal{U}}(R(\chi\prime)^{\beta.T(\chi\prime)}[G])

is finite for all
\chi\neq\chi\prime\in\operatorname{Lin}(N,F)
but
{\mathcal{U}}(R(\chi)^{\beta.T(\chi)}[G])
is infinite. The rest of the proof will reinterpret all those conditions simultaneously using
Theorem

4.1
. To start,
{\mathcal{U}}(R(\chi\prime))
must also be finite for all
\chi\neq\chi\prime
. In particular
{\mathcal{U}}(R)
needs to be finite and hence
F={\mathbb{Q}}
or
{\mathbb{Q}}(\sqrt{-d})
with
d>0
. The exact restrictions are recorded by the following claim.

Claim
: If
\mathcal{U}(RN)
is finite then: (i)
\exp(N)=2
or (ii)
\exp(N)=4;F={\mathbb{Q}},{\mathbb{Q}}(i)
or (iii)
\exp(N)=3,6;\;F={\mathbb{Q}},{\mathbb{Q}}(\sqrt{-3})
.

If
\mathcal{U}(RN)
is infinite then : (iv)
N\cong C_{p}
for a prime
p\geq 5
or (v)
N\cong C_{8},C_{9};\;F={\mathbb{Q}}
.

Decompose
FN\subseteq\mathcal{Z}(F^{\beta}[\Gamma])
as in (
12
),
i.e. using the theorem of Perlis and Walker:

FN=\bigoplus_{d\mid|N|}F(\zeta_{d})^{\oplus a_{d}}
, then there has to be at most one

\zeta_{d}
for which the the unit group of the ring of integers in
F(\zeta_{d})
,
denoted
R_{d}
, is infinite. Moreover, for this value we need
a_{d}=1
.
Note that this property is inhered by subgroups of
N
.
Hence, if
N=N_{1}\times N_{2}
, a direct product of two non-trivial subgroups, then, as
FN=FN_{1}\otimes_{F}FN_{2}
, we get that all simple epimorphic images of both group algebras
FN_{1}
and
FN_{2}
must be such that the unit group of their respective ring of integers is finite; because otherwise we would have at least two distinct
d
and
d^{\prime}
so that
F(\zeta_{d})
and
F(\zeta_{d^{\prime}})
are simple epimorphic images of
FN
and the unit groups
{\mathcal{U}}(R_{d})
and
{\mathcal{U}}(R_{d^{\prime}})
are infinite.
Hence, if
N
is decomposable as a direct product then
{\mathcal{U}}(RN)
is finite and thus, by
Theorem

4.1
, the exponent of
N
is a divisor of
4
or
6
. The restrictions on
F
follow in the same way as at the end of the proof of
Theorem

4.1
, i.e. by determining when
{\mathbb{Q}}(\sqrt{-d},\zeta_{\exp(N)})
is at most of degree
2
over
{\mathbb{Q}}
.

On the other hand, if
N
is indecomposable and its exponent is not a divisor of
4
or
6
, then
N
is cyclic and
N=p^{n}
for some prime
p
. If
n>1
then
FN
has
F(\zeta_{p})
as a summand and
R_{p}
is finite only if
p=2,3
and
F={\mathbb{Q}},{\mathbb{Q}}(\sqrt{-d})
with
d>0
. Thus either
n=1
or
p=2,3
. In the latter case, as
R_{p^{n}}
is finite only if
p^{n}=2,3,4
we obtain the claim about the form of
N
. The according restrictions on
F
can be proven with a similar argument as when
\exp(N)\mid 4,6
.

Claim:
If
N
is cyclic, then
\chi
is the unique faithful character. Moreover if
N=C_{p}
, then
\Gamma
is a split extension.

If
N
is cyclic, then there is a bijection between the irreducible characters and the divisors
d
of
|N|
, say
\chi_{d}
corresponds. Since we always assume that
{\mathcal{U}}(R^{\beta}[\Gamma])
is infinite we know that there is a unique component of
\bigoplus_{d\mid|N|}R[\chi_{d}]^{\beta.T(\chi_{d})}[G]^{\oplus a_{d}}
with infinite unit group.
Theorem

4.1
implies that if
{\mathcal{U}}(R[\chi_{d}]^{\beta.T(\chi_{d})}[G]^{\oplus a_{d}})
is infinite, then also for a multiple of
d
. Hence indeed the only infinite component is
d=n
, i.e. the unique faithful character. If
|N|
is odd, then by the previous claim
N=C_{9}
or
C_{p}
with
p\geq 5
prime. In both cases
\mathcal{U}(R^{\rm Res(\beta)}[G]
is finite and hence
\exp(G_{\beta})\mid 4,6.
Therefore
\alpha(g,h)
needs to devide both
lcm(o(g),o(h))
and
p\geq 5
which is only possible if
\alpha(g,h)=1
for all
g,h\in G
. Hence the extension is split if
N=C_{p}
.

We are now able to finish to obtain the desired restrictions in the case that
\mathcal{U}({\mathbb{Z}}N)
is infinite, i.e.
N=C_{p},C_{9},C_{8}
by the first claim. For
C_{p}
the desired statement follows by the above claims and the fact that there are only two components in this case (hence the faithful one is the one different from the trivial representation, i.e.
\omega_{N}
). For
C_{8}
and
C_{9}
it remains to prove the desired value on
\exp(G_{\beta})
. For this note that
\chi=\omega_{N}
yields
\exp(G_{\beta})\mid 4,6
by
Theorem

4.1
. If
\exp(G)=4
, then
{\mathbb{Q}}(\zeta_{3})^{\gamma}[G]
is infinite for any
2
-cocycle by
Theorem

4.1
. Similarly for
{\mathbb{Q}}(i)^{\gamma}[G]
when
3\mid\exp(G_{\beta})
. This finishes the
\mathcal{U}({\mathbb{Z}}N)
infinite case.

Finally suppose that
\mathcal{U}({\mathbb{Z}}N)
is finite. In this case
\exp(N)\mid 4,6
and
F
is of the form as in the first claim. Also
\exp(G)\mid\exp(G_{\beta T(\chi)})\mid 4,6
for various characters
\chi
of
N
. If
N
is cyclic, then the only restriction is that
{\mathcal{U}}(R^{\beta}[G])
is finite. So suppose that
N
is not cyclic. If
\exp(N)=4
and
3\mid|G|
, then there are several components having an element of order
12
which is not possible. Hence in that case
\exp(|G|)|4
. Similarly if
3\mid\exp(N)
and
4\mid|G|
. Thus we indeed obtain that
\operatorname{lcm}(\exp(G),\exp(N))\mid 4,6
if
N
is non-cyclic.
∎

For applications it would be useful to also understand the torsion units in
\ker(\widetilde{\Psi}_{\chi,\beta})
in case that the (
17
) is abelian (i.e.
N
is abelian but not necessarily central). The first part of
Theorem

5.5
could be read as saying that a torsion unit in
\ker(\widetilde{\Psi}_{\chi,\beta})
must be in
{\mathbb{Z}}[N]
. Indeed because
N
is abelian one can then use
Theorem

5.6
to conclude that the torsion units are trivial and hence of the form
\chi(a)^{-1}a
. So we expect the following to be true.

Conjecture 5.7
.

If the extension (
17
) is abelian, then
\{\text{torsion units in }\ker(\widetilde{\Psi}_{\chi,\beta})\}\subseteq{\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}[N])
. In particular all torsion units in the kernel are trivial.

To finish this section, we illustrate the concepts of
Section

3
and
Section

5
on an example.

Example 5.8
.

Consider

\Gamma=C_{4}\rtimes C_{4}:=\langle a,b\mid a^{4}=b^{4}=1,\,a^{b}=a^{-1}\rangle
which can be viewed in the following way as a central extension

(19)

1\rightarrow\langle y_{1}:y_{1}^{2}=1\rangle\times\langle y_{2}:y_{2}^{2}=1\rangle\longrightarrow\Gamma\overset{\lambda}{\longrightarrow}\langle x_{1}:x_{1}^{2}=1\rangle\times\langle x_{2}:x_{2}^{2}=1\rangle\rightarrow 1

where in fact
y_{1}=a^{2}
and
y_{2}=b^{2}
. The epimorphism
\lambda:\Gamma\rightarrow C_{2}\times C_{2}
is determined by
\lambda(a)=x_{1}
and
\lambda(b)=x_{2}
. Furthermore, we choose as a section
\mu:\langle x_{1},x_{2}\rangle\rightarrow\Gamma
defined by

\mu(1)=1,\mu(x_{1})=a,\mu(x_{2})=b,\mu(x_{1}x_{2})=ab

and consider the induced normalized cocycle
\gamma\in Z^{2}(\langle x_{1},x_{2}\rangle,\langle y_{1},y_{2}\rangle)
(i.e.
\gamma(g,h)=\mu(g)\mu(h)\mu(gh)^{-1}
).
Explicitly:

\gamma(x_{1},x_{1})=y_{1},\gamma(x_{1},x_{2})=1,\gamma(x_{1},x_{1}x_{2})=y_{1}

\gamma(x_{2},x_{1})=y_{1},\gamma(x_{2},x_{2})=y_{2},\gamma(x_{2},x_{1}x_{2})=y_{1}y_{2}

\gamma(x_{1}x_{2},x_{1})=1,\gamma(x_{1}x_{2},x_{2})=y_{2},\gamma(x_{1}x_{2},x_{1}x_{2})=y_{2}.\vskip 5.69046pt

The corresponding class
[\gamma]\in H^{2}(\Gamma,C_{2}\times C_{2})
is determined by

[x_{1},x_{2}]=y_{1},x_{1}^{2}=y_{1}\text{ and }x_{2}^{2}=y_{2}.

Now consider the irreducible characters of
C_{2}\times C_{2}=\langle y_{1},y_{2}\rangle
, say
\operatorname{Irr}(\langle y_{1},y_{2}\rangle)=\{1,\chi_{1},\chi_{2},\chi_{3}\}
with
\chi_{1}(y_{i})=-1,\,\chi_{2}(y_{1})=-1=-\chi_{2}(y_{2})
and
\chi_{3}(y_{1})=1=-\chi_{3}(y_{2})
.
Then using
Theorem

3.2
we obtain that

{\mathbb{Q}}\Gamma\cong{\mathbb{Q}}[C_{2}\times C_{2}]\oplus\bigoplus_{i=1}^{3}{\mathbb{Q}}^{T_{\gamma}(\chi_{i})}[C_{2}\times C_{2}].

It is easily seen that

{\mathbb{Q}}^{T_{\gamma}(\chi_{1})}[C_{2}\times C_{2}]\cong\left(\frac{-1,-1}{{\mathbb{Q}}}\right),\,{\mathbb{Q}}^{T_{\gamma}(\chi_{2})}[C_{2}\times C_{2}]\cong\operatorname{M}_{2}({\mathbb{Q}})\text{ and }{\mathbb{Q}}^{T_{\gamma}(\chi_{3})}[C_{2}\times C_{2}]\cong 2{\mathbb{Q}}(i).

Therefore

\widetilde{\Psi}_{\chi_{2}}:{\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}\Gamma)\rightarrow{\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}^{T_{\gamma}(\chi_{2})}[C_{2}\times C_{2}])

is the only projection with infinite codomain. Moreover, by
Theorem

5.5
the kernel is finite and is given by

\ker(\widetilde{\Psi}_{\chi_{2}})=\langle-a^{2},b^{2}\rangle\cong C_{2}\times C_{2}.

Also
\operatorname{coker}(\widetilde{\Psi}_{\chi_{2}})
is finite by
Theorem

5.3
and a precise description will follow from the methods in the upcoming sections.

6.
Deforming bicyclic units via twisting and their contribution

In this section we will introduce a generic construction of units in twisted group rings (of finite groups) which generalizes the bicyclic units. They differ by a factor determined by the twisting. We will show in
Section

6.2
that these units play a role analogous to the one of elementary matrices inside the special linear group, building on a generalisation of the Jespers-Leal theorem obtained in
Theorem

6.3
. Thereafter we return to the context of the previous section and investigate their contribution to
\operatorname{coker}(\Psi_{\chi})
.

6.1.
Generalized byclic units versus elementary matrices

Before specifying to twisted group rings, we wish to write down a general ‘
S
-order version’ of an important ‘
{\mathbb{Z}}
-order result’ of Jespers and Leal
[
29
]
on bicylic units which was only informally known to some experts. For this let
B
be a finite dimensional semisimple
{\mathbb{Q}}
-algebra,
S
a finite set of places of
\mathbb{Q}
such that
\{\infty\}\subseteq S
and
{\mathbb{Z}}_{S}
the ring of
S
-integers in
{\mathbb{Q}}
. Further let
A
be a finite dimensional semisimple
B
-algebra and
R
an
{\mathbb{Z}}_{S}
-order in
B
.

Example 6.1
.

Let
S=\{\infty,(p_{1}),\ldots,(p_{l})\}
with
p_{i}
a prime number. Then
{\mathbb{Z}}[\frac{1}{p_{1}},\ldots,\frac{1}{p_{l}}]
are the
S
-integers in
{\mathbb{Q}}
. If we now consider some root of unity
\zeta
, then
R={\mathbb{Z}}_{S}[\zeta]={\mathbb{Z}}[\frac{1}{p_{1}},\ldots,\frac{1}{p_{l}},\zeta]
is a possible
{\mathbb{Z}}_{S}
-order in
B={\mathbb{Q}}(\zeta)
. For this choice a first main example is
A={\mathbb{Q}}(\zeta)^{\alpha}[G]
for some
[\alpha]\in H^{2}(G,{\mathbb{Q}}(\zeta)^{*})
and a finite group
G
. Another frequent example is
A=M_{n}(D)
with
D
a finite dimensional division
{\mathbb{Q}}
-algebra.

Let
\mathcal{A}=\{g_{1},\ldots,g_{n}\}
be a
B
-basis of
A
and
\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{q}\}
a
{\mathbb{Z}}_{S}
-basis of
R
. For every idempotent
f\in A
, there exists a minimal integer
n_{f}\in{\mathbb{Z}}
such that
n_{f}f\in\operatorname{span}_{R}(\mathcal{A})
. Associated to these, one can now consider the elements

b(m_{i}x_{i}g,f)=1+n_{f}^{2}(1-f)m_{i}x_{i}gf\text{ and }b(f,m_{i}x_{i}g)=1+n_{f}^{2}fm_{i}x_{i}g(1-f).

with
m_{i}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{S}
and
g\in\mathcal{A}
. As usual, these are units because
(1-f)m_{i}x_{i}gf
has square zero.

Definition 6.2
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be a set of idempotents in
A
. Then the elements in

\{b(m_{i}x_{i}g,f),b(f,m_{i}x_{i}g)\mid f\in\mathcal{F},1\leq i\leq q,g\in\mathcal{A},m_{i}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{S}\}

are called the
generalized bicyclic units
corresponding to
\mathcal{F}
. The group generated by these is denoted by
\text{GBic}^{\mathcal{F}}(A,R)
.

Classically only the generators with
m_{i}=1
are called generalized bicyclic units. The values
m_{i}
are added in order to still obtain all elements of
R\mathcal{A}
in the middle between
(1-f)
and
f
(when taking the group generated by). Note that
R\mathcal{A}
is an
{\mathbb{Z}}_{S}
-order in
A
.

We can now formulate the general version of
[
29
]
saying that generalized bicyclic units should be considered as the analogue of elementary matrices. The proof of
[
29
]
shows that Jespers and Leal handled the case
S=\{\infty\}
, i.e. of
{\mathbb{Z}}
-orders, and with
A
an (untwisted) group ring .

Theorem 6.3
.

Let
e\in\operatorname{PCI}(A)
such that
Ae\cong\operatorname{M}_{n}(D)
with
n\geq 2
. Let
\mathcal{O}
be a
{\mathbb{Z}}_{S}
-order in
D
. If
f
is an idempotent in
A
such that
fe\notin\mathcal{Z}(Ae)
, then there exists a non-zero
y\in{\mathbb{Z}}
such that

1-e+E_{n}(y\mathcal{O})\subseteq\text{GBic}^{\{f\}}(A,R)

Recall that, a set of matrix units in a simple algebra
Ae
is a set of elements
\{E_{i,j}:1\leq j\leq n\}
in
\operatorname{SL}_{1}(Ae)
, where
n
is the reduced degree of
Ae
, such that
\sum_{i=1}^{n}E_{i,i}=1
and
E_{i,j}E_{p,q}=\delta_{j,p}E_{i,q}
. Concretely, fixing the isomorphism
Ae\cong\operatorname{M}_{n}(D)
the element
E_{i,j}
can be identified with the matrix having
1
in the
(i,j)
entry and zero elsewhere. For an ideal
J
in
\mathcal{O}
we denote by
E_{n}(J)
the subgroup of
SL_{n}(\mathcal{O})
generated by the elements
e+rE_{i,j}
for
i\neq j
and
r\in J
.

Proof of
Theorem

6.3
.

Multiplying generalized bicyclic units one sees that

\{1+n_{f}^{2}f\alpha(1-f),1+n_{f}^{2}(1-f)\alpha f\mid\alpha\in R\mathcal{A}\}\subseteq\text{GBic}^{\{f\}}(A,R).

Following
[
26
, Lemma 11.2.4]
) one can decompose
fe=E_{1,1}+\cdots+E_{l,l}
for some
0<l<n
and
\{E_{i,j}\mid 1\leq i,j\leq n\}
a set of matrix units. In case
1\leq i\leq l
and
l+1\leq j\leq n
one readily sees that by using
E_{i,j}E_{p,q}=\delta_{j,p}E_{i,q}
the products can be rewritten as following:

f\mathcal{O}E_{i,j}(1-f)e=\mathcal{O}E_{i,j}\text{ and }(1-f)\mathcal{O}E_{j,i}fe=\mathcal{O}E_{j,i}

for all
1\leq i\leq l
and
l+1\leq j\leq n
. Since
\mathcal{O}
is an
S
-order it has a finite
{\mathbb{Z}}_{S}
-basis, which we denote
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{O}}=\{b_{1},\ldots,b_{k}\}
. Now, for any
i,j
,
[
26
, Lemma 4.6.9]
yields a smallest number
n_{ij}
such that
(1+bE_{i,j})^{n_{ij}}=1+n_{ij}bE_{i,j}\in R\mathcal{A}e
for all
b\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{O}}
. Consequently, if we also consider the smallest value
n_{e}\in{\mathbb{Z}}
such that
n_{e}e\in R\mathcal{A},
then

1+n_{f}^{2}n_{e}n_{ij}\mathcal{O}E_{i,j}\subset\text{GBic}^{\{f\}}(A,R)

for all
1\leq i\leq l
and
l+1\leq j\leq n
. Similarly, for these
i
and
j
one has that
1+n_{f}^{2}n_{e}n_{ji}\mathcal{O}E_{j,i}
is a subet of
\text{GBic}^{\{f\}}(A,R)
. The other indices can now be reached by taking the appropriate commutators. For example if
1\leq i,j\leq l,i\neq j,x\in{\mathbb{Z}}
and
\alpha\in\mathcal{O}
then

1+x^{2}\alpha E_{i,j}=\left(1+x\alpha E_{i,l+1}\,,\,1+xE_{l+1,j}\right).

and using the
E_{j,i}
one also reaches the
l+1\leq i,j\leq n
. Appropriate choices of
x
now yields the desired result.
∎

Recall that the literature on the subgroup congruence problem yields that the difference between
E_{n}(y\mathcal{O})
and the congruence subgroup of level
y
of
\operatorname{SL}_{n}(\mathcal{O})
increases with
y
. Moreover if
n=2
and
D
is a division algebra containing an order with finitely many units
27
27

27

A division algebra contains an order with finite unit group if and only if
D={\mathbb{Q}}(\sqrt{-d})
or
\left(\frac{u,v}{{\mathbb{Q}}}\right)
with
d\geq 0
and
u,b<0
, see
[
2
, Theorem 2.10.]
.
, then
[\operatorname{SL}_{n}(\mathcal{O}),E_{n}(y\mathcal{O})]=\infty
starting already from rather small values of
y
. Therefore, for applications an answer to the following would be incredibly valuable.

Question 6.4
.

Suppose
n=2
and
D
is a division algebra containing an order with finitely many units. What is

(1)

a tight upper bound for the scalar
y
of
Theorem

6.3
in terms of the starting data?

(2)

a tight upper bound for the scalar
y
in terms of the proportions
o(g)/o(ge)
for
g\in G
?

Note that the proof above shows that one obtains the following value for
y\in{\mathbb{Z}}
:

(20)

y=\left(n_{e}\,n_{f}^{2}\,\text{gcm}\{n_{ij},n_{ji}:1\leq i\leq l,l+1\leq j\leq n\}\right)^{2}.

Moreover if
n=2
, then
x=\sqrt{y}
suffices as a multiple. A limitation of the value in (
20
) is that the decomposition
fe=E_{1,1}+\cdots+E_{l,l}
and the numbers
n_{ij}
are not explicit from the starting data in
Theorem

6.3
. A value for
n_{ij}
can be deduced by following the steps in the proofs of
[
26
, Lemma 4.6.6 & 4.6.9]
, however the value would require to know too much of the isomorphism type of
G
and hence to weak for practical use. Nevertheless, according to
[
26
, Lemma 4.6.9]
there exists a
0\neq r\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{S}
such that
\operatorname{M}_{n}(r\mathcal{O})\subseteq R\mathcal{A}e
and that its additive index yields a ‘uniform looking’ upper-bound:

(21)

y=\left(n_{e}\,n_{f}^{2}\,[R\mathcal{A}e:\operatorname{M}_{n}(r\mathcal{O})]\right)^{2}.

Unfortunately in practice the additive index above is hard to compute.

6.2.
Deforming bicyclic units - case of twisted group rings

From now on we consider the setting where
S=\{\infty\}
, i.e.
{\mathbb{Z}}
-orders, and
A={\mathbb{Q}}(\zeta_{n})^{\alpha}[G]
where
\zeta_{n}
is some primitive
n
-th root and
[\alpha]\in H^{2}(G,{\mathbb{Z}}[\zeta_{n}]^{*})\subseteq H^{2}(G,{\mathbb{Q}}(\zeta_{n})^{*})
arbitrary. The importance of this case for the study of
{\mathbb{Q}}[\Gamma]
for
\Gamma
some central extension is highlighted by
Theorem

3.2
and
Proposition

3.3
.

Idempotents from trivial units

Let
\alpha\in Z^{2}(G,{\mathbb{Z}}[\zeta_{n}]^{*})
be a
2
-cocycle of finite order with
o(\alpha)\mid n
. Then, for
0\leq j<n
we can partition
G
into the following sets,

(22)

\mathcal{G}_{j}^{\alpha}=\{g\in G\mid u_{g}^{o(g)}=\zeta_{n}^{j}\}.

The observation is now that if
g\in\mathcal{G}_{0}^{\alpha}=\{g\in G\mid o(u_{g})=o(g)\}
, then
\widehat{u_{g}}=\frac{1}{o(g)}\widetilde{u_{g}}
with

(23)

\widetilde{u_{g}}:=u_{1}+u_{g}+\ldots+u_{g}^{o(u_{g})-1}

is again a non-trivial idempotent in
{\mathbb{Q}}(\zeta_{n})^{\alpha}[G]
if
g\neq 1
. Consequently,
\widetilde{u_{g}}(o(g)-\widetilde{u_{g}})=0
. If
g\in\mathcal{G}_{j}^{\alpha}
for a non-zero
j
, then
28
28

28

Going in the sum till the usual upper bound
o(g)-1
will not yield an idempotent.

\widetilde{u_{g}}=0
. In that case, one could instead take the hat
\widehat{(\cdot)}
of
u_{g}^{n/gcd(j,n)}
which would be an idempotent but in practice this construction will not be useful. Hence:

Question 6.5
.

Is there a generic way to produce a non-trivial idempotent from
g\in\mathcal{G}_{j}^{\alpha}
for a non-zero
j
?

Remark 6.6
.

It is well known (see e.g.
[
35
, Theorem 2.3.1]
) that for a cyclic group
C_{n}=\langle g\rangle
the second cohomology group over a commutative ring
R
is isomorphic to
R^{*}/(R^{*})^{n}
. Due to this, the value of
u_{g}^{o(g)}
is not uniquely determined by the cohomology class
[\alpha]
. For example, the class
[\alpha]\in H^{2}(C_{n},{\mathbb{C}}^{*})
determined by the value
\lambda=u_{g}^{o(g)}
is trivial for any
0\neq\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}
. Therefore the sets
\mathcal{G}_{j}^{\alpha}
are unfortunately dependant of the chosen cocycle. However, one could define in an independent way the sets
\mathcal{G}_{0}
and
\mathcal{G}_{\neq 0}
where the latter would be all
g\in G
such that
u_{g}^{o(g)}
is not in the class of
1
in
R^{*}/(R^{*})^{n}
. In particular for
R={\mathbb{Z}}
the situation simplifies and the value
u_{g}^{o(g)}\in\{\pm 1\}
is uniquely determined by the class
[\alpha]
.

Despite the absence of an answer to
6.5
, one can prove that under natural conditions the set
\mathcal{G}_{0}^{\alpha}
yields enough idempotents to apply
Theorem

6.3
. More precisely that for
\mathcal{F}=\{\widehat{u_{g}}=\frac{1}{o(g)}\widetilde{u_{g}}\mid g\in\mathcal{G}_{0}^{\alpha}\}
the group
\text{GBic}^{\mathcal{F}}({\mathbb{Q}}(\zeta_{n})^{\alpha}[G],{\mathbb{Z}}[\zeta_{n}])
contains sufficiently many elementary matrices.

Corollary 6.7
.

Let
G
be a finite group and
\alpha\in Z^{2}(G,{\mathbb{Z}}[\zeta_{n}]^{*})
of finite order such that
G_{\alpha}
has no fixed-point free non-abelian images
29
29

29

A finite group is called fixed point free if it has an irreducible complex representation
\rho
such that
1
is not an eigenvalue of
\rho(g)
for all
1\neq g\in G
. Such groups are exactly the Frobenius complements
[
26
, Proposition 11.4.6.]
and hence those that are subgroups of
D^{*}
for some finite dimensional division algebra
D
.
. Then for any
e\in\operatorname{PCI}({\mathbb{Q}}(\zeta_{n})^{\alpha}[G])
such that
{\mathbb{Q}}(\zeta_{n})^{\alpha}[G]e\cong M_{m}(D)
with
m\geq 2
we have that

1-e+E_{m}(y\mathcal{O})\subseteq\text{GBic}^{\mathcal{F}}({\mathbb{Q}}(\zeta_{n})^{\alpha}[G],{\mathbb{Z}}[\zeta_{n}])

with
\mathcal{O}
an order in
D
and some
y\in{\mathbb{Z}}
. In particular, if
{\mathbb{Q}}(\zeta_{n})^{\alpha}[G]
has no exceptional components then
\text{GBic}^{\mathcal{F}}({\mathbb{Q}}(\zeta_{n})^{\alpha}[G],{\mathbb{Z}}[\zeta_{n}])
is of finite index in
\operatorname{SL}_{1}({\mathbb{Z}}[\zeta_{n}]^{\alpha}[G])
.

Recall that for a subring
R
of a semisimple algebra
A=\prod_{i=1}^{q}\operatorname{M}_{n_{i}}(D_{i})
,
h_{i}
the projection on the
i
-th component and
E_{i}
a splitting field of
K_{i}:=\mathcal{Z}(D_{i})
one defines

(24)

nr(r)=(\text{Rnr}_{\operatorname{M}_{n_{1}}(D_{1})/K_{1}}(h_{1}(r)),\ldots,\text{Rnr}_{\operatorname{M}_{n_{q}}(D_{q})/K_{q}}(h_{q}(r)))

with
\text{Rnr}_{\operatorname{M}_{n_{i}}(D_{i})/K_{i}}(h_{i}(r)):=\det(1_{E_{i}}\otimes_{K_{i}}h_{i}(r))
the
reduced norm over
K_{i}
and

(25)

\operatorname{SL}_{1}(R)=\ker(nr)=\{r\in{\mathcal{U}}(R)\mid\forall i\,:\,\text{Rnr}_{\operatorname{M}_{n_{i}}(D_{i})/K_{i}}(h_{i}(r))=1\}

the (multiplicative) group of reduced norm
1
elements.
Also recall that a simple quotient of
{\mathbb{Q}}(\zeta_{n})^{\alpha}[G]
is called an
exceptional component
if it is either (I) a non-commutative division algebra different from a totally definite quaternion algebra or (II) of the form
\operatorname{M}_{2}({\mathbb{Q}}(\sqrt{-d}))
or
\operatorname{M}_{2}(\left(\frac{-a,-b}{{\mathbb{Q}}}\right))
with
a,b>0
and
d\in{\mathbb{N}}
. The division algebras appearing in these matrix algebras are exactly those having an order with finite unit group
[
2
, Theorem 2.10.]
.

With the conditions assumed in
Corollary

6.7
, the proof of
[
26
, Theorem 11.3.2]
also works in this setting and therefore we will omit the details. In short, that
G_{\alpha}
has no fixed point free images guarantees that for every
e\in\operatorname{PCI}({\mathbb{Q}}(\zeta_{n})^{\alpha}[G])
there exists
g\in\mathcal{G}_{0}^{\alpha}
so that
\widehat{u_{g}}e
is a non-central idempotent. If
e_{C}\in\operatorname{PCI}({\mathbb{C}}^{\alpha}[G]e)
is such that
({\mathbb{C}}^{\alpha}[G]e)e_{C}
is non-commutative, then the element
g
is chosen among those for which
\rho(g)
has eigenvalue one, where
\rho:{\mathbb{C}}\otimes_{{\mathbb{Q}}(\zeta_{n})}{\mathbb{Q}}(\zeta_{n})[G_{\alpha}]\twoheadrightarrow({\mathbb{C}}\otimes_{{\mathbb{Q}}(\zeta_{n})}{\mathbb{Q}}(\zeta_{n})^{\alpha}[G]e)e_{C}
is the complex representation factorising through the projection of
{\mathbb{Q}}(\zeta_{n})[G_{\alpha}]
on
{\mathbb{Q}}(\zeta_{n})^{\alpha}[G]
in
Corollary

3.5
. The last part follows from the known results on the subgroup congruence problem, e.g. see
[
26
, Theorem 11.2.3.]
.

Convention:
For an integral twisted group ring
\mathbb{Z}^{\alpha}G
, for any element of
g\in G
of odd order, the restriction of
[\alpha]
to
\langle g\rangle
is trivial. Therefore we
may and will always from now on choose a normalized representative
\alpha
such that
u_{g}^{o(g)}=1
in
\mathbb{Z}^{\alpha}G
for all
g
of odd order.

Deforming a bicyclic unit : construction

We will use the
G
-module structure of the twisted group ring
R^{\alpha}[G]
described in
[
15
, §3.2]

g(u_{x})=u_{g}u_{x}u_{g}^{-1}=\alpha(g,x)\alpha(gxg^{-1},g)^{-1}u_{gxg^{-1}}.

For every
x\in G
, the restriction of the conjugation representation from
G
to the
centralizer
C_{G}(x)
admits a
1
-dimensional invariant subspace spanned by
u_{x}
. By
the above this ordinary
1
-dimensional representation
\chi_{x}:C_{G}(x)\rightarrow R^{*}
is given by

\chi_{x}(g)=[u_{g},u_{x}]=\alpha(g,x)\alpha(x,g)^{-1}.

For
R=\mathbb{Z}
, for any
x\in G
we can partition
C_{G}(x)
into two disjoint sets

C_{x}^{+}=\{g\in C_{G}(x)\mid\chi_{x}(g)=1\},\quad C_{x}^{-}=\{g\in C_{G}(x)\mid\chi_{x}(g)=-1\}.

We remark that if
C_{x}^{-}=\emptyset
then
x
(and its conjugacy class) is
called
\alpha
-regular
and the set of all
\alpha
-regular conjugacy classes forms a basis for the center of
\mathbb{Z}^{\alpha}G
(see

[
49
]
)
An extreme case for that is when
\alpha
is trivial. In this case
C_{x}^{-}=\emptyset
for any
x\in G
.

Example 6.8
.

For
g,h\in G
and
\alpha\in Z^{2}(G,R^{*})
,
a straightforward calculation yields that in
R^{\alpha}G
if
[u_{g},u_{h}]=\zeta\in R^{*}
is a root of unity of order
k
, then
k
is a divisor of the greatest common divisor of the orders of
g
and
h
in
G
. Hence, for
\alpha\in Z^{2}(G,\mathbb{Z}^{*})
, all elements of
G
of odd order are
\alpha
-regular.

Using this we now introduce a new type of units.

Definition 6.9
.

With the above notation, the elements of the set

\{u_{1}+y\widetilde{u_{g}};\,u_{1}+\widetilde{u_{g}}y\mid g\in\mathcal{G}_{0}^{\alpha},y\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{\alpha}[G],\,\operatorname{supp}(y)\subseteq C_{g}^{-}\}

are called
H-units.
The subgroup of
{\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}^{\alpha}[G])
generated by these is denoted
\mathcal{H}_{\alpha}(G)
.

At first it might seem surprising that the above elements are invertible in

{\mathbb{Z}}^{\alpha}[G]
. This is because the square of
y\widetilde{u_{g}}
and
\widetilde{u_{g}}y
are zero. Hence they are unipotent units with inverse
u_{1}-y\widetilde{u_{g}}
, resp.
u_{1}-\widetilde{u_{g}}y
. One way to see that those terms are indeed square zero, is by noticing that
y\widetilde{u_{g}}=(o(g)-\widetilde{u_{g}})\frac{y}{o(g)}\widetilde{u_{g}}
. This follows from the fact that
u_{g}^{j}u_{h}\widetilde{u_{g}}=(-1)^{j}u_{h}\widetilde{u_{g}}
for

h\in C_{g}^{-}
and hence when
o(g)
is even
30
30

30

If
o(g)
is odd, the last sum in (
26
) equals
o(g)-1
and hence in general the

H
-unit will not be an integral combination. However, as remarked in
Section

6.2
, elements of odd order are
\alpha
-regular
(i.e.
C_{g}^{-}=\emptyset
) and thus one can only take
y=0
. In particular we don’t need to specify the order of the elements
from which we built the
H
-units in order to be a subset of
{\mathbb{Z}}^{\alpha}[G]
.
and
g\in\mathcal{G}_{0}^{\alpha}

(26)

\begin{array}[]{rcl}(o(g)-\widetilde{u_{g}})u_{h}\widetilde{u_{g}}&=&u_{h}\left(o(g)-\sum_{j=0}^{o(g)-1}(-1)^{j}u_{g}^{j}\right)\widetilde{u_{g}}\\
&=&u_{h}\left(o(g)-\sum_{j=0}^{o(g)-1}(-1)^{j}\right)\widetilde{u_{g}}\\
&=&o(g)u_{h}\widetilde{u_{g}}.\end{array}

Analogously
\widetilde{u_{g}}u_{h}(o(g)-\widetilde{u_{g}})=o(g)\widetilde{u_{g}}u_{h}
.
Thus the
H
-units are indeed elements of
{\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}^{\alpha}[G])
.
Furthermore, for
\mathcal{F}=\{\widehat{u_{g}}=\frac{1}{o(g)}\widetilde{u_{g}}\mid g\in\mathcal{G}_{0}^{\alpha}\}
,
every
H
-unit has an appropriate power that is in
\text{GBic}^{\mathcal{F}}({\mathbb{Q}}^{\alpha}[G],{\mathbb{Z}})
.
However, as will be pointed out in
Remark

8.4
, due to the factor
\frac{y}{o(g)}
in general

(27)

\mathcal{H}_{\alpha}(G)\nsubseteq\text{GBic}^{\mathcal{F}}({\mathbb{Q}}^{\alpha}[G],{\mathbb{Z}}).

A concrete example will be given in
Proposition

8.2
. That
\langle\text{GBic}^{\mathcal{F}}({\mathbb{Q}}^{\alpha}[G],{\mathbb{Z}}),\mathcal{H}_{\alpha}(G)\rangle
is not necessarily obtained by bicylic units, even not up to commensurability, is truly its raison d’être and hence will be crucial for the applications.

Example
.

If
\alpha
is a trivial cocycle, then
\mathcal{G}_{j}^{\alpha}=\emptyset=C_{g}^{-}
for any
j\neq 0
,
g\in G
and
\mathcal{G}_{0}^{\alpha}=G
. Therefore, in that case
{\mathbb{Q}}^{\alpha}[G]={\mathbb{Q}}[G]
and
\mathcal{H}_{\alpha}(G)=\{u_{1}\}
. In particular
\langle\text{GBic}^{\mathcal{F}}({\mathbb{Q}}^{\alpha}[G],{\mathbb{Z}}),\mathcal{H}_{\alpha}(G)\rangle=\text{Bic}(G)
, i.e. we recover exactly the classical bicyclic units.

Remark 6.10
.

(1)

The elements
u_{1}+(o(g)-\widetilde{u_{g}})\frac{u_{h}}{o(g)}\widetilde{u_{g}}
and
u_{1}+\widetilde{u_{g}}\frac{u_{h}}{o(g)}(o(g)-\widetilde{u_{g}})
are in fact instances of a common more general construction of units. Indeed, consider
\alpha\in Z^{2}(G,{\mathbb{Z}}[\zeta_{n}]^{*})
with
o(\alpha)\mid n
and let
\xi
be a
o(g)
-root of unity inside
{\mathbb{Z}}[\zeta_{n}]
. Then
u_{1}+u_{h}\,\widetilde{\xi u_{g}}
is an invertible element of
{\mathbb{Z}}[\zeta_{n}]^{\alpha}G
with inverse
u_{1}-u_{h}\,\widetilde{\xi u_{g}}
. Note that the choice
\xi=-1
yields
u_{1}+u_{h}\,\widetilde{(-u_{g})}=u_{1}+\widetilde{u_{g}}u_{h}=u_{1}+\widetilde{u_{g}}\frac{u_{h}}{o(g)}(o(g)-\widetilde{u_{g}})
, as desired. As we will see in
Section

10.1
, an essential ingredient is that
\widetilde{[u_{g},u_{h}]}=0
.

(2)

The
H
in the name
H
-unit refers to the cohomology group
H^{2}(G,{\mathbb{Z}}^{*})
. Following (
26
), the elements in
Definition

6.9
look like roots of bicyclic units. However these elements and those in the previous point fit in a general new generic construction of units which will be developed in
Section

10
. There the role of the
H^{2}
, i.e. splitting into extensions, will become more apparent. In loc. cit. the units will in fact be called primitive
H
-units (as they are the smallest of their kind).

(3)

Using the twisting as above one could instead have considered a deformed version of the classical bicyclic units, i.e. for any
x\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{\alpha}[G]
all the elements of the form
1+(o(g)-\widetilde{u_{g}})(x+\frac{y}{o(g)})\widetilde{u_{g}}
are units. Such elements include both
H
-units and
\text{GBic}^{\mathcal{F}}({\mathbb{Q}}^{\alpha}[G],{\mathbb{Z}})
. However, as hopefully
Section

10
will convince, it seems to be better to not think in terms of bicyclic units.

Thanks to (
27
), one can
apply
Corollary

6.7
to show that
\langle\text{GBic}^{\mathcal{F}}({\mathbb{Q}}^{\alpha}[G],{\mathbb{Z}}),\mathcal{H}_{\alpha}(G)\rangle
contains sufficiently many elementary matrices. It follows from the proof of
Corollary

6.7
that the value
y\in{\mathbb{Z}}
is the one yielded by
Theorem

6.3
. However by adding the
H
-units, depending on the order of the twisted elements and the number of
\alpha
-irregular elements, one can aim to decrease the value of
y
.

Question 6.11
.

What is a formula for the smallest
y\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that
1-e+E_{m}(y\mathcal{O})\leq\langle\text{GBic}^{\mathcal{F}}({\mathbb{Q}}^{\alpha}[G],{\mathbb{Z}}),\mathcal{H}_{\alpha}(G)\rangle
?

6.3.
On
H
-units in cokernel of the transgression map

We now return to the setting of
Section

3.2
. More precisely we consider the central extension (
3
) corresponding to
[\alpha]\in H^{2}(G,A)
. We also fix a normalized representant
\alpha\in Z^{2}(G,A)
of the form
\alpha(g,h)=\mu(g)\mu(h)\mu(gh)^{-1}
for a section
\mu
of
\lambda:\Gamma\twoheadrightarrow G
.

The
H
-units from the previous section will now allow to construct elements in
\operatorname{coker}(\widetilde{\Psi_{\chi}})
. For this take elements
g\in\mathcal{G}_{0}^{\operatorname{Tra}_{\alpha}(\chi)}
and
h\in C_{g}^{-}
. Note that the existence of such an element
h
indirectly assumes that
g
is
\operatorname{Tra}_{\alpha}(\chi)
-irregular. In particular
\operatorname{Tra}_{\alpha}(\chi)
must be a non-trivial cocycle and hence
\chi\neq\omega_{A}
. Furthermore we will also consider a central subgroup
Q
of
\Gamma
such that
A\cap Q=1
. Now with every such triple
(g,h,Q)
we consider the set

(28)

\mathcal{H}^{min}_{g,h,Q}:=\{1+zu_{h}\widetilde{u_{g}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{\operatorname{Tra}_{\alpha}(\chi)}[G]\mid z\in\{1-u_{q}\,:\,q\in Q\text{ or }u_{q}=0\}\}.

Proposition 6.12
.

Let
\chi\in\operatorname{Hom}(A,{\mathbb{Z}}^{*}),g\in\mathcal{G}_{0}^{\operatorname{Tra}_{\alpha}(\chi)},h\in C_{g}^{-}
and
Q
a central subgroup of
\Gamma
such that
A\cap Q=1
. Suppose
{\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}G)
is finite, then the following hold

(1)

if
q\in Q\setminus\langle\mu(g)\rangle
or
u_{q}=0
, then
1+(1-u_{q})u_{h}\widetilde{u_{g}}\notin\im\left({\left.\kern-1.2pt\widetilde{\Psi_{\chi}}\right|_{{\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}[\Gamma])}}\right)

(2)

if
Q\cap\langle\mu(g)\rangle=1=A\cap\langle\mu(g)\rangle
, then
\langle\,\mathcal{H}^{min}_{g,h,Q}\,\rangle\cong C_{2}^{|Q|}
as subgroup of
\operatorname{coker}\left({\left.\kern-1.2pt\widetilde{\Psi_{\chi}}\right|_{{\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}[\Gamma])}}\right)
.

Keeping in mind that
\{1-q\mid 1\neq q\in Q\}
is a
{\mathbb{Z}}
-basis of
\ker(\omega_{Q})
, a direct computation yields that

\langle\,\mathcal{H}^{min}_{g,h,Q}\,\rangle=\{1+yu_{h}\widetilde{u_{g}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{\operatorname{Tra}_{\alpha}(\chi)}[G]\mid y\in\ker(\omega_{Q})\cup\{1\}\}.

In particular, when
Q\cap\langle u_{g}\rangle=1
then
\langle\,\mathcal{H}^{min}_{g,h,Q}\rangle
can be viewed as subgroup of
\operatorname{coker}(\widetilde{\Psi}_{ker})
. Note also that
qh\in C_{g}^{-}
for all
q\in Q
and hence
\langle\,\mathcal{H}^{min}_{g,h,Q}\,\rangle\leq\mathcal{H}_{\alpha}(G).

Proof of
Proposition

6.12
.

Due to the centrality of
z=1-u_{q}
it follows that
1+zu_{h}\widetilde{u_{g}}
is trivial if and only if
u_{q}\in\langle u_{g}\rangle
(in particular for
z=1
it is never trivial). On turn this is equivalent to
q\in Q\setminus\langle\mu(g)\rangle
(or
q
trivial) thanks to the assumption
o(g)=o(u_{g})
. From now we assume that this is the case and let
y
be a sum of different such elements (
1-u_{q}
with
q\in Q\setminus\langle\mu(g)\rangle
or
1
). Then the unit
1+(o(g)-\widetilde{u_{g}})y\frac{u_{h}}{o(g)}\widetilde{u_{g}}
is also non-trivial. We claim that more generally all such element are not in the image.

An element in
\Psi_{\chi}^{-1}(1+yu_{h}\widetilde{u_{g}})
is of the form
\tau:=1+y\mu(h)(1+\mu(g)+\cdots+\mu(g)^{o(g)-1})+x
for some
x\in\ker(\Psi_{\chi})
and due to the non-triviality
\tau\neq 1
. We need to prove that
\tau\notin{\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}\Gamma)
for any
x
. Assume otherwise and start by decomposing
x
according to
Proposition

3.3
:

x=\sum_{a\in\ker(\chi)}x_{a}(a-1)+\sum_{a\in A\setminus\ker(\chi)}x_{a}(a+1)

with
x_{a}=t_{a}+\sum_{1\neq w\in G}t_{w,a}\mu(w)\in{\mathbb{Z}}[\Gamma]
.

Therefore, since
\mu(g)^{o(g)}\in A
,

\omega_{A}(\tau)=1+yh\widetilde{g}+2\sum_{a\in A\setminus\ker(\chi)}x_{a}.

Note that
yh\widetilde{g}\neq 0
and in fact all its coefficients are
\pm 1
due to all the assumptions. Furthermore,
\omega_{A}(\tau)
is a torsion unit since
{\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}G)
is finite. Since the coefficient of the identity element of
G
is
1+2\sum_{a\in A\setminus\ker(\chi)}t_{a}
, which is non-zero, Theorem
5.6
yields that
\omega_{A}(\tau)=\pm 1
. However
\omega_{A}(\tau)=1+yh\widetilde{g}\mod 2\neq 1
. This yields the desired contradiction and finishes the proof of the claim and in particular of
(1)
.

For the second part, assume that
Q\cap\langle\mu(g)\rangle=1=A\cap\langle\mu(g)\rangle
. Note that
g\in\mathcal{G}_{0}^{\operatorname{Tra}_{\alpha}(\chi)}
implies that
\mu(g)^{o(g)}\in\ker(\chi)
. The stronger condition
A\cap\langle\mu(g)\rangle=1
, equivalently
\mu(g)^{o(g)}=1
, is assumed to have that
\Psi_{\chi}(\widetilde{\mu(g)})=\widetilde{u_{g}}
. Denote
b_{z}:=1+zu_{h}\widetilde{u_{g}}.
Note that
b_{z_{1}}b_{z_{2}}=1+(z_{1}+z_{2})u_{h}\widetilde{u_{g}}
. Moreover, all the
b_{z}
commute and in fact
\langle\,b_{1-q}\mid 1\neq q\in Q\rangle
is isomorphic to the additive group
\ker(\omega_{Q})
. By the general claim every
b_{z_{1}}\ldots b_{z_{l}}
, for different
z_{i}\in\{1,1-u_{q}\}
, is not in the image. Thus it remains to prove that the square of every
b_{z}
is attained. For this rewrite

\begin{array}[]{rcl}b_{1-q}^{2}&=&1+2(u_{h}-u_{qh})\widetilde{u_{g}}\\
&=&\left(1+2u_{h}\widetilde{u_{g}}\right)\left(1+2u_{qh}\widetilde{u_{g}}\right)^{-1}\\
&=&\widetilde{\Psi_{\chi}}(u_{1})\,\widetilde{\Psi_{\chi}}(u_{2}^{-1})\end{array}

where
u_{1}=1+(1-[\mu(g),\mu(h)])\mu(h)\widetilde{\mu(g)}
and
u_{2}=1+(1-[\mu(g),\mu(qh)])\mu(qh)\widetilde{\mu(g)}
. Note that
[\mu(g),\mu(h)],[\mu(g),\mu(qh)]\in A\setminus\ker(\chi)
because
h,qh\in C_{g}^{-}
. That
u_{1}
and
u_{2}
are units can be seen by rewriting them as bicyclic units, e.g.
u_{1}=1+(1-\mu(g))\mu(h)\widetilde{\mu(g)}
.
∎

We expect that
H
-units contribute to
\operatorname{coker}(\Psi_{\chi})
in full generality, in the way they do in
Proposition

6.12
.

Question 6.13
.

Is the conclusion of
Proposition

6.12
also valid without the condition that
{\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}G)
is finite?

Most likely, in general other type of elements can be contained in
\operatorname{coker}(\widetilde{\Psi_{\chi}})
. However in
Section

8
we will see that for
\Gamma
an extension of
C_{2}
with an elementary abelian
2
-group
G=C_{2}^{n}
that these elements will generate the full cokernel. Consequently, in that case
\operatorname{coker}(\widetilde{\Psi}_{ker})=\ker(F_{Cok})\cong C_{2}^{n-2}
. It would be interesting to when this happens.

Remark 6.14
.

With exactly the same proof, the statement of
Proposition

6.12
also holds for the
H
-units
1+\widetilde{u_{g}}u_{h}z
. Hence
6.13
can also be formulated for these elements. In fact it is also an interesting question to understand the image of
\langle b_{1}:=1+u_{h}\widetilde{u_{g}},b_{2}:=1+\widetilde{u_{g}}u_{h}\rangle
. In case
o(u_{h})=o(u_{g})=o(g)=2
, then a direct computation shows that
b_{1}b_{2}^{-1}\in\im\left({\left.\kern-1.2pt\widetilde{\Psi_{\chi}}\right|_{{\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}[\Gamma])}}\right).
Hence
\langle b_{1},b_{2}\rangle\cong C_{2}
as subgroup of
\operatorname{coker}\left({\left.\kern-1.2pt\widetilde{\Psi_{\chi}}\right|_{{\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}[\Gamma])}}\right)
. It is however very likely that this is an order
2
phenomena.

7.
Method to describe
\mathcal{U}({\mathbb{Z}}^{\alpha}[G\times C_{2}^{n}])

Let
G
be a finite group and

C_{2}^{n}=\langle x_{1}\rangle\times\cdots\times\langle x_{n}\rangle

an elementary abelian
2
-group. In this section we will consider a, potentially trivial,
2
-cohomology class
[\alpha]
in the image of the inflation map

(29)

\operatorname{Inf}:H^{2}(G,{\mathbb{Z}}^{*})\rightarrow H^{2}(G\times C_{2}^{n},{\mathbb{Z}}^{*}).

In particular, by
Corollary

2.5
, the subgroup
C_{2}^{n}=\langle u_{x_{i}}\rangle
is central in
{\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}^{\alpha}[G\times C_{2}^{n}])
. For such cocycles we will now present a method to describe
\mathcal{U}({\mathbb{Z}}^{\alpha}[G\times C_{2}^{n}])
whenever
\mathcal{U}({\mathbb{Z}}^{\alpha}[G])
is known.

Convention:

C_{2}^{i}
denotes the concrete subgroup
\langle x_{1},\ldots,x_{i}\rangle
. In particular when we write
{\mathbb{Z}}^{\alpha}[G\times C_{2}^{i}]
we truly mean the subring of
{\mathbb{Z}}^{\alpha}[G\times C_{2}^{n}]
generated by
G\times C_{2}^{i}
.

To start, consider for every
1\leq i\leq n
the natural projection

\psi_{i}:{\mathbb{Z}}^{\alpha}[G\times C_{2}^{i}]\rightarrow{\mathbb{Z}}^{\alpha}[G\times C_{2}^{i-1}]

induced by mapping
x_{i}
to
1
, which is a ring
morphism
31
31

31

This is a ring-morphism due to the centrality of
x_{i}
. In particular for arbitrary

2
-cocycles this method does not work.
. Note that, due to the centrality of
x_{i}
,

{\mathbb{Z}}^{\alpha}[G\times C_{2}^{i}]=\left({\mathbb{Z}}^{\alpha}[G\times C_{2}^{i-1}]\right)[\langle x_{i}\rangle]
and
therefore
\psi_{i}
is globally defined by
\psi_{i}(u+vx_{i})=u+v
for

u,v\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{\alpha}[G\times C_{2}^{i-1}]
. By definition
\psi_{i}
induces an epimorphism

\widetilde{\psi}_{i}:\mathcal{U}({\mathbb{Z}}^{\alpha}[G\times C_{2}^{i}])\rightarrow\mathcal{U}({\mathbb{Z}}^{\alpha}[G\times C_{2}^{i-1}]).

Accordingly, we obtain the following splitting.

Lemma 7.1
.

Let
K_{i}=\ker(\widetilde{\psi}_{i})
. Then,
\mathcal{U}({\mathbb{Z}}^{\alpha}[G\times C_{2}^{i}])=K_{i}\rtimes\mathcal{U}({\mathbb{Z}}^{\alpha}[G\times C_{2}^{i-1}]).
Consequently,

\mathcal{U}({\mathbb{Z}}^{\alpha}[G\times C_{2}^{n}])\cong K_{n}\rtimes\Big(K_{n-1}\rtimes\big(\cdots K_{2}\rtimes(K_{1}\rtimes\mathcal{U}({\mathbb{Z}}^{\alpha}G))\big)\Big).

Proof.

Since
\widetilde{\psi}_{i}
is surjective we obtain an extension

1\rightarrow K_{i}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{U}({\mathbb{Z}}^{\alpha}[G\times C_{2}^{i}])\xrightarrow{\widetilde{\psi}_{i}}\mathcal{U}({\mathbb{Z}}^{\alpha}[G\times C_{2}^{i-1}])\rightarrow 1.

Clearly, this extension is split by the identity map. The second part now follows by iteration.
∎

In view of
Lemma

7.1
we will now concentrate on describing all the kernels
K_{i}
. To this end we define the groups

U_{i}=\{1+2^{i}u\mid u\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{\alpha}G\}\cap\mathcal{U}({\mathbb{Z}}^{\alpha}G).

Our goal is to show that
K_{i}
can be built from isomorphic copies of the groups
U_{1},\ldots,U_{i}
and the unit group
\mathcal{U}({\mathbb{Z}}^{\alpha}G)
. Note that, by
Theorem

5.6
,
U_{i}
is torsion-free when
i\geq 2
and the only torsion elements in
U_{1}
are
\pm 1
.

As a tool we need to consider more generally the groups

U_{k,j}=\{1+2^{k}u\mid u\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{\alpha}[G\times C_{2}^{j}]\}\cap\mathcal{U}({\mathbb{Z}}^{\alpha}[G\times C_{2}^{j}]).

Note that
U_{k,0}=U_{k}
and
U_{k_{1},j_{1}}\leq U_{k_{2},j_{2}}
for
k_{2}\leq k_{1}
and
j_{1}\leq j_{2}
. Next to these, the projections

\varphi_{i}:{\mathbb{Z}}^{\alpha}[G\times C_{2}^{i}]\rightarrow{\mathbb{Z}}^{\alpha}[G\times C_{2}^{i-1}]\mbox{ with }x_{i}\mapsto-1

will also be instrumental. The induced epimorphism on the unit groups is denoted by
\widetilde{\varphi}_{i}
.

Lemma 7.2
.

For all
1\leq k,j
, with notations as above, we have that

•

K_{j}\cong\widetilde{\varphi}_{j}(K_{j})=U_{1,j-1},

•

U_{k,j}\cong U_{k+1,j-1}\rtimes U_{k,j-1}
.

Proof.

By definition,

K_{j}=\{1+u(1-x_{j})\mid u\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{\alpha}[G\times C_{2}^{j-1}]\}\cap\mathcal{U}({\mathbb{Z}}^{\alpha}[G\times C_{2}^{j}]).

Using the centrality of
x_{j}
, one gets that
{\mathbb{Z}}^{\alpha}[G\times C_{2}^{j}]=\left({\mathbb{Z}}^{\alpha}[G\times C_{2}^{j-1}]\right)\langle x_{j}\rangle
and thus one immediately obtains that
\widetilde{\varphi}_{j}
is injective on
K_{j}
. Consequently,
K_{j}\cong\widetilde{\varphi}_{j}(K_{j})
and more explicitly

\widetilde{\varphi}_{j}(K_{j})=\{1+2u\mid u\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{\alpha}[G\times C_{2}^{j-1}]\}\cap\mathcal{U}({\mathbb{Z}}^{\alpha}[G\times C_{2}^{j-1}])=U_{1,j-1}.

Note that
\widetilde{\varphi}_{j}(K_{j})
contains all the units above because
{\mathbb{Z}}^{\alpha}[G\times C_{2}^{j-1}]
is truly meant as the subring of
{\mathbb{Z}}^{\alpha}[G\times C_{2}^{j}]
.

For the second statement, remark that
\widetilde{\psi}_{j}
is the identity map on
U_{k,i}
for all
0\leq i<j
and all
k
. Also,
\widetilde{\psi}_{j}(U_{k,j})=\widetilde{\psi}_{j}(U_{k,j-1})
where
U_{k,j-1}
is the subgroup
U_{k,j}\cap{\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}^{\alpha}[G\times\langle x_{1},\ldots,x_{j-1}\rangle])
. Combined we obtain the internal splitting

(30)

U_{k,j}=\ker({\left.\kern-1.2pt\widetilde{\psi}_{j}\right|_{U_{k,j}}})\rtimes U_{k,j-1}.

Next, since
\ker({\left.\kern-1.2pt\widetilde{\psi}_{j}\right|_{U_{k,j}}})=\{1+2^{k}u(1-x_{j})\mid u\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{\alpha}[G\times C_{2}^{j-1}]\}\cap U_{k,j}
the map
\widetilde{\varphi}_{j}
is injective on it and so
\widetilde{\varphi}_{j}\left(\ker({\left.\kern-1.2pt\widetilde{\psi}_{j}\right|_{U_{k,j}}})\right)\cong U_{k+1,j-1}
, finishing the proof.
∎

An iterative process of the previous lemma decreases the second index of
U_{k,j}
, with the cost of producing extra complements. Hence it now readily follows that we can reduce to the groups
U_{i}
. For the applications to classical group rings in
Section

10
we however need an explicit internal splitting of
K_{i}
. As indicated in the proof of
Lemma

7.2
such a splitting is available for
U_{k,j}
. Inspired by this we define for every tuple
\vec{j}=(j_{1},\ldots,j_{i-1})\in\{0,1\}^{i-1}
the following:

(31)

K_{\vec{j}}:=\{1+u(1-x_{1})^{j_{1}}\cdots(1-x_{i-1})^{j_{i-1}}(1-x_{i})\mid u\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{\alpha}[G]\}\cap{\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}^{\alpha}[G\times C_{2}^{i}]).

Using that
gx_{l}(1-x_{l})=-g(1-x_{l})
, it is easily verified that
K_{\vec{j}}
is a normal subgroup of
{\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}^{\alpha}[G\times\langle x_{l}:j_{l}\neq 0\rangle])
. In general however it won’t be normal in
K_{i}
.

Theorem 7.3
.

Let
G
be a finite group,
[\alpha]\in{\rm Im}(\operatorname{Inf})
as in (
29
). Then, for all
i\geq 2
,

(32)

K_{i}:=\underset{\vec{j}\in\{0,1\}^{i-1}}{\overrightarrow{{{\rtimes}}}}K_{\vec{j}}

where the internal semi-direct product is with respect to a specific ordering on
\{0,1\}^{i-1}
, and
K_{\vec{j}}\cong U_{1+\sum_{t=1}^{i-1}j_{t}}
for
\vec{j}=(j_{1},\ldots,j_{i-1})\in\{0,1\}^{i-1}
.
Furthermore,

\begin{array}[]{rl}\mathcal{U}({\mathbb{Z}}^{\alpha}[G\times C_{2}^{n}])&=K_{n}\rtimes\Big(K_{n-1}\rtimes\big(\cdots K_{2}\rtimes(K_{1}\rtimes\mathcal{U}({\mathbb{Z}}^{\alpha}G))\big)\Big)\\
&=\Big(\big((N_{n}\rtimes N_{n-1})\rtimes\cdots\big)\rtimes N_{1}\Big)\rtimes\big(\mathcal{U}({\mathbb{Z}}^{\alpha}[G])\times\langle x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\rangle\big)\end{array}

for some torsion-free normal subgroup
N_{i}
of
K_{i}
such that
K_{i}\cong N_{i}\times\langle x_{i}\rangle
.

Remark
.

The ordering in (
32
) is deducible from the proof. In particular, in terms of the
U_{i}
the decomposition of
K_{i}
can be defined recursively as follows:
K_{1}\cong U_{1}
. For
i\geq 2
,
K_{i}\cong\mathcal{U}_{i}\rtimes K_{i-1}
with
\mathcal{U}_{2}:=U_{2}
and
\mathcal{U}_{i}:=\mathcal{U}_{i-1}[1]\rtimes\mathcal{U}_{i-1}
where
\mathcal{U}_{i-1}[1]
looks like
\mathcal{U}_{i-1}
but with all indices increased by one. For example,
K_{2}\cong U_{2}\rtimes K_{1},K_{3}\cong(U_{3}\rtimes U_{2})\rtimes K_{2},K_{4}\cong\left((U_{4}\rtimes U_{3})\rtimes(U_{3}\rtimes U_{2})\right)\rtimes K_{3}
and

K_{5}\cong(\Big((U_{5}\rtimes U_{4})\rtimes(U_{4}\rtimes U_{3})\Big)\rtimes\Big((U_{4}\rtimes U_{3})\rtimes(U_{3}\rtimes U_{2})\Big))\rtimes K_{4}.

Remark
.

For certain class of nilpotent groups
G
, Jespers, Leal and del Río give in
[
30
, Proposition 5]
a description of
{\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}G)
in terms of some subnormal series. The
2
-groups in their class are of the form
H\times C_{2}^{n}
for some
n
and
H\leq G
. In that case their subnormal series coincides with the one in
Theorem

7.3
.

Proof of
Theorem

7.3
.

The description of
K_{i}
follows from an iterative use of
Lemma

7.2
or rather the explicit form in (
30
). In fact we will proof more generally that

U_{k,i}=\underset{\vec{j}\in\{0,1\}^{i}}{\overrightarrow{{{\rtimes}}}}U_{k,\vec{j}}

where
U_{k,\vec{j}}=\{1+2^{k}u(1-x_{1})^{j_{1}}\cdots(1-x_{i})^{j_{i}}\mid u\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{\alpha}[G]\}\cap{\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}^{\alpha}[G\times C_{2}^{i}])
. To start one uses

K_{i}\cong\widetilde{\varphi}_{i}(K_{i})\cong U_{1,i-1}\cong U_{2,i-1}\rtimes U_{1,i-2}\cong U_{2,i-1}\rtimes K_{i-1}.

Explicitly the copy of
U_{2,i-1}
in
K_{i}
is given by
\{1+u(1-x_{i-1})(1-x_{i})\in K_{i}\mid u\in{\mathbb{Z}}[G\times\langle x_{1},\ldots,x_{i-2}\rangle]\}
and the copy of
K_{i-1}
is
\{1+v(1-x_{i})\in K_{i}\mid v\in{\mathbb{Z}}[G\times\langle x_{1},\ldots,x_{i-2}\rangle]\}
. In terms of
U_{1,i-1}
the
1-x_{i}
is identified with a factor
2
via
\widetilde{\varphi}
. Now, if
i=2
the procedure finishes here and yields the decomposition
(\ref{decomp in precise pice without saying order})
. Next, for an arbitrary
i
one case use now induction on the copy of
K_{i-1}
inside
K_{i}
. This yields all the terms
K_{\vec{j}}
such that
j(i-1)=0
(i.e. no
x_{i-1}
in the support). The other terms will appear by applying induction to
U_{2,i-1}
(in the recursive process this corresponds to applying the the second part of
Lemma

7.2
to
U_{2,i-1}
).

The first equality of the second part was proven in
Lemma

7.1
. Furthermore, it is easy to see that the brackets can be rearranged to
{\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}^{\alpha}[G\times C_{2}^{n}])=\Big(\big((K_{n}\rtimes K_{n-1})\rtimes\cdots\big)\rtimes K_{1}\Big)\rtimes\mathcal{U}({\mathbb{Z}}^{\alpha}G)
. Hence due to the centrality of the subgroup
C_{2}^{n}
, it remains to prove the existence of such
N_{i}
that is normal in
K_{i-1}
. For this notice that
(\ref{decomp in precise pice without saying order})
yields only one subgroup isomorphic to
U_{1}
, namely
K_{\vec{0}}=\{1+(1-x_{i})u\mid u\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{\alpha}[G]\}\cap\mathcal{U}({\mathbb{Z}}^{\alpha}[G\times\langle x_{i}\rangle]).
All the other
K_{\vec{j}}
will be isomorphic to
U_{l}
with
2\leq l\leq i
and hence are torsion-free. Now, under the isomorphism
K_{\vec{0}}\cong U_{1}
, the subgroup
\langle x_{i}\rangle
corresponds to
\{\pm 1\}
which is the only torsion in
U_{1}
. Therefore
K_{\vec{0}}\cong F_{i}\times\langle x_{i}\rangle
for some torsion-free subgroup
F_{i}
. Taking
F_{i}
together with all the
K_{\vec{j}}
with
\vec{j}\neq\vec{0}
we obtain the desired torsion-free subgroup
N_{i}
of
K_{i}
such that
K_{i}\cong N_{i}\times\langle x_{i}\rangle
.
∎

The decomposition obtained allows to transfer properties of
\mathcal{U}({\mathbb{Z}}^{\alpha}G)
to
\mathcal{U}({\mathbb{Z}}^{\alpha}[G\times C_{2}^{n}])
. Earlier interesting results in that line can be found in
[
3
,
17
,
41
,
18
]
.

Corollary 7.4
.

Let
G
be a finite group,
[\alpha]\in{\rm Im}(\operatorname{Inf})
as in (
29
). If
\mathcal{U}({\mathbb{Z}}^{\alpha}G)
satisfies one of the following properties:

(1)

\pm G
has a normal (torsion-free) complement in
\mathcal{U}({\mathbb{Z}}^{\alpha}[G])

(2)

it is commensurable with a direct product of free-by-free groups

(3)

has a non-trivial amalgam decomposition

(4)

G
satisfies (HSP)
32
32

32

(HSP) stands for the Higman subgroup property, i.e. any finite subgroup in
V({\mathbb{Z}}^{\alpha}G)
is isomorphic to a subgroup in
G
. See also
[
45
]
.

then the same holds for
G\times C_{2}^{n}
and
\mathcal{U}({\mathbb{Z}}^{\alpha}[G\times C_{2}^{n}])
.

In the case of untwisted
33
33

33

The classical Zassenhaus conjectures are stated for group rings, however also literally makes sense for twisted group rings. For example (ZC3) would be that all finite
H\leq U({\mathbb{Z}}^{\alpha}[G])
are conjugated over
{\mathbb{Q}}^{\alpha}[G]
to a subgroup of
G
.
group rings it was proven in
[
18
]
that the first Zassenhaus conjecture (ZC1) is preserved. It would be interesting to prove so for twisted group rings. Especially since the counterexample to (ZC1) of Eisele and Margolis
[
14
]
, positive instances of the Zassenhaus conjectures are of special interest. For a recent survey see
[
46
]
.

Proof of
Corollary

7.4
.

The first part directly follows from the last decomposition in
Theorem

7.3
, since
\big((N_{n}\rtimes N_{n-1})\rtimes\cdots\big)\rtimes N_{1}
is torsion-free.

Next, for the second statement, let
A
be a finite dimensional semisimple
{\mathbb{Q}}
-algebra and
\mathcal{O}
an order in
A
. The desired statement is a direct consequence of the fact that ‘
{\mathcal{U}}(\mathcal{O})
commensurable with a direct product of free-by-free groups’ is fully determined by the type of the simple components of the algebra
A
, as follows from
[
33
, Theorem 2.1]
. For instance,
[
33
, Lemma 3.1. & Proposition 3.3.]
(or
[
27
]
) yields that
34
34

34

To conclude this it is used that free groups are exactly the groups with cohomological dimension one
[
56
,
57
]
. Due to this free-by-free groups have virtual cohomological dimension at most two which is the content of
[
33
, Section 3]
.
the property holds if and only if the simple quotients of
A
are either a field, a totally definite quaternion algebra or a matrix algebra
\operatorname{M}_{2}(D)
with
D
in a list of certain quadratic imaginary extensions of
{\mathbb{Q}}
or certain quaternion algebras over totally real number fields. However if
[\alpha]
is in the image of (
29
), then

{\mathbb{Q}}^{\alpha}[G\times C_{2}^{n}]\cong{\mathbb{Q}}^{\alpha}[G]\otimes_{{\mathbb{Q}}}{\mathbb{Q}}[C_{2}^{n}]\cong\underbrace{{\mathbb{Q}}^{\alpha}[G]\oplus\cdots\oplus{\mathbb{Q}}^{\alpha}[G]}_{2^{n}-times}.

Thus the isomorphism types of simple quotients of
{\mathbb{Q}}^{\alpha}[G\times C_{2}^{n}]
is the same as for
{\mathbb{Q}}^{\alpha}[G]
.

Concerning the third statement, note that, due to
Theorem

7.3
,
\mathcal{U}({\mathbb{Z}}^{\alpha}[G])
is an epimorphic image of
\mathcal{U}({\mathbb{Z}}^{\alpha}[G\times C_{2}^{n}])
and hence its amalgam decomposition can be lifted along the epimorphism.

For the fourth statement, first notice that by induction we may assume that
n=1
. Hence, with the above
notation,
\mathcal{U}({\mathbb{Z}}^{\alpha}[G\times C_{2}])=K_{1}\rtimes\mathcal{U}({\mathbb{Z}}^{\alpha}[G])
with
K_{1}\cong N_{1}\times\langle x_{1}\rangle
where
N_{1}
is a torsion-free normal subgroup. For (SIP), assume now that
H
is a finite subgroup of
\mathcal{U}({\mathbb{Z}}^{\alpha}[G\times C_{2}])
. As
N_{1}
is torsion-free,
H\cong H/(H\cap N_{1})
and so we may consider
H
as a subgroup of
\mathcal{U}({\mathbb{Z}}^{\alpha}[G\times C_{2}])/N_{1}\cong{\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}^{\alpha}[G])\times C_{2}.
Consequently, there exist finite index sets
I
and
J
and
t_{i},k_{j}\in{\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}^{\alpha}[G])
such that
H/(H\cap N_{1})=\{t_{i},x_{1}k_{j}\mid i\in I,\,j\in J\}
. Now consider the larger group
T=\langle t_{i},k_{j},x_{1}\mid i\in I,\,j\in J\rangle
. Using that
x_{1}
is a central element of order
2
, it is directly checked that
T
is a (finitely generated) torsion group. Since it is also linear,
T
is in fact finite. By assumption
\langle t_{i},k_{j}\mid i\in I,\,j\in J\rangle
is isomorphic to a subgroup of
G
and hence
T
to one of
G\times C_{2}
. Consequently, also the smaller group
H\cong H/(H\cap N_{1})
also as needed.
∎

Remark 7.5
.

The proof of (2) in
Corollary

7.4
in fact shows that any group theoretical property
\mathcal{P}
that can be read off the Wedderburn-Artin components (i.e. the simple quotients) is inherited. Other examples of such properties are: Kazhdan’s property
(T)
, property FAb and HFA (see
[
1
,
2
]
). In general, good candidates for such properties are the ones that are constant on commensurability classes.

Following
Corollary

7.4
the property to have a (torsion-free) normal complement
is preserved, however a concrete description thereof seems difficult. For example, in
[
40
]
it was proven that no
normal complement of the trivial units in
{\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}[D_{8}\times C_{2}\times C_{2}])
is generated by bicyclic units, although
a normal complement in
{\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}[D_{8}\times C_{2}])
is
\operatorname{Bic}(D_{8}\times C_{2})

[
23
]
. For
D_{8}\times C_{2}\times C_{2}
it is unknown whether biyclic units nevertheless form a subgroup of finite index. Recently Bächle, Maheshwary and Margolis
have proven
[
4
, Theorem A]
that
\rm rank\,B^{ab}=\rm rank\,\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}G))
where
B
is the group generated by bicyclic and Bass units in
\Gamma
,
B^{ab}
denotes the abelianisation of
B
and the rank is as finitely generated abelian group. As a consequence, they deduced that if the bicyclic units are of finite index
35
35

35

This is equivalent to say that
the bicyclic units together with the Bass units are of finite index in
{\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}\Gamma)
due to a Theorem of Bass and Milnor, see
[
26
, Theorem 11.1.2. & Prop. 9.5.11.]
.
in
\operatorname{SL}_{1}({\mathbb{Z}}\Gamma)
then
\rm rank\,{\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}\Gamma)^{ab}=\rm rank\,\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}\Gamma))
.
One can read this result as a new method to detect whether the bicyclic units are of infinite index in
\operatorname{SL}_{1}({\mathbb{Z}}\Gamma)
, which happens if
\rm rank\,{\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}\Gamma)^{ab}\gneq\rm rank\,\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}\Gamma))
. This motivates the following question.

Question 7.6
.

With notations as above, what is the connection between
\rm rank\,{\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}^{\alpha}G)^{ab}
and
\rm rank\,{\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}^{\alpha}[G\times C_{2}^{n}])^{ab}
?

8.
Full description for elementary abelian 2-groups

Let
G
be an elementary abelian
2
-group of rank
n+2
,

(33)

G=C_{2}\times C_{2}\times\ldots\times C_{2}=\langle g\rangle\times\langle h\rangle\times\langle x_{1}\rangle\times\ldots\times\langle x_{n}\rangle,

and consider the cohomology class
[\alpha]\in H^{2}(G,C_{2})
determined by the values

(34)

[u_{g},u_{h}]=-1,\quad u_{g}^{2}=u_{h}^{2}=u_{x_{i}}^{2}=[u_{g},u_{x_{i}}]=[u_{h},u_{x_{i}}]=[u_{x_{i}},u_{x_{j}}]=1,

for any
1\leq i\neq j\leq n
.
We can think of this
[\alpha]
as the cohomology class determining the group
\Gamma\cong D_{8}\times C_{2}^{n}
and the twisted group ring
{\mathbb{Z}}^{\alpha}G
.
Recall our standing convention of choosing a normalized
2
-cocycle representant of
[\alpha]
.

To start, we handle the case that
n=0
, i.e. we describe
\mathcal{U}({\mathbb{Z}}^{\alpha}[\langle g,h\rangle])
.

8.1.
The starting case
C_{2}\times C_{2}

Denote by
\widetilde{D}
the subalgebra of
M_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
consisting of the elements

\left\{\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}a&b\\
c&d\end{array}\right)\in\operatorname{M}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})\mid a\equiv_{2}d,\,b\equiv_{2}c\right\}.

It easily can be verified that

(35)

\widetilde{D}=\left\{\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}m+n&k+r\\
k-r&m-n\end{array}\right)\in\operatorname{M}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})\mid m,n,k,r\in\mathbb{Z}\right\}.

Consider now the
\mathbb{Z}
-linear map
\phi:\mathbb{Z}^{\alpha}[C_{2}\times C_{2}]\rightarrow\widetilde{D}
defined by

(36)

u_{1}\mapsto\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&0\\
0&1\end{array}\right),\quad u_{g}\mapsto\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&0\\
0&-1\end{array}\right),\quad u_{h}\mapsto\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&1\\
1&0\end{array}\right),\quad u_{gh}\mapsto\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&1\\
-1&0\end{array}\right)

which can easily be seen to be a ring morphism.

Proposition 8.1
.

With notations as above,
{\mathbb{Z}}^{\alpha}[C_{2}\times C_{2}]\cong\widetilde{D}
as rings.

Proof.

The map
\phi
is surjective because

A=\phi\left(mu_{1}+nu_{g}+ku_{h}+ru_{gh}\right),

for an arbitrary element
A=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}m+n&k+r\\
k-r&m-n\end{array}\right)
of
\widetilde{D}
(see (
35
)). Injectivity follows from a linear independence argument.
∎

Next we will obtain a presentation of the unit group
\mathcal{U}(\mathbb{Z}^{\alpha}[C_{2}\times C_{2}])
.
Already in this small example the role of
H
-units is decisive, as they will yield a normal complement for the trivial units.
As expanded at the end of this section, see
Remark

8.4
, the generalized bicyclic units together
with the trivial units are not sufficient to generate the full unit group. Furthermore, without them we would not be
able to handle later on, even not up to commensurability, the elementary abelian
2
-group case.

Proposition 8.2
.

Let
\alpha
be the
2
-cocycle determined by the values
(1,1,-1)
on
(u_{g}^{2},u_{h}^{2},[u_{g},u_{h}])
. Then

\mathcal{U}({\mathbb{Z}}^{\alpha}[C_{2}\times C_{2}])=F_{2}\rtimes D_{8}

where
D_{8}=\langle u_{g},u_{h}\rangle
and
F_{2}=\langle v,w\rangle
is a free group of rank
2
generated by the
H
-units
v=u_{1}+u_{h}-u_{gh}
and
w=u_{1}+u_{h}+u_{gh}
. The action of
D_{8}
on
F_{2}
is defined by
u_{h}^{-1}vu_{h}=w,u_{h}^{-1}wu_{h}=v
and
u_{g}^{-1}xu_{g}=x^{-1}
for
x=v,w
. Furthermore,

\operatorname{SL}_{1}({\mathbb{Z}}^{\alpha}[C_{2}\times C_{2}])=\mathcal{H}_{\alpha}(C_{2}\times C_{2})=\langle v,w,u_{gh}\rangle.

In particular, it is a subgroup of index
2
in
\mathcal{U}({\mathbb{Z}}^{\alpha}[C_{2}\times C_{2}])
. Also,
\phi(\mathcal{U}({\mathbb{Z}}^{\alpha}[C_{2}\times C_{2}]))
has index
3
in
\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}}).

Recall that
\operatorname{SL}_{1}({\mathbb{Z}}^{\alpha}[C_{2}\times C_{2}])
denotes the group of reduced norm
1
elements, see (
25
).

Proof.

The elements
v
and
w
are examples of
H
-units, see
Definition

6.9
and
(
26
). Moreover, their respective image under
\phi
is equal to

\phi(v)=\phi(1+u_{h}-u_{gh})=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&0\\
0&1\end{array}\right)+\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&1\\
1&0\end{array}\right)-\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&1\\
-1&0\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&0\\
2&1\end{array}\right)

and

\phi(w)=\phi(1+u_{h}+u_{gh})=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&0\\
0&1\end{array}\right)+\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&1\\
1&0\end{array}\right)+\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&1\\
-1&0\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&2\\
0&1\end{array}\right).

Thus the images
\{\phi(v),\phi(w)\}
generate a well-known free group of rank
2
, sometimes called the Sanov subgroup, which is of index
24
in
\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})

(see e.g.
[
39
, Example 2]
).
Therefore,
\{v,w\}
generates a
free group of rank
2
in
\mathcal{U}\left({\mathbb{Z}}^{\alpha}[C_{2}\times C_{2}]\right)
,
say of index
r
. Denote this subgroup by
H
and denote by
T\left(\widetilde{D}\right)=\pm\{u_{1},u_{g},u_{h},u_{gh}\}
the trivial units of
\tilde{D}
. It is
easy to verify that
T\left(\widetilde{D}\right)\cong D_{8}
, the
dihedral group of order
8
, and moreover for any
x\neq y\in\phi^{-1}(T\left(\tilde{D}\right))
, the cosets
xH\neq yH
.
Consequently,
[U\left(\mathbb{Z}^{\alpha}[C_{2}\times C_{2}]\right):H]\geq 8
. As can be seen with the matrices
\phi(u_{h})
and
1+E_{12}
, the subgroup
\phi\left(\mathcal{U}(\mathbb{Z}^{\alpha}[C_{2}\times C_{2}])\right)=\mathcal{U}(\widetilde{D})
is not normal in
GL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
and therefore its index is at
least
3
. Since

24=[GL_{2}(\mathbb{Z}):H]=[GL_{2}(\mathbb{Z}):\mathcal{U}(\widetilde{D})].[\phi\big(\mathcal{U}\left(\mathbb{Z}^{\alpha}[C_{2}\times C_{2}]\right)\big):H]\geq 3\cdot 8,

we conclude that
[\mathcal{U}\left(\mathbb{Z}^{\alpha}[C_{2}\times C_{2}]\right):H]=8
. A
simple calculation shows that

u_{h}^{-1}vu_{h}=w,\quad u_{g}^{-1}vu_{g}=v^{-1},\quad u_{gh}^{-1}vu_{gh}=w^{-1}\,\text{ and }\,u_{g}^{-1}wu_{g}=w^{-1}.

Therefore
H
is normal in
\mathcal{U}\left(\mathbb{Z}^{\alpha}[C_{2}\times C_{2}]\right)
,
and consequently

\mathcal{U}\left(\mathbb{Z}^{\alpha}[C_{2}\times C_{2}]\right)=H\rtimes\phi^{-1}(T\left(\tilde{D}\right))\cong F_{2}\rtimes D_{8}.

The second part of the statement can be checked explicitly.
To start, note that
\mathcal{G}_{0}^{\alpha}=\{u_{h},u_{g},u_{1}\},C_{g}^{-}=\{u_{h},u_{gh}\}
and
C_{h}^{-}=\{u_{g},u_{gh}\}
.
So in total one obtains
8
non-trivial
H
-units that generate
\mathcal{H}_{\alpha}(C_{2}\times C_{2})
.
However they all can be expressed in terms of
v,w,u_{gh}
. Indeed
1+u_{gh}\widetilde{u_{g}}=1-u_{h}u_{g}\widetilde{u_{g}}=w^{-1}

and
1+u_{g}\widetilde{u_{h}}=1+u_{g}+u_{g}u_{h}=u_{gh}v
. The others are similar and, in particular, this shows that

\mathcal{H}_{\alpha}(C_{2}\times C_{2})=\langle v,w,u_{gh}\rangle
.

Finally, recall that
u_{gh}^{2}=-1
and thus
[D_{8}:\langle u_{gh}\rangle]=2.
In particular, from the description of
\mathcal{U}({\mathbb{Z}}^{\alpha}[C_{2}\times C_{2}])
obtained earlier, we now see that
\mathcal{H}_{\alpha}(C_{2}\times C_{2})
is a subgroup of index
2
in
\mathcal{U}({\mathbb{Z}}^{\alpha}[C_{2}\times C_{2}])
.
Besides, because
H
-units are unipotent,
\mathcal{H}_{\alpha}(C_{2}\times C_{2})
is a subgroup of
\operatorname{SL}_{1}({\mathbb{Z}}^{\alpha}[C_{2}\times C_{2}])
.
Since the latter does not contain
u_{h}
, as seen from the matrix representation, it is a proper subgroup of
\mathcal{U}({\mathbb{Z}}^{\alpha}[C_{2}\times C_{2}])
and hence indeed is equal to
\mathcal{H}_{\alpha}(C_{2}\times C_{2})
. That
\phi(\mathcal{U}({\mathbb{Z}}^{\alpha}[C_{2}\times C_{2}]))
has index
3
in
\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})
follows from the fact that
\langle\phi(v),\phi(w)\rangle
has index
24
in
\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})
and
\phi(\mathcal{U}({\mathbb{Z}}^{\alpha}[C_{2}\times C_{2}]))=\langle\phi(v),\phi(w)\rangle\rtimes D_{8}.

∎

The above proof shows the importance of having understanding on subgroup of small index in
\operatorname{SL}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}}).

8.2.
The general case

Now we consider the general case of
G=\langle g,h\rangle\times C_{2}^{n}
. More precisely, we will follow the method outlined in
Section

7
and therefore describe the isomorphism type of the groups
U_{1},\ldots,U_{n}
. Combined with
Theorem

7.3
this would reduce a full description of
\mathcal{U}({\mathbb{Z}}^{\alpha}[\langle g,h\rangle\times C_{2}^{n}])
to describing the actions in the semi-direct products.
We use freely the objects and notations introduced in
Section

7
.

Proposition 8.3
.

The groups
U_{i}
satisfy the following:

•

[{\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}^{\alpha}[\langle g,h\rangle]):U_{1}]=8
,

•

[U_{i}:U_{i+1}]=8
for all
i\geq 1
,

•

U_{1}\cong F_{3}\times C_{2}
,

•

If
i\geq 2
, then
U_{i}
is a free group of rank
n_{i}=1+8^{i-1}
.

Proof.

Recall that, by definition,
U_{i}=\{1+2^{i}u\mid u\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{\alpha}[\langle g,h\rangle]\}\cap\mathcal{U}({\mathbb{Z}}^{\alpha}[\langle g,h\rangle])
. In particular, elements of
U_{1}
are of the form
1+2u
for
u\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{\alpha}[\langle g,h\rangle]
and hence the only trivial units of
{\mathbb{Z}}^{\alpha}[\langle g,h\rangle]
contained in
U_{1}

are
\pm 1
. In fact, using the notations of the proof of
Proposition

8.2
, one has that
A=\langle v^{2},w^{2},wv^{-1},-1\rangle\subseteq U_{1}
. Indeed, recall that
v=1+u_{h}-u_{gh}
and
w=1+u_{h}+u_{gh}
, hence a simple calculation yields that

v^{2}=1+2u_{h}-2u_{gh}=1+2(u_{h}-u_{gh}),\quad w^{2}=1+2u_{h}+2u_{gh}=1+2(u_{h}+u_{gh}).

Additionally,
v^{-1}=1-u_{h}+u_{gh}
and hence

wv^{-1}=-1-2u_{g}+2u_{gh}=1+2(-1-u_{g}+u_{gh}).

We conclude that indeed
A\subseteq U_{1}
. However
v
cannot be written in the form
1+2u
(which already can be seen
\text{mod }2
), thus
A\leq U_{1}\lneq\langle v,w\rangle\times\langle-1\rangle
. Note that
[\langle v,w\rangle:\langle v^{2},w^{2},vw^{-1}\rangle]=2
as the product of any two generators of
\langle v,w\rangle
is in
\langle v^{2},w^{2},vw^{-1}\rangle
. Thus
A=U_{1}
. Now recall the Nielsen-Schreier formula that says that a subgroup of index
t
in a free group of rank
e
is a free group of rank
1+t(e-1)
. Consequently, since
\langle v,w\rangle\cong F_{2}
by
Proposition

8.2
, we obtain all together that

(37)

U_{1}=\langle v^{2},w^{2},wv^{-1}\rangle\times\langle-1\rangle\cong F_{3}\times C_{2}.

Now comparing it to the generators of
{\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}^{\alpha}[\langle g,h\rangle])
we see that
[{\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}^{\alpha}[\langle g,h\rangle]):U_{1}]=8
.

Next, for the statements concerning the
U_{i}
, we use
Proposition

8.1

and that
\phi({\mathbb{Z}}^{\alpha}[\langle g,h\rangle])
is an order contained in the maximal order
\operatorname{M}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})
and thus, by a well-known fact, that an element is invertible in the former if and only if it is in the latter. Hence, we obtain that

(38)

U_{i}\cong\phi(U_{i})=\{\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1+2^{i}a_{11}&2^{i}a_{12}\\
2^{i}a_{21}&1+2^{i}a_{22}\end{array}\right)\mid a_{11}\equiv_{2}a_{22},\,a_{12}\equiv_{2}a_{21}\}\cap\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}}).

Further remark that
U_{1}\leq\operatorname{SL}_{1}({\mathbb{Z}}^{\alpha}[C_{2}\times C_{2}])
and hence
\phi(U_{i})\leq\phi(U_{1})\leq\operatorname{SL}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}}).

Consider now the principal congruence subgroup
\Gamma(2^{i})
of level
2^{i}
in
\operatorname{SL}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})
:

\Gamma(2^{i})=\ker(\pi_{2^{i}}):\operatorname{SL}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})\rightarrow\operatorname{SL}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}}/2^{i}{\mathbb{Z}}).

More concretely,
\Gamma(2^{i})=\big(1+2^{i}\operatorname{M}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})\big)\cap\ \operatorname{SL}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})
. Note that
\Gamma(2^{i+1})\leq\phi(U_{i})\leq\Gamma(2^{i})
. The second part of the statement will be a consequence of the following:

\textit{{\it Claim: }}[\Gamma(2^{i}):\phi(U_{i})]=2\text{ and }[\phi(U_{i}):\Gamma(2^{i+1})]=4\text{ for all }i\geq 1

To start, recall the following well-known formula (e.g. see
36
36

36

In this reference the formula for
[\operatorname{SL}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}}):\ker\left(\operatorname{SL}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})\rightarrow\operatorname{PSL}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}}/n{\mathbb{Z}})\right)]
is given. If
n>2
this differs by a factor
2^{-1}
with difference coming from the central matrix
-1
.

[
50
, p 146]
):

(39)

[\operatorname{SL}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}}):\ker\left(\operatorname{SL}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})\rightarrow\operatorname{SL}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}}/n{\mathbb{Z}})\right)]=n^{3}\prod\limits_{p\,\mid\,n}(1-\frac{1}{p^{2}}),

where the product runs over all the prime divisors
p
of
n
.
Consequently,
[\Gamma(2^{i}):\Gamma(2^{i+1})]=\frac{[\operatorname{SL}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}}):\Gamma(2^{i+1})]}{[\operatorname{SL}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}}):\Gamma(2^{i})]}=8
for all
i\geq 1
.
Further note that
\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&2^{i}\\
0&1\end{array}\right)\in\Gamma(2^{i})\setminus\phi(U_{i})
for all
i
. Therefore, if we prove that
A.B\in\phi(U_{i})

for any pair of matrices

A=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1+2^{i}a_{11}&2^{i}a_{12}\\
2^{i}a_{21}&1+2^{i}a_{22}\end{array}\right),B=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1+2^{i}b_{11}&2^{i}b_{12}\\
2^{i}b_{21}&1+2^{i}b_{22}\end{array}\right)\in\Gamma(2^{i})\setminus\phi(U_{i})
,
then
[\Gamma(2^{i}):\phi(U_{i})]=2
, which is the first part of the claim
37
37

37

A concrete matrix in
\phi(U_{i})
but not in

\Gamma(2^{i+1})
is
\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1+2^{i}&2^{i}\\
-2^{i}&1-2^{i}\end{array}\right)
.
. Subsequently, the second part follows from this and our value of the index
[\Gamma(2^{i}):\Gamma(2^{i+1})].
A direct computation shows that
AB\in\phi(U_{i})
if and only if
a_{11}+a_{22}\equiv_{2}b_{11}+b_{22}

and
a_{12}+a_{21}\equiv_{2}b_{12}+b_{21}
. However it also is easy to prove that
A
having determinant
1
already
implies that
a_{11}\equiv_{2}a_{22}
(analogously for
B
). Hence if
A\in\Gamma(2^{i})\setminus\phi(U_{i})
then

a_{12}+a_{21}
is odd and the same for
b_{12}+b_{21}
. All together the conditions for
AB\in\phi(U_{i})
are always
satisfied, as needed. The second part of the statement now follows from the claim as follows:

[U_{i}:U_{i+1}]=[\phi(U_{i}):\phi(U_{i+1})]=[\phi(U_{i}):\Gamma(2^{i+1})]\,.\,[\Gamma(2^{i+1}):\phi(U_{i+1})]=8.

Finally we prove the last part of the result. By the above,
U_{2}
is a torsion-free subgroup of
index
8
in
U_{1}=\langle v^{2},w^{2},wv^{-1}\rangle\times\langle-1\rangle
.
Note that an element
-1.(1+2u)
with
u\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{\alpha}[\langle g,h\rangle]
can not
be of the form
1+4v
with
v\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{\alpha}[\langle g,h\rangle]
,
as seen by working modulo
4
. Thus
U_{2}
is a subgroup of index

4
in
F_{3}=\langle v^{2},w^{2},wv^{-1}\rangle.

Thus the Nielsen-Schreier formula yields that
U_{2}\cong F_{1+4\cdot 2}
. Since
U_{i}\leq U_{i-1}

also
U_{i}
is a free group whose rank is computed with a recursive use of
Nielsen-Schreier’s formula.
∎

Remark 8.4
.

Consider
\mathcal{F}=\{\widehat{u_{g}}=\frac{1}{o(g)}\widetilde{u_{g}}\mid g\in\mathcal{G}_{0}^{\alpha}\}
. The computations in the previous proof show
that
v^{2},w^{2},wv^{-1}
are in the group
\text{GBic}^{\mathcal{F}}({\mathbb{Q}}^{\alpha}[\langle g,h\rangle],{\mathbb{Z}})
generated by the (generalized) bicyclic units (see
Definition

6.2
). Actually these two groups are equal (as can for example be checked by expressing the generating bicyclic units in terms of
v^{2},w^{2},wv^{-1}
).
Hence
{\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}^{\alpha}[C_{2}\times C_{2}])
is an
example where

\text{GBic}^{\mathcal{F}}({\mathbb{Q}}^{\alpha}[G],{\mathbb{Z}})\lneq\mathcal{H}_{\alpha}(G).

That the difference is still of finite index heavily relies on
the fact that the generators of
\mathcal{H}_{\alpha}(G)
are all formed from elements of order
2
(which allowed
wv^{-1}
to be a generalized bicyclic unit). In larger examples however the
H
-units can be an infinite index overgroup, as will be shown in
Section

10.3
.

9.
Description of
\mathcal{U}({\mathbb{Z}}[D_{8}\times C_{2}^{n}])

Let
G
be the group
C_{2}^{n+2}=\langle g,h,x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\rangle
as in (
33
) and

\Gamma\cong D_{8}\times C_{2}\times\ldots\times C_{2}=\langle a,b\rangle\times\langle y_{1}\rangle\times\ldots\times\langle y_{n}\rangle.

where
D_{8}=\langle a,b\mid a^{4}=1=b^{2},a^{b}=a^{-1}\rangle
. We consider the
epimorphism
\lambda:D_{8}\times C_{2}^{n}\rightarrow G
determined by

\lambda(a)=gh,\lambda(b)=g,\lambda(y_{i})=x_{i}
. With the associated canonical
choice of section
\mu:G\rightarrow D_{8}\times C_{2}^{n}
, one directly checks that
the associated
2
-cocycle
\alpha(s,t)=\mu(s)\mu(t)\mu(st)^{-1}
is exactly the one determined by (
34
). In other words, the central extension
\Gamma
associated with the
[\alpha]\in H^{2}(G,C_{2})
from
Section

8
is isomorphic to
D_{8}\times C_{2}^{n}
.

Taking now
\chi\in\operatorname{Hom}(\langle a^{2}\rangle,{\mathbb{Z}}^{*})
with
\chi(a^{2})=-1
we see that
\Psi_{\chi}:{\mathbb{Z}}[\Gamma]\rightarrow{\mathbb{Z}}^{\operatorname{Tra}(\chi)}[G]
is nothing else than the ring epimorphism.

(40)

\psi:{\mathbb{Z}}[D_{8}\times C_{2}^{n}]\rightarrow{\mathbb{Z}}^{\alpha}[G]

defined by

a\mapsto u_{gh},\quad b\mapsto u_{g},\quad x_{i}\mapsto u_{y_{i}}.

In this section we will pullback the description of
\mathcal{U}({\mathbb{Z}}^{\alpha}[G])
, obtained in section
Section

8
, by using the methods from
Section

5.2
in order to obtain a description of
\mathcal{U}({\mathbb{Z}}[D_{8}\times C_{2}^{n}])
. First we invoke
Theorem

5.5
and
Theorem

5.3
to give a precise description of the (co)kernel of the (co)restricted morphism
\widetilde{\psi}_{res}:\mathcal{U}({\mathbb{Z}}[D_{8}])\rightarrow\mathcal{U}({\mathbb{Z}}^{\rm Res(\alpha)}[\langle g,h\rangle]):a\mapsto u_{gh},b\mapsto u_{g}
.

Lemma 9.1
.

With notations as above we have that

(i)

\ker(\widetilde{\psi})=\langle-a^{2}\rangle\cong C_{2},

(ii)

|\operatorname{coker}(\widetilde{\psi}_{res})|=2.

Proof.

The description of the kernel follows from
Theorem

5.5
which says that the kernel is finite and
\ker(\widetilde{\psi})=\{1,-a^{2}\}
, as desired.

In order to describe
\operatorname{coker}(\widetilde{\psi}_{res})
, recall that by
Proposition

8.2

\mathcal{U}({\mathbb{Z}}^{\rm Res(\alpha)}[C_{2}\times C_{2}])=\langle v,w\rangle\rtimes D_{8}

with
v=u_{1}+u_{h}\widetilde{u_{g}}
and
w=u_{1}+\widetilde{u_{g}}u_{h}.
As was claimed (without providing details)
in
Remark

6.14
, in this case the product of any two generators of

\mathcal{U}({\mathbb{Z}}^{\rm Res(\alpha)}[\langle g,h\rangle])
belongs to
{\rm Im}(\widetilde{\psi}_{res})
.
Furthermore
Proposition

6.12
asserts that

v
is a non-trivial element in
\operatorname{coker}(\widetilde{\psi}_{res})
,
hence
\widetilde{\psi}_{res}
is not surjective and thus
|\operatorname{coker}(\widetilde{\psi}_{res})|=2
.

To prove the above stated claim on the product of generators, note first that the trivial units are attained. So we need to consider now the torsion-free part. For this consider the bicyclic units
b_{1}=1+(1-b)ab(1+b)
and
b_{2}=1+(1+b)ab(1-b)
. Recalling (
26
) we see that
\psi(b_{1})=v^{2}
and
\psi(b_{2})=w^{2}
. A direct computation also gives that

wv^{-1}=-u_{1}-2u_{g}-2u_{h}u_{g}=-1(u_{1}+(1+u_{h})u_{g}(1-u_{h}))

Therefore if we consider the bicyclic unit
b_{3}=1-(1-ab)b(1+ab)
, we also notice that
\psi(-b_{3})=wv^{-1}
. Thus the claim follows and it finishes the proof.
∎

Lemma

9.1
combined with
Proposition

8.3
and
Theorem

7.3
yield the description we were looking for.

Proposition 9.2
.

The following hold:

(1)

\mathcal{U}({\mathbb{Z}}[D_{8}])\cong F_{3}\rtimes(\pm D_{8})
with
F_{3}
generated by bicyclic units,

(2)

U_{1}\cong F_{9}\times C_{2}
,

(3)

U_{i}\cong F_{n_{i}}
with
n_{i}=1+8^{i}
for
i\geq 2
,

(4)

\pm D_{8}\times C_{2}^{n}
has a torsion-free normal complement in
\mathcal{U}({\mathbb{Z}}[D_{8}\times C_{2}^{n}])
.

This result for example yields that
{\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}[D_{8}\times C_{2}])\cong\left(F_{9}\rtimes F_{3}\right)\rtimes(\pm D_{8}\times C_{2}).
Such a decomposition was already obtained in
[
32
, Theorem 5]
.

Proof.

To start note that
\psi(\pm D_{8})=\langle g,h\rangle\cong D_{8}
. Also it is not hard to see
that the elements
b_{1},b_{2},b_{3}
(used in the proof of

Lemma

9.1
) are in fact generators for

\langle\text{Bic}(D_{8})\rangle
, the group generated by the bicyclic units of
D_{8}

(e.g. see
[
26
, part (3) of proof Example 1.5.4 ]
). Hence we already know that
