\langle\text{Bic}(D_{8}),a^{2}\rangle\cong\langle\text{Bic}(D_{8})\rangle\times\langle a^{2}\rangle\cong\pm 1.\langle v^{2},w^{2},wv^{-1}\rangle=U_{1}(\mathbb{Z}^{\alpha}[C_{2}\times C_{2}])

with
U_{i}(\mathbb{Z}^{\alpha}[C_{2}\times C_{2}]):=\{1+2^{i}u\mid u\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{\alpha}[\langle g,h\rangle]\}\cap\mathcal{U}({\mathbb{Z}}^{\alpha}[\langle g,h\rangle])

and where the second isomorphism is given by
\widetilde{\psi}_{res}
.
The latter is isomorphic to
F_{3}\times C_{2}
by
Proposition

8.3
.

Now, via
Proposition

8.3
and
Lemma

9.1

one infers that
\ker(\widetilde{\psi})\subset\langle\text{Bic}(D_{8}),\pm D_{8}\rangle

and that
\widetilde{\psi}(\langle\text{Bic}(D_{8}),\pm D_{8}\rangle)
is of index two in

{\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}^{\alpha}[C_{2}\times C_{2}])
, thus equals
\im(\widetilde{\psi})
. Consequently, the first part
follows and we have given a new proof of the well known fact that

{\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}[D_{8}])=\langle\text{Bic}(D_{8})\rangle\rtimes(\pm D_{8})
.
In particular all elements in
\langle\text{Bic}(D_{8})\rangle
are of the form

1+(1-a^{2})u
with
u\in{\mathbb{Z}}[D_{8}]
.
This in particular implies that for all
i

(41)

U_{i}({\mathbb{Z}}D_{8})=\{1+2^{i}(1-a^{2})u\mid u\in{\mathbb{Z}}[D_{8}]\}\cap\mathcal{U}({\mathbb{Z}}[D_{8}])

Next, due to the above, we see that for each
i
the torsion-free elements of
U_{i}({\mathbb{Z}}D_{8})
are in

\langle\text{Bic}(D_{8})\rangle
. In particular, for each
i\geq 2
this means that

U_{i}({\mathbb{Z}}D_{8})\leq\langle\text{Bic}(D_{8})\rangle
and
\widetilde{\psi}
is injective on
them by
Lemma

9.1
. Using the description (
41
) we moreover have that
\widetilde{\psi}(U_{i}({\mathbb{Z}}D_{8}))=U_{i+1}({\mathbb{Z}}^{\alpha}[C_{2}\times C_{2}]).

In summary,
\widetilde{\psi}
induces an isomorphism

(42)

U_{i}({\mathbb{Z}}D_{8})\cong U_{i+1}({\mathbb{Z}}^{\alpha}[C_{2}\times C_{2}]).

for all
i\geq 2
. If
i=1
then by
Theorem

7.3
the group
U_{1}({\mathbb{Z}}D_{8})\cong\{\pm 1\}\times N_{1}
with
N_{1}
torsion-free. By the above
N_{1}\leq\langle\text{Bic}(D_{8})\rangle
and concretely
N_{1}=\{1+2(1-a^{2})u\mid u\in{\mathbb{Z}}[D_{8}]\}
.
So via
\widetilde{\psi}
we have the isomorphism
N_{1}\cong U_{2}({\mathbb{Z}}^{\alpha}[C_{2}\times C_{2}])
. Therefore the second and third statement now follows from
Proposition

8.3
. The last statement follows from the first and
Corollary

7.4
.
∎

As mentioned after
Remark

7.5
, it is known that the group generated by the bicyclic units form a normal complement in
{\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}[D_{8}\times C_{2}^{n}])
if
n\leq 1
, but for
n=2
they do not form a normal complement as shown in
[
40
]
. In fact, it is expected that if
n\geq 2
the bicyclic units are even of infinite index. It would be especially instructive to describe a minimal set of generators (in a generic way) of the torsion-free normal complement mentioned
Proposition

9.2
.

To finish this section, we would like to record the exact size and generators of
\operatorname{coker}(\widetilde{\psi})
.

Lemma 9.3
.

With notations as above we have that

(1)

|\operatorname{coker}(\widetilde{\psi})|=[\mathcal{U}({\mathbb{Z}}^{\alpha}[G]):{\rm Im}(\widetilde{\psi})]=2^{n+3}-4n-6,

(2)

\operatorname{coker}(\widetilde{\psi})
is generated by
H
-units.

Proof.

By
Theorem

7.3
, each of the unit groups

{\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}[\Gamma])
and
{\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}^{\alpha}[G])
is determined by respective groups
K_{\vec{j}}.

To distinguish, we respectively write
K_{\vec{j}}^{(i)}(\Gamma)
and
K_{\vec{j}}^{(i)}(G)

for
\vec{j}\in\{0,1\}^{i-1}.
The methods in
Section

7

imply that

|\operatorname{coker}(\widetilde{\psi})|=|\operatorname{coker}(\widetilde{\psi}_{res})|+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{\vec{j}\in\{0,1\}^{i-1}}[K_{\vec{j}}^{(i)}(G):\widetilde{\psi}(K_{\vec{j}}^{(i)}(\Gamma))].

Following
Lemma

9.1
,
|\operatorname{coker}(\widetilde{\psi}_{res})|=2
.
Next, to compute
[K_{\vec{j}}^{(i)}(G):\widetilde{\psi}(K_{\vec{j}}^{(i)}(\Gamma))]
we use (see
Theorem

7.3
) the
isomorphism
K_{\vec{j}}^{(i)}(G)\cong U_{1+\sum_{t=1}^{i-1}j(t)}
which we will denote
by
\theta
(where we abuse notations as it in fact depends on
i
and
\vec{j}
).
For notational
simplicity denote
\sum\vec{j}:=\sum_{t=1}^{i-1}j(t)
. Note that
\theta(\widetilde{\psi}(K_{\vec{j}}^{(i)}(\Gamma)))\cong\widetilde{\psi}_{res}(\theta(K_{\vec{j}}^{(i)}(\Gamma))).
Therefore, if

\vec{j}\neq\vec{0}
, using (
42
) and
Proposition

8.3

yields

[K_{\vec{j}}^{(i)}(G):\widetilde{\psi}(K_{\vec{j}}^{(i)}(\Gamma))]=[\theta\left(K_{\vec{j}}^{(i)}(G)\right):\theta\left(\widetilde{\psi}(K_{\vec{j}}^{(i)}(\Gamma))\right)]=[U_{1+\sum\vec{j}}\,:\,U_{2+\sum\vec{j}}]=8.

If
\vec{j}=\vec{0}
, then both
K_{\vec{j}}^{(i)}(G)
and
K_{\vec{j}}^{(i)}(\Gamma)

contain a copy of
C_{2}
(namely
\langle u_{y_{i}}\rangle
, resp.
\langle x_{i}\rangle
) and

\widetilde{\psi}
induces an isomorphism on these. Thus in this the same
passage through the above isomorphisms yields that
[K_{\vec{j}}^{(i)}(G):\widetilde{\psi}(K_{\vec{j}}^{(i)}(\Gamma))]=[\langle v,w,vw^{-1}\rangle:U_{2}]=4
. So all together

|\operatorname{coker}(\widetilde{\psi})|=2+4n+8\sum_{i=1}^{n}(2^{i-1}-1)=2+4n-8n+8(2^{n}-1)=2^{n+3}-4n-6.

For the second part of the statement, using the elements in
\mathcal{H}_{\alpha}(C_{2}\times C_{2})

we will give explicit generators of the quotient
K_{\vec{j}}^{(i)}(G)/\widetilde{\psi}(K_{\vec{j}}^{(i)}(\Gamma))
. First recall that by
Proposition

8.3

U_{1+\sum\vec{j}}/U_{2+\sum\vec{j}}
is of order
8
if
\vec{j}\neq\vec{0}
and
4
else. Now consider the set

(43)

\{v^{2^{1+\Sigma\vec{j}}},(vw^{-1})^{2^{\Sigma\vec{j}}},v^{2^{\Sigma\vec{j}}}w^{-2^{\Sigma\vec{j}}}\}.

Note that the second and third generators are equal if
\vec{j}=\vec{0}
. Computing their
image under the isomorphism
\phi
from the proof of
Proposition

8.3
, and
also of all their double products, one deduces that they generate an elementary abelian
2
-group
of order
8
if
\vec{j}\neq\vec{0}
and of order
4
else. In particular, for all
\vec{j}

their images generate
\phi(U_{1+\sum\vec{j}})/\phi(U_{2+\sum\vec{j}})
and so the set
itself generates
U_{1+\sum\vec{j}}/U_{2+\sum\vec{j}}
. It now remains to construct
three
H
-units in
K_{\vec{j}}^{(i)}(G)
which map under
\theta
to the elements in (
43
).
For notation ease we denote
(1-u_{x})^{\vec{j}}=(1-u_{x_{1}})^{j(1)}\ldots(1-u_{x_{i-1}})^{j(i-1)}
. We claim that the desired units are:

\begin{array}[]{l}z^{(i)}_{\vec{j},1}=1+(1-u_{x_{i}})(1-u_{x})^{\vec{j}}\,u_{h}\widetilde{u_{g}},\\
\\
z^{(i)}_{\vec{j},2}=1-(1-u_{x_{i}})(1-u_{x})^{\vec{j}}\,\widetilde{u_{h}}u_{g},\\
\\
z^{(i)}_{\vec{j},3}=\left(1+(1-u_{x})^{\vec{j}}\,u_{h}\widetilde{u_{g}}\right)\left(1-(1-u_{x})^{\vec{j}}\,u_{h}\widetilde{(u_{x_{i}}u_{g})}\right).\end{array}

Writing out the third element results in

z^{(i)}_{\vec{j},3}=1+(1-u_{x})^{\vec{j}}u_{h}u_{g}(1-u_{x_{i}})-2^{\Sigma\vec{j}}(1-u_{x})^{\vec{j}}(1-u_{g})(1-u_{x_{i}}).

Thus we see that
\{z^{(i)}_{\vec{j},1},z^{(i)}_{\vec{j},2},z^{(i)}_{\vec{j},3}\}\subset K_{\vec{j}}^{(i)}(G)
and clearly

\theta(z^{(i)}_{\vec{j},1})=v^{2^{1+\Sigma\vec{j}}},\,\,\theta(z^{(i)}_{\vec{j},2})=(vw^{-1})^{2^{\Sigma\vec{j}}},\,\,\theta(z^{(i)}_{\vec{j},3})=v^{2^{\Sigma\vec{j}}}w^{-2^{\Sigma\vec{j}}}.

This finishes the proof of the second statement.
∎

10.
A new generic construction of units in integral group rings

In this section we introduce a new generic construction of units in
{\mathbb{Z}}G
which in fact are elements of

\operatorname{SL}_{1}({\mathbb{Z}}G)
(see (
25
)). These elements originated as the pullback of the products of the

H
-units in twisted group rings from
Definition

6.9
along the transgression morphisms
of
Section

3.2
. However, they can also be defined directly as will be shown in
Section

10.2
. Furthermore, as explained in
Section

10.1
, those units of
Definition

6.9

can be constructed in general finite dimensional semisimple
F
-algebras, with
F
a number field. Finally,
in
Section

10.3
, we will give an infinite family of groups where the newly
constructed units contain the bicyclic units as subgroup of infinite index. In particular, these elements
are indeed a new step towards the problem of describing generators of
{\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}G)
, up to commensurability,
generically in
{\mathbb{Z}}G
. Interestingly, these units will also yield the first generic construction of free
groups of rank larger than
2
, see
Theorem

10.6
.

Notation.
Recall that
\tilde{H}=\sum\limits_{h\in H}h
for any finite subgroup
H
in an algebra
A
and
\widehat{H}=\frac{1}{|H|}\tilde{H}
. If
A
is semisimple we will denote by
\operatorname{PCI}(A)
the set of primitive central idempotents of
A

10.1.
Restricted construction of units for orders in general semisimple algebras

Let
A
be a finite dimensional semisimple
F
-algebra, with
F
a number field, and let
\mathcal{O}
be a
{\mathbb{Z}}
-order
in
A
. Inspired by
Definition

6.9
and
Remark

6.10

we define the following.

Definition 10.1
.

Let
x,t\in{\mathcal{U}}(\mathcal{O})
be torsion units such that
[t,x]\in N_{{\mathcal{U}}(\mathcal{O})}(\langle t\rangle)
is of finite order and
\widetilde{[t,x]}=0
. Then the elements

u_{x^{\pm 1},\widetilde{t}}:=1+x^{\pm 1}\widetilde{t}\mbox{ and }u_{\widetilde{t},x^{\pm 1}}:=1+\widetilde{t}x^{\pm 1}

will be called
primitive
H
-units.

As the name suggests, the elements in
Definition

10.1
are indeed invertible elements in
{\mathcal{U}}(\mathcal{O})
,
as proven below. The reason for adding, compared to
Definition

6.9
, primitive in the name
will be clarified later on in
Remark

10.10
.

Theorem 10.2
.

Let
x,t\in{\mathcal{U}}(\mathcal{O})
be as in
definition

10.1
. Then
u_{x^{\pm 1},\widetilde{t}}
and
u_{\widetilde{t},x^{\pm 1}}
are unipotent units in
{\mathcal{U}}(\mathcal{O})
. In particular they are of infinite order.

We will prove that
\left(x^{\pm}\widetilde{t}\right)^{2}=0
, hence the inverse of
u_{x^{\pm 1},\widetilde{t}}
is
1-x^{\pm 1}\widetilde{t}
. Similarly for
u_{\widetilde{t},x^{\pm 1}}
.

Example 10.3
.

The archetypical example is a tuple
(x,t)
of torsion units such that
[t,x]\in F^{*}
is a root of unity. In that case
[t,x]
is both central and
\widetilde{[t,x]}=0
. If
A=R^{\alpha}[G]
is some twisted group ring and
x,t\in G
, then the linear independence of the basis elements yields that
u_{x^{\pm 1},\widetilde{t}}
and
u_{\widetilde{t},x^{\pm 1}}
are non-trivial (i.e.
\neq 1
) if
\tilde{x}\neq 0
in
A
. However, in general, there is no transparent characterisation of being non-trivial.

In order to prove
Theorem

10.2
we need the following lemma which was shared to us by Ángel del Río and whose proof depends on useful identities from
[
9
]
.

Lemma 10.4
.

Let
G=\langle g,a\rangle
be a finite meta-cyclic group with
\langle g\rangle
a normal subgroup such that
o(g)=o(ga)
. Then
o(a)\mid o(g).

Proof.

Denote
m=o(g)
,
n=[G:\langle g\rangle]=\frac{|G|}{m}
and
s=[G:\langle a\rangle]=[\langle g\rangle:\langle g\rangle\cap\langle a\rangle]
.
Then
s
divides
m
and
o(a)=\frac{|G|}{s}=\frac{mn}{s}
.

For a prime
p
denote by
v_{p}(k)
the
p
-adic valuation of
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
. Suppose that
o(a)=\frac{nm}{s}
does not divide
m=o(g)
, or equivalently
n
does not divide
s
, i.e.
v_{p}(n)>v_{p}(s)
for some prime
p
.

Let
i
be the smallest non-negative integer such that
a^{-1}ga=g^{i}
and
1\leq t\leq m
such that
a^{n}=g^{t}
.
Note that
g=a^{-n}ga^{n}=g^{i^{n}}
and so
i^{n}\equiv 1\mod m
. Next, by using formula
[
9
, eq. (2.2)]

(notice that in loc. cit. the role of
a
and
g
are interchanged) we have
(ag)^{n}=a^{n}g^{S(i|n)}=g^{t+S(i|n)}

with
S(i|n):=\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}i^{j}
. Then, by the assumption,
m=o(g)=o(g^{-1}(ga)g)=o(ag)=n\frac{m}{gcd(m,t+S(i|n))}
and
hence
n=gcd(m,t+S(i|n))
. In particular
n
divides both
t+S(i|n)
and
m
.
Also,
\frac{nm}{s}=o(a)=n.o(g^{t})=\frac{nm}{\gcd(m,t)}
. Therefore
s=\gcd(m,t)
. All together we deduce that

v_{p}(s)=\min\{v_{p}(t),v_{p}(m)\}<v_{p}(n)=\min\{v_{p}(m),v_{p}(t+S(i|n))\}.

This entails that
v_{p}(t)=v_{p}(s)<v_{p}(n)
. Consequently also
p\mid m
and
v_{p}(t)<v_{p}(t+S(i|n)).
In particular
v_{p}(t)=v_{p}(t+S(i|n)-t)=v_{p}(S(i|n))
, hence
v_{p}(S(i|n))<v_{p}(n)
.

Now let
q
be the multiplicative order of
i
modulo
p,
i.e. the smallest integer
q
with
p\mid i^{q}-1.

Since
i^{n}\equiv 1\mod m
and
p
divides
m
, it follows that
q
divides
n.

Moreover,
q
divides
p-1.
Hence
v_{p}(n)=v_{p}(\frac{n}{q})\geq 1.

From
[
9
, Lemma 8.2]
it follows that in case
p
is odd or
p=2
and
i^{q}\equiv 1\mod 4
, we have
v_{p}(S(i^{q}|\frac{n}{q}))=v_{p}(\frac{n}{q})
. Otherwise
p=2,v_{p}(i^{q}+1)\geq 2
and

v_{2}(S(i^{q}|\frac{n}{q}))=v_{2}(\frac{n}{q})+v_{2}(i^{q}+1)-1>v_{2}(\frac{n}{q}).

So all together,
v_{p}(S(i^{q}|\frac{n}{q}))\geq v_{p}(\frac{n}{q}).

Moreover, by
[
9
, Lemma 8.1]
,
S(i|n)=S(i|q)S(i^{q}|\frac{n}{q})
.
Thus,
v_{p}(\frac{n}{q})=v_{p}(n)>v_{p}(S(i|n))=v_{p}(S(i|q))+v_{p}(S(i^{q}|\frac{n}{q}))\geq v_{p}(\frac{n}{q}),
which is clearly a contradiction. The contradiction came from the assumption that
v_{p}(n)>v_{p}(s)
for some prime
p
. Hence we conclude that
v_{p}(n)\leq v_{p}(s)
for all primes
p
, that is,
n
divides
s
and hence
o(a)\mid o(g).

∎

We can now proceed to the proof of the theorem.

Proof of
Theorem

10.2
.

Assume
x,t\in{\mathcal{U}}(\mathcal{O})
are torsion units such that
[t,x]\in N_{{\mathcal{U}}(\mathcal{O})}(\langle t\rangle)
is of finite order and
\widetilde{[t,x]}=0
.
For simplicity of notation, denote
[t,x]=a.
Since
x^{-1}tx=ta
we have that
o(t)=o(ta)
. Thus
G=\langle a,t\rangle
satisfies the conditions of
Lemma

10.4
and hence
o(a)\mid o(t)
.

Next, recall the notation
S(s\mid n):=\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}s^{j}
for any numbers
s,n\in{\mathbb{N}}
. Since
a\in N_{{\mathcal{U}}(\mathcal{O})}(\langle t\rangle)
there is some
i\neq 0
such that
ta=at^{i}
. In other words,
tx=xat^{i}
and
x^{-1}t=at^{i}x^{-1}.
Via induction one directly obtains that

(44)

x^{-1}t^{k}=a^{k}t^{iS(i|k)}x^{-1}\mbox{ and }t^{k}x=xa^{k}t^{iS(i|k)}.

These formulas to swap
t
and
x
enable us to compute
\widetilde{t}x^{\pm 1}\widetilde{t}.
We claim that:

\widetilde{t}x^{-1}\widetilde{t}=\frac{o(t)}{o(a)}\widetilde{a}\,\widetilde{t}\,x^{-1}\mbox{ and }\widetilde{t}x\widetilde{t}=\frac{o(t)}{o(a)}x\,\widetilde{t}\,\widetilde{a}.

In order to prove this claim, denote
\frac{o(t)}{o(a)}=m\in{\mathbb{N}}
and rewrite
\widetilde{t}=\sum_{\ell=0}^{m-1}t^{\ell\cdot o(a)}\left(\sum_{j=0}^{o(a)-1}t^{j}\right).
Then, using the rule in (
44
) and that
a\widetilde{t}=\widetilde{t}a
, because
a
normalizes
\langle t\rangle
, we obtain that

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\widetilde{t}x\widetilde{t}&=&x\,\Big(\sum\limits_{\ell=0}^{m-1}a^{l\,o(a)}t^{iS(i\mid l\,o(a))}\big(\sum\limits_{j=0}^{o(a)-1}a^{j}t^{iS(i\mid j)}\big)\Big)\,\widetilde{t}\\
&=&x\left(\sum\limits_{\ell=0}^{m-1}t^{iS(i\mid l\,o(a))}\big(\sum\limits_{j=0}^{o(a)-1}a^{j}\widetilde{t}\,\big)\right)\\
&=&x\,\widetilde{t}\,(\sum\limits_{\ell=0}^{m-1}\widetilde{a}).\end{array}

In the second and last equality we have used that
\widetilde{t}t^{j}=\widetilde{t}
for every
j
.
The expression for
\widetilde{t}x^{-1}\widetilde{t}
is computed analogously. Now, since by assumption
\widetilde{a}=0
,
we obtain that
\left(x^{\pm 1}\widetilde{t}\right)^{2}=0=(\widetilde{t}x^{\pm 1})^{2}.
Consequently,

u_{x^{\pm 1},\widetilde{t}}^{n}=1+nx\widetilde{t}
for all
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}
. In particular it is invertible with
inverse
1-x^{\pm 1}\widetilde{t}
and has infinite order. Similarly for
u_{\widetilde{t},x^{\pm 1}}.

∎

10.2.
General construction and properties of H-units

In this section we start with constructing a class of units in
\operatorname{SL}_{1}({\mathbb{Z}}G)
from any triple
(g,h,Q)
satisfying
the properties mentioned in the definition below. A first new feature of these, that we will obtain in
Theorem

10.6
,
is that they produce free groups of large rank. Furthermore, as shown later in
Section

10.3
,
the group consisting of these units can contain the bicyclic units as subgroup of infinite index and hence also up to commensurability they are new.

Definition 10.5
.

Let
G
be a finite group and
(g,h,Q)
a triple satisfying

•

Q
is a normal subgroup in
\langle g,h,Q\rangle
,

•

[g,h]Q\in\mathcal{Z}(\langle g,h,Q\rangle/Q)
and
o(hQ)=2
.

Denote
\widetilde{(\pm g)}_{Q}:=\sum\limits_{i=0}^{o(gQ)-1}(\pm g)^{i}
and for any tuple
(x_{1},x_{2},y_{1},y_{2})\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}^{4}
define the element

(45)

v_{(x_{1},x_{2},y_{1},y_{2})}:=1+\frac{1}{2|Q|}\widetilde{Q}(1-[g,h])\Big(h\,[x_{1}\,\widetilde{(-g)_{Q}}+x_{2}\,\widetilde{g}_{Q}]+y_{1}\,\widetilde{(-g)}_{Q}+y_{2}\,\widetilde{g}_{Q}\Big).

A quadruple
(x_{1},x_{2},y_{1},y_{2})\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}^{4}
will be called
admissible for
(g,h,Q)
if

(46)

y_{1}+y_{2}+o(gQ)\,y_{1}y_{2}=o(gQ)\,x_{1}x_{2}\mbox{ and }x_{2}\pm x_{1}\equiv 0\equiv y_{2}\pm y_{1}\mod 2|Q|.

The elements
v_{(x_{1},x_{2},y_{1},y_{2})}
for admissible
(x_{1},x_{2},y_{1},y_{2})
will be called
an H-unit
.

The first condition to be admissible will exactly correspond to being invertible (more precisely,
to belong to
\operatorname{SL}_{1}({\mathbb{Z}}G)
) and the second condition yields that the element is in
{\mathbb{Z}}G
.

For a fixed triple
(g,h,Q)
as in Definition
10.5
, the set of elements of the form (
45
) for admissible quadruples (
46
)
will be denoted
\mathcal{H}(g,h,Q)
and

(47)

\mathcal{H}(G)=\langle\mathcal{H}(g,h,Q)\mid(g,h,Q)\mbox{ as in \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{def h-units} }\rangle

is the group generated by all
H
-units.

Among others, the next result says that
\mathcal{H}(g,h,Q)
is not only a set, but even a subgroup of the group of reduced norm
1
elements.

Theorem 10.6
.

Let
(g,h,Q)
be a triple as in
Definition

10.5
. Then,

(1)

\mathcal{H}(g,h,Q)
is a finitely generated subgroup of
\operatorname{SL}_{1}({\mathbb{Z}}G)
and
v_{(x_{1},x_{2},y_{1},y_{2})}^{-1}=v_{(-x_{1},-x_{2},y_{2},y_{1})},

(2)

\mathcal{H}(g,h,Q)\neq 1
if and only if
[g,h]\notin\langle g\rangle Q
.

Moreover, for
\mathcal{H}(g,h,Q)\neq 1
,

(3)

if
38
38

38

Since we assume
\mathcal{H}(g,h,Q)
to be non-trivial, by part (2),
o(gQ)|Q|=2
if and only if
Q=1
and
o(g)=2
.

o(gQ)|Q|=2
, then
\mathcal{H}(g,h,Q)\cong F_{3}\times C_{2}
, and

(4)

if
o(gQ)|Q|>2
, then
\mathcal{H}(g,h,Q)\cong F_{n}
with
n=1+\frac{(o(gQ)|Q|)^{3}}{6}\prod_{p}(1-\frac{1}{p^{2}})
, where the product runs over the prime divisors
p
of
o(gQ)|Q|
.

As
\mathcal{H}(g,h,Q)
is finitely generated, one only needs to construct
v_{(x_{1},x_{2},y_{1},y_{2})}
for a finite
number of admissible
(x_{1},x_{2},y_{1},y_{2})
. We have not tried to give a precise upperbound, but in principle this could be done.
In fact the generators should somehow correspond to the ‘minimal solutions’ of the equations in (
46
).

For any
a,c\in G
, with
c\notin N_{G}(\langle a\rangle)
, consider the corresponding bicyclic unit
b(a,c)=1+(1-a)c\widetilde{a}
.
It was proven by Marciniak and Sehgal
[
43
]
that
\langle b(a,c),b(a,c)^{*}\rangle\cong F_{2}
, where
b(a,c)^{*}

is the image of
b(a,c)
under the canonical involution of
{\mathbb{Q}}G
. For nilpotent groups
G
a similar statement holds for their
torsion variant
[
19
,
44
]
: the set consisting of the unipotent units belonging to
\operatorname{Bic}(G)
and that form a free product with
b(a,c)\,a
is profinitely dense in
\operatorname{Bic}(G)

[
21
]
.
However,
Theorem

10.6
yields the first generic construction of large free groups (different
from taking artificially a copy of
F_{n}
inside
F_{2}
). See
[
16
]
for a survey on constructing free subgroups
of
{\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}G).

Proof of
Theorem

10.6
.

To start, note that
(-x_{1},-x_{2},y_{2},y_{1})
is admissible if
(x_{1},x_{2},y_{1},y_{2})
is. Moreover, it directly follows from the
x_{2}\pm x_{1}\equiv 0\equiv y_{2}\pm y_{1}\mod 2|Q|
congruences that both elements in the statement are in
{\mathbb{Z}}G
. To prove the statements of the result, we will “locate” precisely the
H
-units inside
{\mathbb{Q}}[\langle g,h,Q\rangle]
.

First we consider when
\mathcal{H}(g,h,Q)
is trivial, i.e. when all elements
v_{(x_{1},x_{2},y_{1},y_{2})}
equal
1
.
Or equivalently, all
\frac{1}{2|Q|}\widetilde{Q}(1-[g,h])\Big(h\,[x_{1}\,\widetilde{(-g)_{Q}}+x_{2}\,\widetilde{g}_{Q}]+y_{1}\,\widetilde{(-g)}_{Q}+y_{2}\,\widetilde{g}_{Q}\Big)=0
.
Since
{\mathbb{Z}}\langle g,h,Q\rangle\frac{\widetilde{Q}}{|Q|}\cong{\mathbb{Z}}(\langle g,h,Q\rangle/Q)
, it easily follows that we may
assume that
Q=1
; and thus
o(h)=2
and
[g,h]\in\mathcal{Z}(\langle g,h\rangle)
. Hence, we need to verify when all

(1-[g,h])\Big(h\,[x_{1}\,\widetilde{(-g)_{Q}}+x_{2}\,\widetilde{g}_{Q}]+y_{1}\,\widetilde{(-g)}_{Q}+y_{2}\,\widetilde{g}_{Q}\Big)=0
.
Of course, if
[g,h]=1
then the latter always holds.
Hence, for the remaining of the proof of part (2) we may also assume that
1\neq[g,h]
. Now, as
o(h)=2
and
1\neq[g,h]\in\mathcal{Z}(\langle g,h\rangle)
one has that
o([g,h])=2
. Furthermore,
o(g)
is even and
g^{2}
is central in
\langle g,h\rangle
.

Now suppose that
[g,h]\in\langle g\rangle
. Write
[g,h]=g^{k}
and thus
h^{-1}gh=g^{k+1}
. As
o(h^{-1}gh)=o(g)
it follows that
k+1
is odd and thus

[g,h]\in\langle g^{2}\rangle
is central. Therefore the required triviality follows from
(1-[g,h])(\widetilde{g}_{Q})=0=(1-[g,h])\widetilde{(-g)_{Q}}
. So we have shown that if
[g,h]\in\langle g\rangle
then
\mathcal{H}(g,h,Q)=1
.
Conversely, assume
\mathcal{H}(g,h,Q)=1
, in particular
v(1,-1,1,-1)=1
and, because
o(g)
is even, we thus get
(1-[g,h])(h+1)(\widetilde{(-g)_{Q}}+\widetilde{g_{Q}})=(1-[g,h])(h+1)\widetilde{(g^{2})_{Q}}=0
. In other words,
(h+1)\widetilde{g^{2}_{Q}}=[g,h](h+1)\widetilde{g^{2}_{Q}}
. Clearly, a support argument in the group ring
{\mathbb{Z}}\langle g,h\rangle
yields that
[g,h]\in\langle g^{2}\rangle
or
[g,h]\in h\langle g^{2}\rangle
. However, the latter is impossible as elements in
h\langle g^{2}\rangle
are not central in
\langle g,h\rangle
. Hence, we have shown that if
\mathcal{H}(g,h,Q)=1
then
[g,h]\in\langle g^{2}\rangle
, and thus part (2) of the result follows.

From now on we may assume that
\mathcal{H}(g,h,Q)\neq 1
. In particular, according to the above
[g,h]\notin\langle g\rangle Q
. Furthermore, as mentioned above, we thus also have that
o([g,h]Q)=2
.

Consider the central idempotent
e:=\widehat{\langle Q,-[g,h]\rangle}
in
{\mathbb{Q}}[\langle g,h,Q\rangle]
and the associated decomposition
{\mathbb{Q}}[\langle g,h,Q\rangle]={\mathbb{Q}}[\langle g,h,Q\rangle](1-e)\oplus{\mathbb{Q}}[\langle g,h,Q\rangle]e
.
Notice that
v_{(x_{1},x_{2},y_{1},y_{2})}(1-e)=1-e
, i.e. the projection on the first part is trivial. Thus we need to prove
the desired statements (1), (3) and (4) within the second component.
For this, put
\overline{g}=gQ
and
\overline{h}=hQ
. As mentioned above,
o(\overline{g})
is even and note that
{\mathbb{Q}}[\langle g,h,Q\rangle]e\cong{\mathbb{Q}}^{\alpha}[\langle\overline{g}\rangle\times\langle\overline{h}\rangle]

with
[\alpha]\in H^{2}(\langle\overline{g}\rangle\times\langle\overline{h}\rangle,{\mathbb{Z}}^{*})
determined by
u_{\overline{g}}^{o(\overline{g})}=1=u_{\overline{h}}^{o(\overline{h})}
and
[u_{\overline{g}},u_{\overline{h}}]=-1
. Under that isomorphism
v_{(x_{1},x_{2},y_{1},y_{2})}
corresponds to the element

1+u_{\overline{h}}\,[x_{1}\,\widetilde{(-u_{\overline{g}})}+x_{2}\,\widetilde{u_{\overline{g}}}]+y_{1}\,\widetilde{(-u_{\overline{g}})}+y_{2}\,\widetilde{u_{\overline{g}}}.

A direct verification yields that
\widetilde{(-u_{\overline{g}})}.\widetilde{u_{\overline{g}}}=0
and

\widetilde{\pm u_{\overline{g}}}^{2}=o(\overline{g})\widetilde{\pm u_{\overline{g}}}
.
Also,
[x_{1}\,\widetilde{(-u_{\overline{g}})}+x_{2}\,\widetilde{u_{\overline{g}}}]\,u_{\overline{h}}=u_{\overline{h}}\,[x_{2}\,\widetilde{(-u_{\overline{g}})}+x_{1}\,\widetilde{u_{\overline{g}}}].
Using all this,
one could straightforwardly compute the image of the product
v_{(x_{1},x_{2},y_{1},y_{2})}\;v_{(-x_{1},-x_{2},y_{2},y_{1})}

and deduce that it is equal to
1
if and only if
y_{1}+y_{2}+o(\overline{g})\,y_{1}y_{2}=o(\overline{g})\,x_{1}x_{2}
.
However, by decomposing
{\mathbb{Q}}^{\alpha}[\langle\overline{g}\rangle\times\langle\overline{h}\rangle]

further one can also give the following conceptual explanation, which moreover also will yield the remainder of the result.

Remark that
\overline{g}^{2}
is central and hence, by
Theorem

3.2
,

{\mathbb{Q}}^{\alpha}[\langle\overline{g}\rangle\times\langle\overline{h}\rangle]\cong\bigoplus\limits_{\chi\in\operatorname{Lin}(\langle\overline{g}^{2}\rangle,{\mathbb{Q}})}{\mathbb{Q}}(\chi)^{\operatorname{Tra}_{\alpha}(\chi)}[C_{2}\times C_{2}]\cong\bigoplus\limits_{d\,\mid\,o(\overline{g}^{2})}{\mathbb{Q}}(\zeta_{d})^{\alpha_{d}}[C_{2}\times C_{2}].

Explicitly, denoting
C_{2}\times C_{2}=\langle a,b\rangle
,
[\alpha_{d}]\in H^{2}(\langle a,b\rangle,\langle\pm\zeta_{d}\rangle)
is determined by
u_{a}^{2}=\zeta_{d},u_{b}^{2}=1
and
u_{a}u_{b}=-u_{b}u_{a}
.
The projections onto the direct summands are given by the transgression maps from
Proposition

3.3
,
hence
u_{\overline{g}}\mapsto u_{a},u_{\overline{h}}\mapsto u_{b}
and
u_{\overline{g}^{2}}\mapsto\zeta_{d}\,u_{1}
.
Next, notice that
\widetilde{(\pm u_{\overline{g}})}=(1\pm u_{\overline{g}})\,.\,\widetilde{(u_{\overline{g}}^{2})}
.
Due to this,
\widetilde{(\pm u_{\overline{g}})}
maps to
0
in every direct summand except for the one indexed by the trivial representation of
\langle\overline{g}^{2}\rangle
.
In other words, there is only a single component of
{\mathbb{Q}}[\langle g,h,Q\rangle]
where
v_{(x_{1},x_{2},y_{1},y_{2})}
has a
non-trivial projection and this component is isomorphic to
{\mathbb{Q}}^{\alpha_{1}}[\langle a,b\rangle]
with
u_{a}^{2}=1=u_{b}^{2}

and
[u_{a},u_{b}]=-1
. In that component the projection of
v_{(x_{1},x_{2},y_{1},y_{2})}
is

1+o(\overline{g}^{2})\big(u_{b}[x_{1}(1-u_{a})+x_{2}(1+u_{a})]+y_{1}(1-u_{a})+y_{2}(1+u_{a})\big).

Restricting to
{\mathbb{Z}}[\langle g,h,Q\rangle]
, we can now compose with the isomorphism
\phi
from
Section

8.1
, defined in (
36
). This composition is defined by

\displaystyle u_{a}\mapsto\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&0\\
0&-1\end{array}\right),\quad u_{b}\mapsto\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&1\\
1&0\end{array}\right),\quad u_{ab}\mapsto\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&1\\
-1&0\end{array}\right).

By doing so we have obtained a ring morphism
\Phi:{\mathbb{Z}}[\langle g,h,Q\rangle]\rightarrow\widetilde{D}
which on
\mathcal{H}(g,h,Q)
is defined as

(48)

\Phi:v_{(x_{1},x_{2},y_{1},y_{2})}\mapsto\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1+2\,o(\overline{g}^{2})\,y_{2}&2o(\overline{g}^{2})\,x_{1}\\
2o(\overline{g}^{2})\,x_{2}&1+2\,o(\overline{g}^{2})\,y_{1}\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1+o(\overline{g})\,y_{2}&o(\overline{g})\,x_{1}\\
o(\overline{g})\,x_{2}&1+o(\overline{g})\,y_{1}\end{array}\right).

Moreover, the elements of
\mathcal{H}(g,h,Q)
being trivial on all the other components,
\Phi
is injective on

\mathcal{H}(g,h,Q)
. In particular, see (
24
),
nr(v_{(x_{1},x_{2},y_{1},y_{2})})=1
if

\det(\Phi(v_{(x_{1},x_{2},y_{1},y_{2})}))=1
.
The latter holds as
\det(\Phi(v_{(x_{1},x_{2},y_{1},y_{2})}))=1+o(\overline{g})(y_{1}+y_{2})+o(\overline{g})^{2}y_{1}y_{2}-o(\overline{g})^{2}x_{1}x_{2}=1
, by the definition of an admissible quadruple.
In fact, since
\Phi
is injective on
\mathcal{H}(g,h,Q)
, the first condition of being admissible is equivalent
to
\det(\Phi(v_{(x_{1},x_{2},y_{1},y_{2})}))=1
.
Now, we also see that the inverse is

\Phi(v_{(x_{1},x_{2},y_{1},y_{2})})^{-1}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1+o(\overline{g})\,y_{1}&-o(\overline{g})\,x_{1}\\
-o(\overline{g})\,x_{2}&1+o(\overline{g})\,y_{2}\end{array}\right).

Hence indeed
v_{(x_{1},x_{2},y_{1},y_{2})}^{-1}=v_{(-x_{1},-x_{2},y_{2},y_{1})}
. In conclusion,
\mathcal{H}(g,h,Q)
is a subgroup of
\operatorname{SL}_{1}({\mathbb{Z}}G)
.

All the above in fact yields more. Namely that

\Phi:\mathcal{H}(g,h,Q)\hookrightarrow\Gamma(o(\overline{g})),

where
\Gamma(n)
is the principal congruence subgroup of level
n
of
\operatorname{SL}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})
. However,
\Phi
is not onto due to the congruences
x_{2}\pm x_{1}\equiv 0\equiv y_{2}\pm y_{1}\mod 2|Q|
.

Claim:

x_{2}\pm x_{1}\equiv 0\equiv y_{2}\pm y_{1}\mod 2|Q|
if and only if
x_{i}=|Q|l_{i},y_{i}=|Q|t_{i}

for
l_{i},t_{i}\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that
l_{1}\equiv l_{2}\mod 2
and
t_{1}\equiv t_{2}\mod 2
.

Indeed, if
x_{2}\pm x_{1}\equiv 0\mod 2|Q|
, then
2x_{1}\equiv 0
and
2x_{2}\equiv 0

\mod 2|Q|
.
Hence
x_{2}\equiv x_{1}\equiv 0\mod|Q|
and consequently
x_{i}=|Q|l_{i}
,
for some
l_{i}\in{\mathbb{N}}
.
Now, as
2|Q|
divides
x_{2}\pm x_{1}
, one must have that
2\mid l_{1}\pm l_{2}
,
as desired. Conversely,
x_{i}
of that form clearly satisfy
x_{2}\pm x_{1}\mod 2|Q|
.
The proof for the
y_{i}
is exactly the same, hence the claim follows.

Now denote

(49)

V_{m}=\left\{\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1+m\,l_{2}&m\,t_{1}\\
m\,t_{2}&1+m\,l_{1}\end{array}\right)\in\operatorname{SL}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})\mid l_{1}\equiv l_{2}\text{ and }t_{1}\equiv t_{2}\mod 2\right\}.

Notice that the groups
U_{i}\cong\phi(U_{i})
from the proof of
Proposition

8.3
are equal to
V_{2^{i}}
. By the claim above

(50)

\Phi(\mathcal{H}(g,h,Q))=V_{o(\overline{g})|Q|}.

Since,
\Gamma(2m)\leq V_{m}\leq\Gamma(m)
for every
m\geq 1
, we have obtained that
\Phi(\mathcal{H}(g,h,Q))
is a finite index subgroup in
\operatorname{SL}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})
. In particular it is finitely generated, finishing the proof of (1).

Finally, to obtain (3) and (4) we recall the well-known fact that
\operatorname{SL}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})\cong C_{4}\star_{\langle-1\rangle}C_{6}
with
C_{4}=\langle\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&-1\\
1&0\\
\end{array}\right)\rangle
and
C_{6}=\langle\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&-1\\
1&-1\\
\end{array}\right)\rangle.
Hence, by Kurosh’s theorem,
\Gamma(m)
is free if and only if it is torsion-free.
Moreover, all periodic subgroups are conjugated to a subgroup of
C_{4}
or
C_{6}
. As
\Gamma(m)
is normal in
\operatorname{SL}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})
, to verify when
\Gamma(m)
is free it is enough to verify explicitly
which powers of the two matrices are in
\Gamma(m)
. By doing so we see that
\Gamma(m)
is torsion-free if

m\neq 2
and
-1
is the only torsion element in
\Gamma(2)
. As a consequence, the same conclusion is valid
for
V_{m}
instead of
\Gamma(m)
. Also recall, e.g. see the proof of
Proposition

8.3
,
that
V_{2}=U_{1}\cong F_{3}\times\langle-1\rangle
. All this applied to
\Phi(\mathcal{H}(g,h,Q))=V_{o(\overline{g})|Q|}

yields that
\mathcal{H}(g,h,Q)
is a finitely generated free group, except if
|Q|=1
and
o(\overline{g})=2
.
In this case
\mathcal{H}(g,h,Q)\cong F_{3}\times C_{2}.

When
m:=o(\overline{g})|Q|>2
the rank of the free group
V_{m}
can be computed. In order to do so, recall that
o(\overline{g})
is even.
In particular
\mathcal{H}(g,h,Q)\cong V_{m}
with
m
even. Next, with the same method as in the proof of
Proposition

8.3
, it can be shown that
[\Gamma(m):V_{m}]=2
when
m
is even. Also, because
V_{m}\leq F_{2}\leq\Gamma(2)=F_{2}\times C_{2}
, we need to compute
[F_{2}:V_{m}]=[\Gamma(2):V_{m}]/2
. This can readily be done using (
39
):

[\Gamma(2):V_{m}]=[\Gamma(2):\Gamma(m)]\;[\Gamma(m):V_{m}]=2\frac{[\operatorname{SL}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}}):\Gamma(m)]}{[\operatorname{SL}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}}):\Gamma(2)]}=2\frac{m^{3}}{6}\prod\limits_{p\mid m}(1-\frac{1}{p^{2}}).

Finally, by Nielsen-Schreier’s formula, we obtain that
V_{m}\cong F_{n}
with
n=1+\frac{m^{3}}{6}\prod\limits_{p\mid m}(1-\frac{1}{p^{2}})
.
∎

In order to make further use of it in the text, we explicitly state the following fact that has been noticed in the previous proof.

Remark 10.7
.

Let
(g,h,Q)
be a triple as in
Definition

10.5
. Note that
[g,h]\notin Q
yields that
o([g,h]Q)=2
.
Consequently,
\frac{1}{2}\widehat{Q}(1-[g,h])=\widehat{Q}\;\widehat{(-[g,h])}=\widehat{\langle Q,-[g,h]\rangle}.

Interestingly, a further inspection of the proof of
Theorem

10.6
yields the following matrix description of
\mathcal{H}(g,h,Q)
.

Theorem 10.8
.

Let
(g,h,Q)
be a triple such that
\mathcal{H}(g,h,Q)\neq 1
and let
H=\langle g,h,Q\rangle
. Then there exists a unique primitive central idempotent
e
of
{\mathbb{Q}}H
such that
39
39

39

Confusing at first,
1-e+{\mathbb{Q}}He
however consists simply of the elements of
{\mathbb{Q}}H
projecting to the identity in all simple components of
{\mathbb{Q}}H
except the simple component
({\mathbb{Q}}H)e
where any element of
{\mathbb{Q}}He
is taken.

\mathcal{H}(g,h,Q)=\operatorname{SL}_{1}({\mathbb{Z}}H)\cap\left(1-e+{\mathbb{Q}}He\right).

Moreover,
\mathcal{H}(g,h,Q)=1-e+V_{m}
for
m=o(gQ)|Q|
and
V_{m}
defined in (
49
). Also, considering any maximal order
\mathcal{O}
in
{\mathbb{Q}}He
and denoting by
\Gamma(m)
the principal congruence subgroup of level
m=o(gQ)|Q|
in
\operatorname{SL}_{1}(\mathcal{O})
, one has that

\Gamma(2m)\leq V_{m}\leq\Gamma(m)\text{ and }[\Gamma(m):V_{m}]=2.

In particular,
\mathcal{H}(g,h,Q)
is a finite index normal subgroup of
1-e+{\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}He)
.

Proof.

Note that the proof of
Theorem

10.6
in fact works locally, in the sense that it is carried out in
{\mathbb{Q}}[\langle g,h,Q\rangle]

rather than
{\mathbb{Q}}[G]
. Furthermore, the morphism
\Phi
defined in (
48
) in fact coincides with the projection
onto its simple component
{\mathbb{Q}}^{\alpha_{1}}[\langle a,b\rangle]
(notations as in the proof). More precisely, this projection factors
through
{\mathbb{Q}}^{\alpha}[\langle\overline{g}\rangle\times\langle\overline{h}\rangle]
. Projecting thereon is given by first multiplying with the central idempotent
e

and then the subsequent
projection onto
{\mathbb{Q}}^{\alpha_{1}}[\langle a,b\rangle]
is given by multiplying with
e_{T}
(we use the subscript
T
because
the component arises from the trivial character).
The element
e_{T}\,e
is a primitive central idempotent of
{\mathbb{Q}}[\langle g,h,Q\rangle]
and
\Phi
is a concrete realization of the composition of
multiplying with
e_{T}\,e
followed by applying
\phi
(defined in (
36
)). In particular

\Phi
is injective on
{\mathbb{Q}}H\cap(1-e_{T}\,e+{\mathbb{Q}}He_{T}\,e)
.

Next, as
ge_{T}\,e=a
and
he_{T}\,e=b
, one has that
o(ge_{T}\,e)=2=o(he_{T}\,e)
.
Using this a direct verification yields that any element in

{\mathbb{Q}}H\cap(1-e_{T}\,e+{\mathbb{Q}}He_{T}\,e)
can be rewritten in the form (
45
) for some
x_{1},x_{2},y_{1},y_{2}\in{\mathbb{Q}}
. However,
as explained in the proof of
Theorem

10.6
, via the injectivity of
\Phi
, such an element is in
\operatorname{SL}_{1}({\mathbb{Z}}G)
if and only if

(x_{1},x_{2},y_{1},y_{2})
is admissible for
(g,h,Q)
. Hence
\mathcal{H}(g,h,Q)=\operatorname{SL}_{1}({\mathbb{Z}}H)\cap(1-e_{T}\,e+{\mathbb{Q}}He_{T}\,e)
and thus
e_{T}\,e
is the
desired primitive central idempotent of
{\mathbb{Q}}H
. The moreover part of the statement of the result was explicitly obtained in the proof of

Theorem

10.6
, see (
50
). The final finite index assertion holds because
{\mathbb{Q}}He_{T}e\cong\operatorname{M}_{2}({\mathbb{Q}})
hence
the center of the unit group of an order therein is finite and therefore
\operatorname{SL}_{1}({\mathbb{Z}}Ge)
is of finite index in
{\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}He)
.

It remains to prove that
\mathcal{H}(g,h,Q)
is normal in
1-e+{\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}He)
or in other words that
V_{m}
is normal in

{\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}He)\cong{\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}^{\alpha_{1}}[\langle a,b\rangle]).
A presentation of the latter group was obtained in

Proposition

8.2
. The image under
\phi
of the generators are the following matrices:

\left\{\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&2\\
0&1\end{array}\right),\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&0\\
2&1\end{array}\right),\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&0\\
0&-1\end{array}\right),\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&1\\
1&0\end{array}\right)\right\}.

A direct verification yields that these matrices normalize
V_{m}
for every
m
, finishing the proof.
∎

Note also that, unlike primitive
H
-units, the elements
v_{(x_{1},x_{2},y_{1},y_{2})}
are not necessarily unipotent.
For instance, using the map
\Phi
in (
48
) we see that

(51)

-1+v_{(x_{1},x_{2},y_{1},y_{2})}\text{ is nilpotent }\Leftrightarrow\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}y_{1}=-y_{2}\\
y_{1}y_{2}=x_{1}x_{2}\end{array}\right.

Finally, it would be interesting to investigate how different
H
-units interact.

Question 10.9
.

Let
(g_{i},h_{i},Q_{i}),
with
i=1,2
, be two different triples as in
Definition

10.5
.
What is the structure of the group
\langle\mathcal{H}(g_{1},h_{1},Q_{1}),\mathcal{H}(g_{2},h_{2},Q_{2})\rangle
?

A first interesting contribution to
10.9
would be to determine when it is the direct product of

\mathcal{H}(g_{1},h_{1},Q_{1})
and
\mathcal{H}(g_{2},h_{2},Q_{2})
.

Remark 10.10
.

The proof of
Theorem

10.6
has shown that locally, i.e. in
{\mathbb{Q}}[\langle g,h,Q\rangle]
,
v_{(x_{1},x_{2},y_{1},y_{2})}
injects via
\Phi
to an element of
\operatorname{SL}_{1}({\mathbb{Z}}^{\alpha}[C_{2}\times C_{2}])
. By
Proposition

8.2
,
\Phi(v_{(x_{1},x_{2},y_{1},y_{2})})

is a product of elements as in
Definition

10.1
. In particular the elements in the latter are somehow the smallest, hence the name primitive. Besides, the map
\Phi
is directly related with decomposing
\langle g,h,Q\rangle
as a non-split extension of
\langle Q,[g,h],g^{2}\rangle
by
C_{2}\times C_{2}.
Thus the non-triviality of that second cohomology group was explicitly necessary for the existence of
H
-units. This clarifies our choice of the name for those units.

10.3.
H-units extend bicyclic units with infinite index

Recall that a simple quotient of
{\mathbb{Q}}[G]
is called an
exceptional component of type II
if it is of the
form
40
40

40

The division algebras appearing in these matrix algebras are exactly those having an order with finite unit
group
[
2
, Theorem 2.10.]
.

\operatorname{M}_{2}({\mathbb{Q}}(\sqrt{-d}))
or
\operatorname{M}_{2}(\left(\frac{-a,-b}{{\mathbb{Q}}}\right))
with
a
and
b
strict positive
integers and
d\in{\mathbb{N}}
(i.e.
\left(\frac{-a,-b}{{\mathbb{Q}}}\right)
is a totally definite quaternion algebra) and it is called an
exceptional component of type I
if it is a division algebra which is not a totally definite
quaternion algebra. The terminology ‘exceptional’ refers to the fact that in their absence the bicyclic units are of finite index
in
\operatorname{SL}_{1}({\mathbb{Z}}G)
, e.g. see
Corollary

6.7
. Therefore, as
\operatorname{SL}_{1}(\mathcal{O})
for
\mathcal{O}
an order in a division
algebra has no unipotent units, the focus of current research is on the type II exceptional components

As demonstrated by
[
2
, Appendix A]
the most recurrent
41
41

41

{\mathbb{Q}}G
has an exceptional
2\times 2
component exactly
when it maps onto one of the
52
groups in
[
13
]
. Now the table in
[
2
, Appendix A]
says that only
16
of
them have no simple component of the type
\operatorname{M}_{2}({\mathbb{Q}})
. Among nilpotent groups there are only
5
such groups.

component of that type is
\operatorname{M}_{2}({\mathbb{Q}})
and this is a simple quotient of
{\mathbb{Q}}G
if and only if
G
surjects onto
D_{8}
or
S_{3}
.
It follows that, when
3\nmid|G|
and
\operatorname{M}_{2}({\mathbb{Q}})
is a simple component of
{\mathbb{Q}}G
, one has a triple
(g,h,Q)
satisfying the following:

(52)

g,h\in N_{G}(Q)\text{ and }\langle g,h,Q\rangle/Q\cong D_{8}.

In fact the stronger properties
Q\triangleleft G
and
G/Q=\langle gQ,hQ\rangle=\langle ghQ,hQ\rangle\cong D_{8}
are satisfied. We will call a triple satisfying (
52
)
a
D_{8}
-triple.

Convention:
For a
D_{8}
-triple
(g,h,Q)
we will always assume that
o(gQ)=o(hQ)=2
and
D_{8}\cong C_{4}\rtimes C_{2}=\langle ghQ\rangle\rtimes\langle hQ\rangle.

Using
H
-units built on
D_{8}
-triples we can describe generically a subgroup of finite index for the following class of groups.

Theorem 10.11
.

Let
G
be a
2
-group such that the only exceptional components of
{\mathbb{Q}}G
are of the form
\operatorname{M}_{2}({\mathbb{Q}})
, then
\langle\operatorname{Bic}(G),\mathcal{H}(G)\rangle
is of finite index in
\operatorname{SL}_{1}({\mathbb{Z}}G)
. Consequently,
\langle\mathcal{B}(G),\mathcal{H}(G)\rangle
is of finite index in
{\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}G)
.

In the above
\mathcal{B}(G)
denotes the subgroup of
{\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}G)
generated by the bicyclic and Bass units (see
[
26
, Section 1.2.]
for definitions).

Proof.

Denote by
\mathcal{E}_{exc}
the set of primitive central idempotents
e
such that
{\mathbb{Q}}Ge\cong\operatorname{M}_{2}({\mathbb{Q}})
.
For
e\in\mathcal{E}_{exc}
consider the associated projection
\pi_{e}:G\rightarrow Ge

(which is the restriction of the natural projection from
{\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Q}}G)
to
{\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Q}}G)e
). Since
G
is a
2
-group one has that

\pi_{e}(G)=Ge\cong D_{8}
. Consider
Q=\ker(\pi_{e})
and take
g,h\in G
such that

Ge\cong\langle\pi_{e}(g)\rangle\rtimes\langle\pi_{e}(h)\rangle
, with
o(gQ)=4,o(hQ)=2
and

[gQ,hQ]=(gQ)^{2}
. In this way we obtain a
D_{8}
-triple
(gh,h,Q)
such that
1\neq\mathcal{H}(gh,h,Q)
(by
Theorem

10.6
)
and
G=\langle gh,h,Q\rangle
. This allows to apply
Theorem

10.8
to conclude that

H_{e}:=\mathcal{H}(gh,h,Q)\leq\operatorname{SL}_{1}({\mathbb{Z}}G)
is of finite index in
1-e+{\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}Ge)
.

Now consider
\mathcal{E}:=\operatorname{PCI}({\mathbb{Q}}G)\setminus\mathcal{E}_{exc}
and take
e^{\prime}\in\mathcal{E}
, i.e.
{\mathbb{Q}}Ge^{\prime}\ncong\operatorname{M}_{2}({\mathbb{Q}})
.
By assumption, if
{\mathbb{Q}}Ge^{\prime}
is a division algebra it needs to be a totally definite quaternion algebra, and hence
\operatorname{SL}_{1}({\mathbb{Z}}Ge^{\prime})
is
finite
[
37
]
. It now remains to consider the case that
{\mathbb{Q}}Ge^{\prime}
is not a division algebra, say
\operatorname{M}_{n}(D)
, and let
\mathcal{O}

be an order in the division algebra
D
. In this case classical arguments can be used. Namely, for such a component
Corollary

6.7
gives a
y\in{\mathbb{Z}}
and
subgroup
1-e^{\prime}+E_{n}(y\mathcal{O})\leq\operatorname{Bic}(G)
. Because
{\mathbb{Q}}Ge^{\prime}
is non-exceptional, the solution to the subgroup
congruence problem in higher rank (e.g. see
[
26
, Theorem 11.2.3]
) yields that
E_{e^{\prime}}:=E_{n}(y\mathcal{O})
is
of finite index in
\operatorname{GL}_{n}(\mathcal{O})
. All together we obtained a subgroup
H=\prod_{e}H_{e}\times\prod_{e^{\prime}}E_{e^{\prime}}
of
\operatorname{SL}_{1}({\mathbb{Z}}G)

which is of finite index in
\prod_{e\in\operatorname{PCI}({\mathbb{Q}}G))}\operatorname{SL}_{1}({\mathbb{Z}}Ge)
, hence also in
\operatorname{SL}_{1}({\mathbb{Z}}G)
. The second part now classically follows
by a Bass-Milnor Theorem
[
26
, Theorem 11.1.2]
which says that the Bass units map to a subgroup of finite index in the
Whitehead group
K_{1}({\mathbb{Z}}G):=\operatorname{GL}({\mathbb{Z}}G)^{ab}
of
{\mathbb{Z}}G
and hence
[
26
, Prop. 9.5.11]
jointly with
H
it is of finite
index in
{\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}G)
. In particular also
\langle\mathcal{B}(G),\mathcal{H}(G)\rangle
is of finite index in
{\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}G)
.
∎

Remark 10.12
.

The first part of the proof of
Theorem

10.11
shows that when
G
does not map onto
S_{3}

one can extend the Jespers-Leal theorem
[
29
]
by including also the exceptional component
\operatorname{M}_{2}({\mathbb{Q}})
.
Concretely, let
e\in\operatorname{PCI}({\mathbb{Q}}G)
such that
{\mathbb{Q}}Ge\cong\operatorname{M}_{2}({\mathbb{Q}})
and
3\nmid|Ge|
. Then
\operatorname{SL}_{1}({\mathbb{Z}}G)

contains a subgroup
W_{e}
, consisting of
H
-units, that is of finite index in
1-e+{\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}Ge)\subset 1-e+{\mathbb{Q}}Ge
.
Moreover
W_{e}
contains a principal congruence subgroup of level
2o(gQ)|Q|=2\cdot 2\cdot\frac{|G|}{8}=\frac{|G|}{2}
of
\operatorname{SL}_{1}({\mathbb{Z}}Ge)
. More precisely,
W_{e}\cong V_{|G|/4}.

The condition
3\nmid|Ge|
stems from the fact that the proof of
Theorem

10.6
requires
Proposition

8.2
. On turn the latter originates from a splitting of
D_{8}
and needs a precise understanding on subgroups of small index in
\operatorname{SL}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}}).
Using
[
48
]
, the necessary tools seem to exist to extend the results to
S_{3}
. In particular we expect that the above and
Theorem

10.11
extends to non
2
-groups.

By a result of Jespers and del Río
[
25
]
natural examples for finite groups
G
as in Theorem
10.11

can be found when
{\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}G)
is virtually a direct product of free products of abelian groups. Such groups have been classified and
the
2
-groups of this type are isomorphic to
K\times C_{2}^{n}
with
K
one of the following types :

\mathcal{G}_{1}
:

\langle x,y\mid x^{4}=y^{4}=1\text{ and }y^{2},x^{2}=[x,y]\text{ central }\rangle,

\mathcal{G}_{2}
:

\langle x,y_{1},\ldots,y_{n}\mid x^{4}=y_{i}^{2}=[y_{i},y_{j}]=1\text{ and }x^{2},[x,y_{i}]\text{ central }\rangle,

\mathcal{G}_{3}
:

\langle x,y_{1},\ldots,y_{n}\mid x^{4}=y_{i}^{4}=y_{i}^{2}[x,y_{i}]=[y_{i},y_{j}]=1\text{ and }x^{2},y_{i}^{2}\text{ central }\rangle,

\mathcal{G}_{4}
:

\langle x,y_{1},\ldots,y_{n}\mid x^{2}=y_{i}^{2}=[y_{i},y_{j}]=[[x,y_{i}],y_{j}]=[x,y_{i}]^{2}=1\rangle,

\mathcal{G}_{5}
:

\langle x,y_{1},\ldots,y_{n}\mid x^{2}=y_{i}^{4}=y_{i}^{2}[x,y_{i}]=[y_{i},y_{j}]=[[x,y_{i}],x]=1\rangle,

\mathcal{G}_{6}
:

\langle x,y_{1},\ldots,y_{n}\mid x^{4}=y_{i}^{4}=x^{2}y_{1}^{2}=y_{i}^{2}[x,y_{i}]=[y_{i},y_{j}]=[y_{i}^{2},x]=1\rangle,

\mathcal{G}_{7}
:

\langle x,y_{1},\ldots,y_{n}\mid x^{4}=x^{2}y_{i}^{4}=y_{i}^{-2}[x,y_{i}]=[y_{i},y_{j}]=1\rangle.

Remark 10.13
.

In
[
25
]
the relation
y_{i}^{2}[x,y_{i}]=1
is written for the groups in class
\mathcal{G}_{7}
(which are the groups of ‘type (g)’in loc. cit.). Inspection of the proof however shows that it must be
y_{i}^{-2}[x,y_{i}]=1.

The group in
\mathcal{G}_{1}
is simply
C_{4}\rtimes C_{4}
with
C_{4}
acting by inversion. It is known
[
26
, Corollary 12.7.2]
that
\operatorname{Bic}(C_{4}\rtimes C_{4})
is of infinite index in
\operatorname{SL}_{1}({\mathbb{Z}}G)
.

For groups
G
as above,
[
25
, Theorem 1.3]
tells that the non-division algebra components of
{\mathbb{Q}}G
are isomorphic to

\operatorname{M}_{2}({\mathbb{Q}})
. Moreover,
{\mathbb{Q}}G
does not have exceptional components of type I. Thus we can use
Theorem

10.11

to obtain that
\mathcal{H}(G)
is a so-called congruence subgroup of
\operatorname{SL}_{1}({\mathbb{Z}}G)
. More importantly, as a byproduct we obtain a more precise version of
[
12
, Theorem 1.1]
and a classification-free proof.

Corollary 10.14
.

Let
G=K\times C_{2}^{n}
, with
K
a group in
\mathcal{G}_{1}\cup\ldots\cup\mathcal{G}_{7}
, and let
q
denote the number of simple components
of the type
\operatorname{M}_{2}({\mathbb{Q}})
in
{\mathbb{Q}}G
. Then, there exist
D_{8}
-triples
(g_{i},h_{i},Q_{i})
,
1\leq i\leq q
, such that

\langle\mathcal{H}(g_{i},h_{i},Q_{i})\rangle\cong\prod_{i=1}^{q}\mathcal{H}(g_{i},h_{i},Q_{i})
is a finite index normal subgroup of

\operatorname{SL}_{1}({\mathbb{Z}}G)
. In particular, the
H
-units
\mathcal{H}(G)
are of finite index despite that
\operatorname{Bic}(G)
can be of infinite index.
Moreover, if
G\ncong D_{8}
,
\langle\mathcal{H}(g_{i},h_{i},Q_{i})\rangle\cong F_{n}^{q}
with
n=1+\frac{|G|^{3}}{2.4^{4}}
.

In fact
\langle\mathcal{H}(g_{i},h_{i},Q_{i})\rangle
is the largest finite index subgroup in
\operatorname{SL}_{1}({\mathbb{Z}}G)
which is the direct product of free groups. This follows from
Theorem

10.8
, saying that
\mathcal{H}(g_{i},h_{i},Q_{i})=\operatorname{SL}_{1}({\mathbb{Z}}G)\cap 1-e+{\mathbb{Q}}Ge
for an associated
e\in\operatorname{PCI}({\mathbb{Q}}G)
, and the indecomposability of
SL_{1}(\mathcal{O})
for
\mathcal{O}
any order in some
\operatorname{M}_{n}(D)
(
n\neq 1
)
[
38
]
.
That
\prod_{e\in\operatorname{PCI}({\mathbb{Q}}G)}\operatorname{SL}_{1}({\mathbb{Z}}G)\cap 1-e+{\mathbb{Q}}Ge
is the largest direct product of free groups was already obtained by del Río and Ruiz in
[
12
, Theorem 1.1]
. However our proof is uniform, i.e. we do not use the classification for
G
, and yield more explicit generators.

Proof of
Corollary

10.14
.

Let
e\in\operatorname{PCI}({\mathbb{Q}}G)
. By
[
25
, Theorem 1.3]
, either
{\mathbb{Q}}Ge
is a totally definite quaternion algebra or it is isomorphic
to
\operatorname{M}_{2}({\mathbb{Q}})
. In the former case
\operatorname{SL}_{1}({\mathbb{Z}}Ge)
is finite, so we only need to consider the case
{\mathbb{Q}}Ge\cong\operatorname{M}_{2}({\mathbb{Q}}).

As pointed out in
Remark

10.12
, the proof of
Theorem

10.11
gives a
D_{8}
-triple
(g_{e},h_{e},Q_{e})

such that
\mathcal{H}(g_{e},h_{e},Q_{e})
is of finite index in
1-e+\mathcal{U}({\mathbb{Z}}Ge)
. In particular, picking one such triple for every
e

now yields a subgroup of
\operatorname{SL}_{1}({\mathbb{Z}}G)
which is even of finite index in the overgroup
\prod_{e}\operatorname{SL}_{1}({\mathbb{Z}}Ge)
such that

\langle\mathcal{H}(g_{i},h_{i},Q_{i})\rangle\cong\prod_{i=1}^{q}\mathcal{H}(g_{i},h_{i},Q_{i})
.
Following
Theorem

10.8
,
\mathcal{H}(g_{e},h_{e},Q_{e})
is isomorphic to a certain group
V_{m}
which
is normal in
{\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}Ge)
. This implies that
\prod_{i=1}^{q}\mathcal{H}(g_{i},h_{i},Q_{i})
is normal in
\prod_{e}{\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}Ge)
and in particular
in the subgroup
{\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}G)
. Now, as mentioned earlier for example in the group
\mathcal{G}_{1}
the bicyclic units are of
infinite index. This finishes the first part.

That
\mathcal{H}(g_{i},h_{i},Q_{i})\cong F_{n}
with
n
as in the statement is a combination of
Theorem

10.6
and
Remark

10.12
. More precisely, the latter says that
\mathcal{H}(g_{i},h_{i},Q_{i})\cong V_{m}
with
m=o(gQ)|Q|=\frac{|G|}{4}
. Now would
q\neq 0
and
o(gQ)|Q|=2
, then
Q=1
and
o(g)=2.
This however entails that
G\cong D_{8},
which was excluded. Therefore,
Theorem

10.6
yields that
\mathcal{H}(g_{i},h_{i},Q_{i})
is a free group and as
m
is a
2
-power the product in
Theorem

10.6
only runs over the prime divisor
2
, yielding the desired formula.
∎

To finish this section, we exhibit a first surprising application of
H
-units which indicate a first of many new possible paths of research.

Example of a normal complement via
H
-units

Consider

\Gamma=C_{4}\rtimes C_{4}:=\langle a,b\mid a^{4}=b^{4}=1,\,a^{b}=a^{-1}\rangle,

a group in the class
\mathcal{G}_{1}
.
Recall that

{\mathbb{Q}}[\Gamma]\cong{\mathbb{Q}}[C_{2}\times C_{2}]\oplus 2{\mathbb{Q}}(i)\oplus\left(\frac{-1,-1}{{\mathbb{Q}}}\right)\oplus\operatorname{M}_{2}({\mathbb{Q}})
. In particular we can apply all the results above. Concretely, notice that
(ab,b,\langle b^{2}\rangle)
is a
D_{8}
-triple fulfilling the non-triviality condition of
Theorem

10.6
with
o(\overline{ab})|\langle b^{2}\rangle|=4
. As there is only one matrix component and
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}\Gamma))
is finite,
Theorem

10.6
now yields that

\mathcal{H}(ab,b,\langle b^{2}\rangle)\cong F_{1+\frac{4^{3}.3}{6.4}}=F_{9}\text{ finite index normal subgroup in }{\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}\Gamma).

Furthermore, by
Theorem

10.8
,

\mathcal{H}(ab,b,\langle b^{2}\rangle))=\operatorname{SL}_{1}({\mathbb{Z}}\Gamma)\cap(1-e+{\mathbb{Q}}\Gamma e)
with
{\mathbb{Q}}\Gamma e\cong\operatorname{M}_{2}({\mathbb{Q}})
and

\mathcal{H}(ab,b,\langle b^{2}\rangle))=1-e+V_{4}
is a normal subgroup. Furthermore,

\operatorname{SL}_{1}({\mathbb{Z}}\Gamma)=\langle\mathcal{H}(ab,b,\langle b^{2}\rangle)),G\cap\operatorname{SL}_{1}({\mathbb{Z}}\Gamma)\rangle.

The latter can be seen from the facts: (i)
\operatorname{SL}_{1}({\mathbb{Z}}\Gamma)\leq 1\times\operatorname{SL}_{1}\left(\frac{-1,-1}{{\mathbb{Z}}}\right)=Q_{8}\times\operatorname{SL}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})
and (ii)
\Gamma\cap\operatorname{SL}_{1}({\mathbb{Z}}\Gamma)=\langle a^{2}\rangle
(all other
g\in\Gamma
have a non-trivial projection in one of the commutative components) and with
a^{2}
mapping to
-1
in
\operatorname{SL}_{1}\left(\frac{-1,-1}{{\mathbb{Z}}}\right)
. With a bit more of work
42
42

42

For example, via the methods as in the proof of Claim 4 in the proof of
Theorem

10.16
one can prove that
{\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}\Gamma)=\langle\pm\Gamma,\operatorname{SL}_{1}({\mathbb{Z}}G)\rangle
. The latter fact can alternatively be deduced from
[
28
, Theorem 5.1]
.
one can proof that
{\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}\Gamma)=\langle\mathcal{H}(ab,b,\langle b^{2}\rangle),\pm\Gamma\rangle
. All together we recover
[
28
, Theorem 5.1.]
(also see
[
4
, Example 5.5]
):

{\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}[\Gamma])\cong F_{9}\rtimes\pm\Gamma

where
F_{9}=\mathcal{H}(ab,b,\langle b^{2}\rangle)
. This description of the free group
F_{9}
yields a
new set of generators.

A full presentation
can also be obtained. A presentation was given in
[
4
, Example 5.5]
,
using
[
28
, Theorem 5.1.]
, but our methods will yield a considerably more symmetric presentation. Indeed,
as
F_{9}=\mathcal{H}(ab,b,\langle b^{2}\rangle)=1-e+V_{4}
, it is enough to compute the action of

\pi_{e}(\Gamma)=\langle\pi_{e}(ab),\pi_{e}(b)\rangle\cong D_{8}
on
V_{4}
, all seen as subgroups of
\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})
.
To do so, recall that
Proposition

8.2
and its proof yields a matrix representation of
all elements and also record the required actions. More precisely,
V_{2}=\langle w^{2},v^{2},w^{-1}v,-1\rangle
with

w=\left(\begin{array}[]{ll}1&2\\
0&1\end{array}\right)
and
v=\left(\begin{array}[]{ll}1&0\\
2&1\end{array}\right)
.
Also,
\pi_{e}(ab)=\left(\begin{array}[]{lr}1&0\\
0&-1\end{array}\right)
and
\pi_{e}(b)=\left(\begin{array}[]{ll}0&1\\
1&0\end{array}\right).

Via a direct application of the Reidemeister-Schreier method one obtains that

(53)

V_{4}=\langle v^{4},w^{4},w^{2}v^{-2},x,v^{2}xv^{-2}\mid x\in S\rangle

with
S=\{(w^{-1}v)^{2},(wv^{-1})^{2},(w^{-1}v)(wv^{-1})^{-1}\}.

Using the action in
Proposition

8.2
(or the matrices above) one readily verifies that

y^{\pi_{e}(ab)}=y^{-1}\text{ for }y\in\{v^{4},w^{4},(w^{-1}v)(wv^{-1})^{-1}\}.

and

z^{\pi_{e}(b)}=z^{-1}\text{ for }z\in\{w^{2}v^{-2},(w^{-1}v)^{2},(wv^{-1})^{2}\}.

Furthermore,
(w^{2}v^{-2})^{\pi_{e}(ab)}=w^{-4}(w^{2}v^{-2})v^{4}
and

\left((w^{-1}v)(wv^{-1})^{-1}\right)^{\pi_{e}(b)}=(w^{-1}v)^{2}(w^{-1}v)(wv^{-1})^{-1}(wv^{-1})^{2}.

The remaining actions are computed similarly, yielding all together the following presentation:

V({\mathbb{Z}}\Gamma)=\langle s,t,u,x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},y_{1},y_{2},y_{3}\rangle\rtimes\langle a,b\rangle\cong F_{9}\rtimes\Gamma

where the action is the following:

\begin{array}[]{lll}s^{ab}=s^{-1}&x_{1}^{ab}=x_{2}&y_{1}^{ab}=s^{-1}y_{2}s\\
t^{ab}=t^{-1}&x_{2}^{ab}=x_{1}&y_{2}^{ab}=s^{-1}y_{1}s\\
u^{ab}=t^{-1}us&x_{3}^{ab}=x_{3}^{-1}&y_{3}^{ab}=s^{-1}y_{3}s\\
s^{b}=t&x_{1}^{b}=x_{1}^{-1}&y_{1}^{b}=uy_{1}^{-1}u^{-1}\\
t^{b}=s&x_{2}^{b}=x_{2}^{-1}&y_{2}^{b}=uy_{2}^{-1}u^{-1}\\
u^{b}=u^{-1}&x_{3}^{b}=x_{1}^{-1}x_{3}x_{2}&y_{3}^{b}=uy_{1}^{-1}y_{3}y_{2}u^{-1}\\
\end{array}

The previous example naturally raises the following question:

Question 10.15
.

When is
\langle\operatorname{Bic}(G),\mathcal{H}(G)\rangle
a normal complement for the trivial units? Also, when is
\operatorname{SL}_{1}({\mathbb{Z}}G)
a complement?

10.4.
Applications to the abelianisation conjectures

Recall that
{\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}G)
is a finitely generated group and hence
{\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}G)^{ab}
is the direct product of a finitely generated free abelian group

{\mathbb{Z}}^{n}
and a finite abelian group. One calls
n
the rank of the abelian group
{\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}G)^{ab}
. Also recall that the center
\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{U}({\mathbb{Z}}G))
of
\mathcal{U}({\mathbb{Z}}G)
is finitely generated, hence its rank is finite. Finally, recall that
{\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}G)=\pm V({\mathbb{Z}}G)

with
V({\mathbb{Z}}G)
the
group of invertible elements with augmentation one
.

We finish the article with another application of
H
-units. We do this by giving an answer, for the class of groups considered above,
to the following recent questions on the abelianisation of the unit group of
{\mathbb{Z}}G
:

(R1)

Is the rank of the abelian groups
\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{U}({\mathbb{Z}}G))
and
{\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}G)^{ab}
equal? In particular if
\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{U}({\mathbb{Z}}G))
is finite, is
{\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}G)^{ab}
also finite? (see
[
2
, Question 7.8 and Proposition 7.9]
, The labelling of the questions is taken from
[
4
]
.)

(P)

If
V({\mathbb{Z}}G)^{ab}
contains an element of prime order
p
, then so does
G^{ab}
?
(see
[
4
, page 2]
)

(E1)

Is
\exp V({\mathbb{Z}}G)^{ab}=\exp G^{ab}
? (see
[
4
, page 2]
)

The labelling of the questions is taken from
[
4
]
. In question (E1) by
\exp\Gamma
of a group
\Gamma
we mean the least common multiple of the elements of finite order in
\Gamma
. Note that question (E2) implies question (P).

In general, as proven in
[
2
, Proposition 6.1]
,

\rm rank\,{\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}G)^{ab}\geq\rm rank\,\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{U}({\mathbb{Z}}G)).

In case
{\mathbb{Q}}G
does not have exceptional components the above is
an equality by
[
31
, Theorem 6.3]
, and hence question (R1) has a positive answer in that case. However in
[
4
, Theorem D]

one group, where
{\mathbb{Q}}G
has an exceptional component of the form
\operatorname{M}_{2}({\mathbb{Q}}(i))
, was found where the rank of the abelianisation is non-zero but the center of the unit group is finite (thus the rank of
{\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}G)^{ab}
is larger than expected).
Surprisingly, crucially using
\mathcal{H}(G)
, for each group in
\mathcal{G}_{i}\times C_{2}^{n}
, for
1\leq i\leq 7
, we obtain an
expression for the exponent and rank of the abelianisation.

Recall that for
G\in\mathcal{G}_{i}
and
e\in\operatorname{PCI}({\mathbb{Q}}G)
, one has that
{\mathbb{Q}}Ge
either is a division algebra that is not exceptional of type I or it is a simple component of type
M_{2}({\mathbb{Q}})
(and thus of exceptional type II). The set of primitive central idempotents
e
of the latter type we will denote (as in the proof of Theorem
10.11
) as

\mathcal{E}_{exc}
. Also recall that for
e\in\mathcal{E}_{exc}
the group

G
can be written as an extension as follows:

1\rightarrow N\longrightarrow G\xrightarrow{\pi_{e}}Ge\cong D_{8}=\langle a:a^{4}=1\rangle\rtimes\langle b:b^{2}=1\rangle\rightarrow 1.

Consider the following property:

(54)

(\star)\,\forall e\in\mathcal{E}_{exc},\exists\,g,h\in G:o(\pi_{e}(g))=o(ab)\text{ and }o(\pi_{e}(h))=o(b).

Note that this property is satisfied if
G
is a split extension of
D_{8}.
Under the additional property
(\star)
we will give a positive answer to (R1) and (P), despite that such groups may have arbitrarily many exceptional components.

Theorem 10.16
.

Let
G=K\times C_{2}^{n}
with
K
a group in
\mathcal{G}_{1}\cup\ldots\cup\mathcal{G}_{7}
and
\pi
the natural map of
{\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}G)
onto
{\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}G)^{ab}
. Then

\rm rank\,{\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}G)^{ab}=\rm rank\,\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{U}({\mathbb{Z}}G))+\rm rank\,\pi(\langle\mathcal{H}(G)_{un}\rangle),

where
\mathcal{H}(G)_{un}=\{x\in\mathcal{H}(G)\mid x\text{ is unipotent }\}.
Furthermore,

\exp(V({\mathbb{Z}}G)^{ab})=\operatorname{lcm}\left(\exp(G^{ab}),\exp\left(V({\mathbb{Z}}G)/\mathcal{Z}(V({\mathbb{Z}}G))\,cl_{{\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}G)}(\langle\operatorname{Bic}(G),\pm G\rangle\right)^{ab}\right).

Moreover, if
G
satisfies
(\star)
, then
cl_{{\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}G)}(\langle\operatorname{Bic}(G),\pm G\rangle)
, i.e. the normal closure of the bicyclic and trivial units, together with the centre
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}G))
is of finite index in
{\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}G)
and (R1) and (P) have a positive answer.

Recall that for the group
\mathcal{G}_{1}
the group
\langle\operatorname{Bic}(G),\pm G\rangle
is of infinite index, which we expect to be a rather general phenomena for the classes of groups considered. Thus it is somehow surprising that under property
(\star)
their normal closure is of finite index.

Remark 10.17
.

(1) The combination of
[
20
, Lemma 4.4]
with
Theorem

10.8
yields that
\langle\mathcal{H}(G)_{un}\rangle
is of infinite index in
\operatorname{SL}_{1}({\mathbb{Z}}G)
whenever
|G|\geq 32
and
G=K\times C_{2}^{n}
with
K\in\mathcal{G}_{1}\cup\ldots\cup\mathcal{G}_{7}
. Thus for this class of groups the rank-formula reduces the problem of
determining the abelianisation to a significantly smaller group.

(2) The proof of
Theorem

10.16
will furthermore yield that

\rm rank\,{\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}G)^{ab}=\rm rank\,\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{U}({\mathbb{Z}}G))+\rm rank\,\left({\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}G)/\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{U}({\mathbb{Z}}G))\,cl_{{\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}G)}(\langle\operatorname{Bic}(G),\pm G\rangle)\right)^{ab}.

Clearly
D_{8}\in\mathcal{G}_{4}
and
G=D_{8}\times C_{2}^{n}
is a cut group (i.e.
\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{U}({\mathbb{Z}}G))
is finite) and
G
satisfies
(\star)
, thus
Theorem

10.16
yields that

\rm rank\,({\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}[D_{8}\times C_{2}^{n}])^{ab})=0

for all
n
, answering
7.6
in case
G=D_{8}
.

Proof of the torsion-free part of
Theorem

10.16
.

Recall that taking abelianisation is right exact. More precisely, for any group
\Gamma
and normal subgroup
N
ons has the short exact sequence

(55)

1\rightarrow\pi_{\Gamma}(N)\rightarrow\Gamma^{ab}\rightarrow(\Gamma/N)^{ab}\rightarrow 1

where
\pi_{\Gamma}:\Gamma\rightarrow\Gamma^{ab}
is the canonical projection and thus

\pi_{\Gamma}(N)\cong N/N\cap[\Gamma,\Gamma]
.

In particular

(56)

\rm rank\,(\Gamma^{ab})=\rm rank\,(\pi_{\Gamma}(N))+\rm rank\,((\Gamma/N)^{ab}).

Furthermore, it follows from the proof of
[
26
, Proposition 5.5.1]
that
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}G))\cap\operatorname{SL}_{1}({\mathbb{Z}}G)
is finite. Since
[{\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}G),{\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}G)]\leq\operatorname{SL}_{1}({\mathbb{Z}}G)
and thus also
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}G))\cap[{\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}G),{\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}G)]
is finite, this implies that

(57)

\rm rank\,\pi(\langle\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}G)),N\rangle)=\rm rank\,\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}G))+\rm rank\,\pi(N)

for every normal subgroup
N
in
{\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}G)
such that
\pi(N)\cap\pi(\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}G)))
is finite. The latter condition is for example satisfied for a subgroup of
\langle\operatorname{SL}_{1}({\mathbb{Z}}G),\pm G\rangle.
Moreover, if
N\leq\operatorname{SL}_{1}({\mathbb{Z}}G)
, then

\rm rank\,\left(\operatorname{SL}_{1}({\mathbb{Z}}G)/N\right)^{ab}=\rm rank\,\left(\operatorname{SL}_{1}({\mathbb{Z}}G)\,\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}G)/N\,\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}G)\right)^{ab}

and thus,
because
\langle\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}G)),\operatorname{SL}_{1}({\mathbb{Z}}G)\rangle
is of finite index in
{\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}G)
(see
[
26
, Proposition 5.5.1]
),

(58)

\rm rank\,\left(\operatorname{SL}_{1}({\mathbb{Z}}G)/N\right)^{ab}\geq\rm rank\,\left({\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}G)/N\,\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}G)\right)^{ab}.

Consider
\mathcal{E}_{exc}:=\{e\in\operatorname{PCI}({\mathbb{Q}}G)\mid{\mathbb{Q}}Ge\cong\operatorname{M}_{2}({\mathbb{Q}})\}
and let
e_{1},\ldots,e_{q}
be its distinct elements.
For the rest of the proof consider the subgroup

\langle\pm G,\operatorname{Bic}(G),\prod_{i=1}^{q}\mathcal{H}(g_{i},h_{i},Q_{i})\rangle
delivered by
Corollary

10.14
, where
by
Theorem

10.8
,

\mathcal{H}(g_{i},h_{i},Q_{i})=\operatorname{SL}_{1}({\mathbb{Z}}G)\cap(1-e_{i}+{\mathbb{Q}}Ge_{i}).

As explained in the proof of
Theorem

10.8
, for each
i
we have a morphism as in (
48
), which we now denote by
\Phi_{i}
, is the composition of projecting to
{\mathbb{Q}}Ge_{i}
with the isomorphism
\phi
defined in (
36
) (
Section

8.1
). Moreover, by
Remark

10.7
, one has that
e_{i}=\widehat{\langle Q_{i},-[g_{i},h_{i}]\rangle}=\widehat{Q_{i}}\,\frac{(1-[g_{i},h_{i}])}{2}
. By
Theorem

10.8
,
\mathcal{H}(g_{i},h_{i},Q_{i})=1-e_{i}+V_{o(g_{i}Q_{i})\,|Q_{i}|}
. Note that
o(g_{i}Q_{i})\,|Q_{i}|=2|Q_{i}|=|G|/4
does not depend on
i
, and thus
|Q_{i}|=2^{m-1}
for some positive integer
m
.

First we choose
N=\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}G))\,cl_{{\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}G)}(\langle\operatorname{Bic}(G),\pm G\rangle).
It follows from
43
43

43

In
[
4
]
the result is only proven for bicyclic units of the form
1+(1-h)g\widetilde{h}
, but the same proof works for those of the form
1+\widetilde{h}g(1-h)
.

[
4
, Proposition 3.1]
that any bicyclic unit of
{\mathbb{Z}}G
is a product of commutators of elements of
\langle\operatorname{Bic}(G),\pm G\rangle
. Hence,
\pi(cl_{{\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}G)}(\langle\operatorname{Bic}(G),\pm G\rangle))
is finite. Consequently, because of (
56
),
and (
57
) we obtain that:

(59)

\displaystyle\rm rank\,{\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}G)^{ab}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\rm rank\,\left(\pi(\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{U}({\mathbb{Z}}G))\,cl_{{\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}G)}(\langle\operatorname{Bic}(G))\right)

\displaystyle+\rm rank\,\left({\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}G)/\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{U}({\mathbb{Z}}G))\,cl_{{\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}G)}(\langle\operatorname{Bic}(G),\pm G\rangle)\right)^{ab}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\rm rank\,\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{U}({\mathbb{Z}}G))+\rm rank\,\left({\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}G)/\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{U}({\mathbb{Z}}G))\,cl_{{\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}G)}(\langle\operatorname{Bic}(G),\pm G\rangle)\right)^{ab}.

Thus, if
\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{U}({\mathbb{Z}}G))\,cl_{{\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}G)}(\langle\operatorname{Bic}(G),\pm G\rangle)
is of finite index, then (R1) has a positive answer.

Now consider the bicyclic unit
b_{\widetilde{h_{i}},g_{i}}:=1+\widetilde{h_{i}}g_{i}(1-h_{i}^{-1})
. Using that
o(h_{i}Q)=2
one sees that

e_{i}b_{\widetilde{h_{i}},g_{i}}=e_{i}\left(1+\frac{o(h_{i})}{o(h_{i}Q)}(1+h_{i})g_{i}(1-h_{i})\right)=e_{i}\left(1+o(h_{i})(1+h_{i})g_{i}\right)=e_{i}v_{(\frac{-o(h_{i})}{2},\frac{o(h_{i})}{2},\frac{-o(h_{i})}{2},\frac{o(h_{i})}{2})}.

Therefore, using (
48
), composing with
\phi
gives that

\Phi_{i}(b_{\widetilde{h_{i}},g_{i}})=\left(\begin{array}[]{ll}1+o(h)&-o(h)\\
o(h)&1-o(h)\end{array}\right).

Next consider the bicyclic units
b_{h_{i},\widetilde{g_{i}}}:=1+(1-g_{i})h_{i}\widetilde{g_{i}}
and
b_{\widetilde{g_{i}},h_{i}}:=1+\widetilde{g_{i}}h_{i}(1-g_{i})
. Analogously as with the preceding unit one verifies that

\begin{array}[]{rcl}e_{i}b_{h_{i},\tilde{g_{i}}}&=&e_{i}\left(1+\frac{o(g_{i})}{o(g_{i}Q_{i})}(1-g_{i})h_{i}(1+g_{i})\right)=e_{i}v_{(0,o(g_{i}),0,0)}\\
e_{i}b_{\tilde{g_{i}},h_{i}}&=&e_{i}\left(1+\frac{o(g_{i})}{o(g_{i}Q_{i})}(1+g_{i})h_{i}(1-g_{i})\right)=e_{i}v_{(o(g_{i}),0,0,0)}.\end{array}

Thus after composing with
\phi
we obtain the matrices

\Phi_{i}(b_{h_{i},\tilde{g_{i}}})=\left(\begin{array}[]{lc}1&2o(g_{i})\\
0&1\end{array}\right)\text{ and }\Phi_{i}(b_{\tilde{g_{i}},h_{i}})=\left(\begin{array}[]{lc}1&0\\
2o(g_{i})&1\end{array}\right).

Now suppose that
G
has property
(\star).
In particular,
o(h_{i})=2=o(g_{i})
and so the matrices obtained above are simply those already encountered in
Proposition

8.2
. Namely :

(\Phi_{i}(b_{\widetilde{h_{i}},g_{i}}),\Phi_{i}(b_{h_{i},\tilde{g_{i}}}),\Phi_{i}(b_{\tilde{g_{i}},h_{i}}))=(vw^{-1},w^{2},v^{2}).

Therefore, by (
37
) we obtain that

\Phi_{i}(\pm\langle b_{h_{i},\tilde{g_{i}}},b_{\tilde{g_{i}},h_{i}},b_{\widetilde{h_{i}},g_{i}}\rangle)\cong V_{2}=\{\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1+2\,l_{2}&2\,t_{1}\\
2\,t_{2}&1+2\,l_{1}\end{array}\right)\in\operatorname{SL}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})\mid l_{1}\equiv l_{2}\text{ and }t_{1}\equiv t_{2}\mod 2\}.

In particular, because of the description given in (
50
), we get that
\mathcal{H}(g_{i},h_{i},Q_{i})\cong\Phi_{i}(\mathcal{H}(g_{i},h_{i},Q_{i}))\,\triangleleft\,\Phi_{i}(\pm\langle b_{h_{i},\tilde{g_{i}}},b_{\tilde{g_{i}},h_{i}},b_{\widetilde{h_{i}},g_{i}}\rangle)
. Denote

H:=cl_{{\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}G)}\left(\langle b_{h_{i},\tilde{g_{i}}},b_{\tilde{g_{i}},h_{i}},b_{\widetilde{h_{i}},g_{i}},g_{i},h_{i}\mid 1\leq i\leq q\rangle\right).

Next, note that

[H,\mathcal{H}(g_{i},h_{i},Q_{i})]=[1-e_{i}+He_{i},\mathcal{H}(g_{i},h_{i},Q_{i})]\cong[\Phi_{i}(H),\Phi_{i}(\mathcal{H}(g_{i},h_{i},Q_{i}))],

with
\Phi_{i}(g_{i})=\left(\begin{array}[]{lr}1&0\\
0&-1\end{array}\right)
and
\Phi_{i}(h_{i})=\left(\begin{array}[]{ll}0&1\\
1&0\end{array}\right).
We will prove that
H
is of finite index in
\prod_{i=1}^{q}{\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}Ge_{i}).
Since the latter contains
{\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}G)
and
H\leq cl_{{\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}G)}(\langle\operatorname{Bic}(G),\pm G\rangle)
, this would finish the proof of the last statement of the Theorem.

Recall that
\mathcal{H}(g_{i},h_{i},Q_{i})=1-e_{i}+V_{|G|/4}
. Hence, by the above descriptions,

(60)

[H,\prod_{i=1}^{q}\mathcal{H}(g_{i},h_{i},Q_{i})]=\prod_{i=1}^{q}1-e_{i}+[\langle V_{2},\Phi(g_{i}),\Phi(h_{i})\rangle,V_{|G|/4}].

We claim that
V_{|G|/4}/[\langle V_{2},\Phi(g_{i}),\Phi(h_{i})\rangle,V_{|G|/4}]
is finite. To prove this we need following general group theoretical inequality:

Claim
1
:
Let
N_{2}\leq N_{1}
be normal finite index subgroups of some group
\Gamma
. Denote
[N_{1}/[\Gamma,N_{2}]:N_{2}/[\Gamma,N_{2}]]=n
which divides
[N_{1}:N_{2}]
. If
N_{1}/[\Gamma,N_{1}]
is finite, then
N_{2}/[\Gamma,N_{2}]
is finite. Furthermore,
|[\Gamma,\Gamma]/[\Gamma,N_{2}]|
divides
\left([\Gamma:N_{1}]n\right)^{[s/2]+1}
where
s
is the product of all
p^{e_{p}}
with
e_{p}
the maximum exponent of
p
dividing
n[\Gamma:N_{1}]
.

Indeed, to prove the claim and for notation simplicity we denote
M:=[\Gamma,N_{2}].
Note that
N_{2}/M
is central in

\Gamma/M
and thus the latter is central-by-finite. Hence, by a well-known theorem of Schur,
[\Gamma/M,\Gamma/M]
is finite.
Moreover, by
[
59
, Theorem 1]
,
|[\Gamma/M,\Gamma/M]|\mid([\Gamma/M:\mathcal{Z}(G)/M])^{[t/2]+1}
with
t
the product of
all
p^{l_{p}}
with
l_{p}
the maximum exponent of
p
dividing
[\Gamma/M:\mathcal{Z}(\Gamma)/M]
. As

[\Gamma/M:\mathcal{Z}(\Gamma)/M]\mid[\Gamma/M:N_{1}/M].[N_{1}/M:N_{2}/M]
it also divides the multiple mentioned in the statement of the claim.
Now, as
[\Gamma,N_{1}]/M\leq[\Gamma,\Gamma]/M
is finite, and by assumption also
N_{1}/[\Gamma,N_{1}]
, we obtain that
N_{1}/M
is finite.
In particular
N_{2}/M
is finite as desired.

Using this we can now prove that:

Claim 2:

V_{|G|/4}/[\langle V_{2},\Phi(g_{i}),\Phi(h_{i})\rangle,V_{|G|/4}]
is finite.

Applying Claim
1
to
\Gamma=\langle V_{2},\Phi(g_{i}),\Phi(h_{i})\rangle,N_{2}=V_{|G|/4}
and
N_{1}=V_{2}
we see that it is enough to prove that

V_{2}/[\langle V_{2},\Phi(g_{i}),\Phi(h_{i})\rangle,V_{2}]\text{ is finite.}

The latter can be seen via an explicit set of generators. Concretely, following (
37
),
V_{2}=\langle w^{2},v^{2},w^{-1}v,-1\rangle
with
w=\left(\begin{array}[]{ll}1&2\\
0&1\end{array}\right)
and
v=\left(\begin{array}[]{ll}1&0\\
2&1\end{array}\right)
. It is readily computed that

(61)

v^{\Phi_{i}(h_{i})}=w,v^{\Phi_{i}(g_{i})}=v^{-1}\text{ and }w^{\Phi_{i}(g_{i})}=w^{-1}.

From this one verifies that the square of each generator is a single commutator. Therefore,
V_{2}/[\langle\Phi_{i}(g_{i}),\Phi_{i}(h_{i})\rangle,V_{2}]
is an elementary abelian
2
-group, finishing the proof of Claim 2.

Thus all together we have proven that when
G
satisfies property
(\star)
,
then
\prod_{i=1}^{q}[H,\mathcal{H}(g_{i},h_{i},Q_{i})]
is of finite index in
\prod_{i=1}^{q}{\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}Ge_{i})
.
But by the normality of
H
in
{\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}G)
we have that
[H,\mathcal{H}(g_{i},h_{i},Q_{i})]\leq H
for each
i
and hence
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}G))\,H
indeed is of finite index in
{\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}G).
As mentioned earlier, by (
59
) this implies that (R1) holds for
G
if it satisfies
(\star)
.

Now consider again a general
G\in\mathcal{G}_{j}\times C_{2}^{n}.
We will now apply (
56
) to
\Gamma={\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}G)
and
N=\langle\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}G),\mathcal{H}(G)_{un}\rangle
. Using (
57
), we see that the first part of the statement follows if
\left({\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}G)/\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}G)).\langle\mathcal{H}(G)_{un}\rangle\right)^{ab}
is finite.

For this recall that each
\mathcal{H}(g_{i},h_{i},Q_{i})
contains the element
1-e_{i}+\begin{pmatrix}1&2^{m}\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}
(for simplicity, we abuse notation by writing matrices in
e_{i}
-part). As
V_{o(g_{i}Q_{i}).|Q_{i}|}
is normal in
{\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}Ge_{i})
, the H-units
\mathcal{H}(g_{i},h_{i},Q_{i})

contain the group
A_{i}:=1-e_{i}+cl_{{\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}Ge_{i})}(\langle\begin{pmatrix}1&2^{m}\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}\rangle).
Notice that
A_{i}\leq\langle\mathcal{H}(G)_{un}\rangle.
Now as abelianisation is right exact and
by (
10.4
) it is enough to prove that
\left(\operatorname{SL}_{1}({\mathbb{Z}}G)/\prod_{i}A_{i}\right)^{ab}
is finite. To prove the latter
it is sufficient to show that
\prod_{i=1}^{q}\left(\operatorname{SL}_{1}({\mathbb{Z}}Ge_{i})/A_{i}\right)^{ab}
is finite. This is directly verified using the presentation in
Proposition

8.2
as also

\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
2^{m}&1\end{pmatrix}=1-e_{i}+h^{-1}e_{i}\begin{pmatrix}1&2^{m}\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}he_{i}\in A_{i}
. This finishes the proof of the torsion-free part of the Theorem.
∎

Next,

Proof of the torsion part of
Theorem

10.16
.

For the torsion statement we consider the short exact sequence (
55
) for
\Gamma=V({\mathbb{Z}}G)
and
N=\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}G))\,cl_{V({\mathbb{Z}}G)}(\langle\operatorname{Bic}(G),G\rangle)
. Hereby the following is crucial:

Claim 3:
For
\Gamma=V({\mathbb{Z}}G)
and
N=\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}G))\,cl_{V({\mathbb{Z}}G)}(\langle\operatorname{Bic}(G),G\rangle)
holds:

(62)

\exp(\Gamma^{ab})=lcm(\exp(\pi(N)),\exp(\Gamma/N)^{ab}).

When
\pi(N)
is finite Claim 3 is clear, as the claim even holds more generally for any short exact sequence of finitely generated abelian groups with finite kernel. In particular, recalling that
\pi(cl_{V({\mathbb{Z}}G)}(\langle\operatorname{Bic}(G),G\rangle))
is finite, we have that Claim 3 holds when
G\notin\mathcal{G}_{7}\times C_{2}^{n}
as for such groups
G
we have that
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}G))
is finite and hence also so is
\pi(N)
.

To handle the family
\mathcal{G}_{7}\times C_{2}^{n}
we need we need to do some more work.
First recall that by
[
30
, Theorem 6]
and
[
25
, beginning of
Section 5]
the only simple components of
{\mathbb{Q}}[G]
are of the form
{\mathbb{Q}},{\mathbb{Q}}(i),\left(\frac{-1,-1}{{\mathbb{Q}}}\right),\left(\frac{-1,-1}{{\mathbb{Q}}(\sqrt{2})}\right)
,
or
\operatorname{M}_{2}({\mathbb{Q}})
. Therefore by
[
30
, Lemmas 2 and 3]
and
[
25
, Lemma 5.3]

G
is a subgroup of

C_{2}^{n_{1}}\times C_{4}^{n_{2}}\times Q_{8}^{n_{3}}\times D_{8}^{n_{4}}\times Q_{16}^{n_{5}}
for some
n_{1},\ldots,n_{5}\in{\mathbb{N}}
with
n_{5}=0

if
K\notin\mathcal{G}_{7}
. Recall that both
Q_{8}
and
Q_{16}
have a unique subgroup of order
2
which moreover is central.
Thus the second part of Claim 4, see below, holds for every subgroup of
C_{2}^{n_{1}}\times C_{4}^{n_{2}}\times Q_{8}^{n_{3}}\times D_{8}^{n_{4}}\times Q_{16}^{n_{5}}
. Also note that
G
has a normal subgroup, say
A
, so that both groups
A
and
G/A
are abelian and also
\exp(G/A)
divides
4
.
A result of Cliff, Sehgal and Weiss (see
[
54
, Theorem 31.1]
) yields that
V_{tf}({\mathbb{Z}}G):=V({\mathbb{Z}}G)\cap(1+\ker(\omega_{A})\ker(\omega_{G}))
is a torsion-free normal subgroup of
V({\mathbb{Z}}G)
and
V({\mathbb{Z}}G)=V_{tf}({\mathbb{Z}}G)\,G\cong V_{tf}({\mathbb{Z}}G)\rtimes G
.

Claim 4:
if
x\in V_{tf}({\mathbb{Z}}G)
is such that
x^{m}\in\mathcal{Z}(V({\mathbb{Z}}G))
for some
m\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{>0}
, then
x\in\mathcal{Z}(V({\mathbb{Z}}G))
. Furthermore,
g^{o(g)/2}\in\mathcal{Z}(G)
for every
g\in G
with
o(g)>2.

Take
e\in\operatorname{PCI}({\mathbb{Q}}G)
. Note that the Cliff-Sehgal-Weiss result also holds for the quotient groups
Ge
of
G
.
Thus also
V({\mathbb{Z}}[Ge])=V_{tf}({\mathbb{Z}}[Ge])\rtimes Ge
has such a decomposition. Furthermore, in view of the explicit description
of
V_{tf}({\mathbb{Z}}G)
, the decompositions are compatible in the following sense: the natural epimorphism
\pi_{e}:G\rightarrow Ge
extends
to an epimorphism
\phi_{e}:{\mathbb{Z}}G\rightarrow{\mathbb{Z}}[Ge]
and its restriction yields a morphism
\phi_{e}:V({\mathbb{Z}}G)\rightarrow V({\mathbb{Z}}[Ge])
. It follows from the description of the basis of the kernel of a relative augmentation map that
\phi_{e}(V_{tf}({\mathbb{Z}}G)){\bf\subseteq}V_{tf}({\mathbb{Z}}[Ge])
and
\phi_{e}(\mathcal{Z}(V({\mathbb{Z}}[G]))\subseteq\mathcal{Z}(V({\mathbb{Z}}[Ge]))
.
This compatibility combined with the assumption yields that

(63)

\phi_{e}(x)^{m}\in V_{tf}({\mathbb{Z}}[Ge])\cap\mathcal{Z}(V({\mathbb{Z}}[Ge])).

Now if
Ge
is abelian or isomorphic to
Q_{8}
or
D_{8}
then
\mathcal{Z}(V({\mathbb{Z}}[Ge]))
is finite. Thus

\phi_{e}(x)^{m}=e
by (
63
) and, since
\phi_{e}(x)
belongs to the torsion-free group

V_{tf}({\mathbb{Z}}[Ge]
, we thus even get
\phi_{e}(x)=e
(in particular it is central). Next consider the case that
Ge\cong Q_{16}
the quaternion group of order
16
. From the description of the unit group of
{\mathbb{Z}}Q_{16}
obtained in
[
32
, Theorem 4]
it follows that
V_{tf}({\mathbb{Z}}[Ge])=V_{tf}({\mathbb{Z}}Q_{16})
is the direct product of an infinite cyclic group, which moreover is
V_{tf}({\mathbb{Z}}Q_{16})\cap\mathcal{Z}\left(V({\mathbb{Z}}[Ge])\right)
, and a non-abelian free group. Thus (
63
) can only happen if
\phi_{e}(x)\in\mathcal{Z}\left(V({\mathbb{Z}}[Ge])\right).

So, we have shown that
\phi_{e}(x)
is central for each
e\in\operatorname{PCI}({\mathbb{Q}}G)
. Therefore
xe
, the projection of
x
in the simple component
{\mathbb{Q}}Ge
, is central for each
e
. Hence,
x
itself is central and the claim follows.

Proof of Claim 3:
by Claim 4, and because
G\subseteq N
, we can choose a transversal
\mathcal{T}
of
N
in
\Gamma
such that

\mathcal{T}\subseteq V_{tf}({\mathbb{Z}}G)
and
the only elements in
\mathcal{T}
with some power central are the central elements. Now, for
x\in\Gamma^{ab}
write
x=t\,y\,z
, with
t\in\pi(\mathcal{T}),z\in\pi(\mathcal{Z}(V({\mathbb{Z}}G)))
and
y\in\pi(\langle\operatorname{Bic}(G),G\rangle).
If
x
is periodic, then so are
t,y,z.
To see this, recall that
\pi(cl_{V({\mathbb{Z}}G)}(\langle\operatorname{Bic}(G),G\rangle))
is finite. Thus for some positive integer
n
we have

t^{n}
is a central unit. However, by the choice of the transversal, this implies that also
t
is periodic. Consequently, the remaining component
z
also needs to be periodic. With this Claim 3 now follows directly.

The central and bicyclic units contribute as predicted by conjecture (E1):

Claim 5:

\exp(\pi\left(\mathcal{Z}(V({\mathbb{Z}}G))\,cl_{V({\mathbb{Z}}G)}(\langle\operatorname{Bic}(G),G\rangle)\right))=\exp(G^{ab}).

First we note that
\exp(G^{ab})\mid\exp(\pi(N))
with
N=\mathcal{Z}(V({\mathbb{Z}}G))\,cl_{V({\mathbb{Z}}G)}(\langle\operatorname{Bic}(G),G\rangle))
. To see this, consider the relative augmentation
\omega_{G^{\prime}}:{\mathbb{Z}}[G]\rightarrow{\mathbb{Z}}[G/G^{\prime}]
. As
{\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}[G/G^{\prime}])
is abelian, we have an induced morphism
\widetilde{\omega_{G^{\prime}}}:V({\mathbb{Z}}G)^{ab}\rightarrow V({\mathbb{Z}}[G/G^{\prime}])
. Since
G^{\prime}\subseteq[V({\mathbb{Z}}G),V({\mathbb{Z}}G)]
and
\widetilde{\omega_{G^{\prime}}}(g[V({\mathbb{Z}}G),V({\mathbb{Z}}G)])=gG^{\prime}
for
g\in G
, we have that any periodic element of
G/G^{\prime}
is a
{\left.\kern-1.2pt\widetilde{\omega_{G^{\prime}}}\right|_{\pi(N)}}
-image of a periodic element of
\pi(N)
. Hence it follows that
\exp(G/G^{\prime})\mid\exp(\pi(N))
.

Thus in order to prove Claim 5, it is enough to show that
o(\pi(\alpha))
divides
\exp(G^{ab})
for every element
\alpha
of
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}G)),\operatorname{Bic}(G)
and
G
. For the latter this trivially is true. Now consider a central unit
\alpha\in\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}G))
. By
[
26
, Proposition 5.5.1]
one has that
\mathcal{Z}(V({\mathbb{Z}}G))\cap[V({\mathbb{Z}}G),V({\mathbb{Z}}G)]\leq\mathcal{Z}(V({\mathbb{Z}}G))\cap\operatorname{SL}_{1}({\mathbb{Z}}G)
is finite. Thus if
o(\pi(\alpha))
is finite, then
\alpha
itself is a periodic unit. Consequently, by a result of Berman and Higman
[
52
, Corollary 7.1.3]
(or see Theorem
5.5
),
\alpha\in G
and in particular
o(\pi(\alpha))\mid\exp(G^{ab})
.
