Next consider
\alpha=1+(1-x)y\widetilde{x}\in\operatorname{Bic}(G)
whose inverse is
\alpha^{-1}=1-(1-x)y\widetilde{x}
. We will prove that

(64)

o(\pi(\alpha))\mid 2.

If
o(x)=2,
then (
64
) was obtained in
[
4
, Proposition 3.1]
. So suppose that
o(x)>2
and thus
o(x)=4
or
8
. However,
o(x)=8
only occurs for the class
\mathcal{G}_{7}
and in that case
x
generates a normal subgroup
[
25
, Lemma 5.7]
. In particular if
o(x)=8,
then
\alpha=1.
In conclusion, we may suppose that
o(x)=4.

It was noticed in the proof of

[
4
, Proposition 3.1]
that
[\alpha^{-1},x^{k}]=1+(1-x)(1-x^{-k})y\widetilde{x}
for any non-negative integer
k
.
Consequently, for
\mathcal{I}
a subset of
\{1,\ldots,o(x)\}
one has that

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\prod\limits_{k\in\mathcal{I}}[\alpha^{-1},x^{k}]&=&1+\sum\limits_{k\in\mathcal{I}}(1-x)(1-x^{-k})y\widetilde{x}\\
&=&1+|\mathcal{I}|(1-x)y\widetilde{x}-(1-x)(\sum\limits_{k\in\mathcal{I}}x^{-k})y\widetilde{x}.\end{array}

Now take
\mathcal{I}=\{o(x)-1,o(x)\}
and using that
\widetilde{x}=(1+x)(1+x^{2})
we see that

(1-x)(\sum\limits_{k\in\mathcal{I}}x^{-k})y\widetilde{x}=(1-x)(1+x)y(1+x)(1+x^{2})=(1-x^{2})(1+x^{2})y(1+x)=0,

where we used that
x^{2}
is central (by Claim 4). Altogether we have proven that
\alpha^{2}=1+2(1-x)y\widetilde{x}\in[V({\mathbb{Z}}G),V({\mathbb{Z}}G)],
yielding (
64
).

The statement (
64
) also holds for the units
1+\widetilde{x}y(1-x)
and follows from an analogue proof. This finishes the proof of Claim 5.

Claim 2 together with (
62
) yields the upper bound for
\exp(V({\mathbb{Z}}G)^{ab})
we were looking for.

Now suppose that
G
satisfies
(\star)
, then we already have proven that the group generated by

\mathcal{Z}(V({\mathbb{Z}}G))
and
cl_{V({\mathbb{Z}}G)}(\langle\operatorname{Bic}(G),G)\rangle
is of finite index in
V({\mathbb{Z}}G)
. More precisely, recall that
\mathcal{H}(g_{i},h_{i},Q_{i})=1-e_{i}+V_{|G|/4}
and also the identification from (
60
). With this we can reformulate Claim 2 saying that

\prod_{i}\mathcal{H}(g_{i},h_{i},Q_{i})/[cl_{V({\mathbb{Z}}G)}(\langle\operatorname{Bic}(G),g_{i},h_{i}\rangle),\mathcal{H}(g_{i},h_{i},Q_{i})]
is finite. To control the exponent of the latter we will pass over to an overgroup:

|\frac{\mathcal{H}(g_{i},h_{i},Q_{i})}{[cl_{V({\mathbb{Z}}G)}(\langle\operatorname{Bic}(G),g_{i},h_{i}\rangle),\mathcal{H}(g_{i},h_{i},Q_{i})]}|\text{ divides }|\frac{\langle V_{2},\Phi(g_{i}),\Phi(h_{i})\rangle}{[\langle V_{2},\Phi(g_{i}),\Phi(h_{i})\rangle,V_{|G|/4}]}|.

Next note that the proof of Claim 2 entails that
(\langle V_{2},\Phi(g_{i}),\Phi(h_{i})\rangle)^{ab}
is a finite elementary abelian
2
-group.
Therefore using the explicit bound from
44
44

44

More precisely we replace
[\Gamma:N_{1}]n
by the multiple

[\Gamma:N_{1}].[N_{1}:N_{2}]=[\Gamma:N_{2}]
.
Claim 1 in the setting of Claim 2 yields that

\exp\left(\langle V_{2},\Phi(g_{i}),\Phi(h_{i})\rangle/[\langle V_{2},\Phi(g_{i}),\Phi(h_{i})\rangle,V_{|G|/4}]\right)\text{ divides }2\,([\langle V_{2},\Phi(g_{i}),\Phi(h_{i})\rangle:V_{|G|/4}])^{[t/2]+1}

where
t
is the product of all
p^{t_{p}}
with
t_{p}
the maximum exponent of
p
dividing
[\langle V_{2},\Phi(g_{i}),\Phi(h_{i})\rangle:V_{|G|/4}].
Now, recalling that
V_{2^{i}}\cong U_{i}
by (
38
),
Proposition

8.3
says that
[V_{2}:V_{|G|/4}]
is a power of two. Furthermore,
Proposition

8.2
yields that
[\langle V_{2},\Phi(g_{i}),\Phi(h_{i})\rangle:V_{2}]
is a
2
-power. Summarized we have proven that

\exp\left(\prod_{i}\frac{\mathcal{H}(g_{i},h_{i},Q_{i})}{[cl_{V({\mathbb{Z}}G)}(\langle\operatorname{Bic}(G),g_{i},h_{i}\rangle),\mathcal{H}(g_{i},h_{i},Q_{i})]}\right)\text{ is a power of }2.

Combining this with the fact that
[{\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}Ge_{i}):V_{|G|/4}]
is
2
-power and

\exp\left(\frac{V({\mathbb{Z}}G)}{\mathcal{Z}(V({\mathbb{Z}}G))\,cl_{{\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}G)}(\langle\operatorname{Bic}(G),\pm G\rangle)}\right)\text{ divides }\exp\left(\prod_{i}\frac{{\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}Ge_{i})}{[cl_{V({\mathbb{Z}}G)}(\langle\operatorname{Bic}(G),g_{i},h_{i}\rangle),\mathcal{H}(g_{i},h_{i},Q_{i})]}\right)

we obtain that
2
is the only prime divisor of the left hand
side quotient group. Therefore, as
\exp(G^{ab})\mid\exp(G)=4
and thanks to the value obtained for
\exp V({\mathbb{Z}}G)^{ab}
conjecture (P) holds, finishing the proof.
∎

The statement that
cl_{{\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}G)}(\langle\operatorname{Bic}(G),\pm G\rangle)
is of finite index when
G
satisfies
(\star)
does not mention
H
-units. However we would like to emphasize that the proof needed them and hence the result is truly a combined use of bicyclic and
H
-units. In upcoming work by the first author a systematic study of the abelianisation of
H
-units and their role on the rank of
{\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{Z}}G)^{ab}
will be done.
